Every table already existed — `0006` and `0007` built them — so ingestion needed **no migration at all**, and the Shopify adapter needed none either: two slices in a row, where every previous one added at least one.

   ~~**What a Shopify adapter now costs.**~~ **It cost what this paragraph predicted, and the prediction is worth keeping because it was right.** `lib/integrations/commerce/types.ts` was written while there was one adapter precisely so the second would be a new file rather than a branch through the service — and it was. `lib/services/ingestion.ts` gained **one line** (a `parseSourceConfig` branch) and `index.ts` gained one registry entry; the pipeline, the review screen, change detection and the apply path were untouched. The interface held exactly as `0005`'s header promised.

   **What the paragraph did *not* predict, and what actually took the work: the admin UI.** There was no kind selector — `SourcesList.tsx` hard-coded `kind: "xml_feed"` and the create action's schema demanded `url` and `itemPath` from every kind — so a Shopify source could not be created at all, and the edit form would have written an XML config over it. Six of the eleven changed files are UI. **The lesson for the next adapter: the adapter is the cheap half.** An interface can be provider-agnostic while every screen above it is still shaped like the first provider, and nothing fails until someone tries to use the second one.

   **The two things deliberately left out of ingestion, so nobody assumes them — both still true after Shopify.** **Images are not ingested** — that needs binaries downloaded into Storage, catalogued in `media_assets` and reconciled against `product_media`, which fails in ways a text mapping never does; a source's image URLs are visible in the raw payload on the review screen, so nothing is lost, only deferred. Shopify makes this gap **wider**, not narrower: a Shopify product's images are the thing a merchant most expects to arrive. And **runs are manual only** — `import_jobs.trigger` accepts `'scheduled'` and `'webhook'`, and `product_sources.sync_schedule` is stored and read by nothing. Wiring a schedule is a second consumer of the jobs route from the scheduled-publish phase, and it should not be built without reading that phase's warning that `*/5` is nothing like every five minutes.

   ~~**The theme editor is the risky one**: it turns the design tokens of a pixel-verified site into editable data, which makes the 16 baselines a constraint on every change rather than a safety net.~~ **It was, and the risk was managed rather than avoided — see the decisions log.** The technique that worked is worth reusing: introduce the variable holding today's literal value and prove *that* pixel-neutral, then make the token editable and seed it with the same value. Neither step moves a pixel, so the baselines stayed a safety net throughout.
2. ~~**Phase 7c**~~ — **done and verified.** The public rewire is complete: 7a (homepage), 7b (store), and now `/[category]` and `/article/[slug]`. The Supabase `BindingSource` landed with it at `lib/services/bindingSources.ts`, with a category page's lead and grid as its first real consumer. The deep-compare, the build and all 16 visual baselines pass against the live project. The `image`/`video` URL-vs-asset-id decision was *not* revisited: block fields still store URLs, while `featured_asset_id`, `hero_asset_id` and `product_media` store ids. That split is now three phases old and still deliberate.
3. ~~**`pattern` in the builder**~~ — **done.** `/admin/patterns` has a list, the shared builder, history and rollback, and the library rail can insert a pattern into a page as a copy. The prediction held exactly: the route is the `pages` route with three values changed (`"pattern"`, `BLOCK_TREE_KEY.pattern`, the permissions), because `lib/db/repositories/documents.ts` aliases `name`→`title` and `key`→`slug`. Preview needed nothing either — `/preview/[token]` pulls the tree out with `BLOCK_TREE_KEY[type]`. **What it did cost was the same lesson the Shopify adapter recorded: the screens, not the abstraction.** The builder accepted a pattern unchanged; `PublishBar` still hard-coded `/admin/pages/${doc.id}/history`, and the insertion half needed a new store operation and a rail group.

   ⚠️ ~~**Only detachable patterns exist, and that is enforced rather than assumed**~~ — true when written, and **fixed since**: the public paths expand `_ref`s, so `createPatternAction` accepts `syncMode` from the client. The enforcement-rather-than-assumption stance is the part worth keeping: the option was withheld in code, not merely undocumented, which is why nobody shipped a page that silently lost a section in the three phases it took to close.

   ~~**`template` is still not in the builder**~~ — **it is now**, and the paragraph that stood here predicted the shape correctly: `BLOCK_TREE_KEY.template` is still `null`, and what closed the gap was teaching `treeKey` to be a path plus an area switcher above the canvas. Templates are **back in the admin nav**, which they earned the same way patterns did — by acquiring a route in the same commit as the link. `templates` was still an **empty table** when that work landed, so nothing had to be migrated into the new shape.

4. **~~Drag-from-library, and then a layout engine~~ — all three slices are built.** They were scoped in a session that built none of them, and **`/BUILDER_PLAN.md` has now been deleted**, which was the retirement condition written into its own header: delete each slice as it lands, delete the file when all three have. Both halves were honoured by the sessions that did the work rather than deferred — the same discipline this file records failing at five times over a branch name. What each slice cost and taught is in the decisions log; the code wins wherever a summary here disagrees with it.

   ~~**Slice 1 — drag a section from the library onto the canvas.**~~ **Done** — see the decisions log for what it cost and what it taught. The scoping's three findings all held: `store.insert` already took an index and needed no change; `DndContext` was inside `Canvas.tsx` *and* inside the empty-tree ternary and had to be hoisted to `BuilderLayout`; explicit `gap:N` droppables beat inferring an index from a hovered block. Verified with the four gates, `npm run build`, and **16/16 baselines unmoved**.

   ~~**Slice 2 — prove the drag in a real browser.**~~ **Done, and green on its first CI run** — `tests/e2e/builder.spec.ts` gained a second serial group with its own scratch page, and **58/58 E2E passed with nothing skipped**, which is the number that matters: the specs `test.skip` themselves when `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL`/`E2E_ADMIN_PASSWORD` are absent, so a green job with three silently skipped specs would look identical to a green job that ran them. ⚠️ **The E2E suite still cannot run in a session container** — it needs those credentials and a service-role key, and no container has them — so **CI remains the only place this slice is ever exercised.** A future change to the drag will not fail locally no matter how wrong it is.

   ~~**Slice 3 — the layout engine (arbitrary nesting).**~~ **Built.** The scoping was right about where the work was: `children` was supported-but-dead infrastructure — in the type, in `0003`'s schema comment, and recursed into by `traverse.ts`, so `validate.ts`, `media.ts`, `diff.ts` and `binding.ts` inherited nesting for free through `walkBlocks` — and everything above the data layer had to be built. What landed:
   - **`BlockSlot`** on the manifest, beside `fields` rather than in it. Children live in `BlockNode.children`, the home every traversal already descends into; a field holding blocks would route them around publish validation, media extraction, diffing and binding at once. **Declaring a slot is what makes children legal** — `validateBlock` refuses them on a manifest without one, so the other twenty-two blocks are provably leaves.
   - **`columns`**, the first container and the one component permitted its own responsive behaviour. It offers **the site's own breakpoints (680/820/1024)** rather than new numbers: inventing was licensed by the override, and was not necessary.
   - **`components/admin/builder/tree.ts`** — pure, path-aware `locate`/`insertAt`/`insertAfter`/`removeByKey`/`moveByKey`/`moveInto`, replacing the root-array `reorderByKey` (now deleted). All non-mutating, all returning the original reference when nothing changed, because `commit` reads that as a no-op and undo's cheapness depends on structural sharing.
   - `SectionRenderer` recursing **only for a manifest with a slot**, so a leaf is called exactly as before — which is what keeps the baselines meaningful across the change.
   - The canvas rendering nested `SortableContext`s, gap ids carrying their container (`gap:<parentKey>:<index>`), and an empty container advertising a droppable registered for *any* drag.

   **Three traps, each of which was silent.** ⚠️ **The canvas's `[&_button]:pointer-events-none` is a descendant selector** — left on a container it disables every nested block's own toolbar, and a `pointer-events-auto` utility cannot win the specificity; it is applied to leaves only now. ⚠️ **Clicking a nested block selected its container**, because every frame carries the same `onMouseDown` and the ancestor's ran last; fixed with `stopPropagation`. ⚠️ **`findDraft` is deliberately a second recursion** rather than a reuse of `findBlock`: writing to what `findBlock` returns inside a `produce` mutates a copy immer never finalises, so the edit vanishes with no error.

   **Deliberately not done: the store enforces no slot policy.** `insert` will put a block into any container it is given, and `validateTree` reports the breach. That matches how the rest of the store behaves — it does not refuse an invalid field value either — and keeps the manifests out of it. The UI only ever offers slots that exist.

   **Still not in scope, and untouched by the above: templates' named areas.** `BLOCK_TREE_KEY.template` is `null` because templates hold `{ areas: Record<string, BlockTree> }` and `BuilderDocument.treeKey` is a single string. `blockTreesOf` in `lib/services/documents.ts` already handles areas for validation and revisions. **Nesting and areas are different problems** — conflating them doubles the slice.

Also outstanding from Phase 1: ~~a repository layer over `lib/db`~~ — **done, and this line was stale for several phases**: `lib/db/repositories/` holds sixteen modules (`documents`, `pages`, `articles`, `products`, `media`, `navigation`, `theme`, `ingestion`, `revisions`, `templates`, `patterns`, `taxonomy`, `scheduledJobs`, `previewSessions`, `errors`). ~~What remains is **the RLS integration suite**~~ — **done and merged: `tests/integration/rls.test.ts`, 26 assertions, green on every CI run it has had** (PR #37, `02fd7fb`; it shipped 25 and ended at 26 when the `is_system` characterisation test was inverted and gained a companion). **`0019` took it to 32**, the six new ones asserting that a child row follows its parent's publish state — unrun in the session that wrote them, for the same Docker reason, and awaiting their first CI run. It could not be executed in the session that wrote it — a container cannot start a stack, Docker's daemon is unreachable — so it was written against a live probe of the deployed project and verified only in CI; **the integration suite went 69 → 94 tests and 8 → 9 files**, which is the number that matters, because a suite that failed to collect would have reported a green job just the same. See the decisions-log entry below.

</details>

### How to run & verify

All commands from `design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/starter/`.

- **Dev:** `npm install && npm run dev` → http://localhost:3000
- **The four gates (run before every commit):**
  `npm run format:check && npm run lint && npm run typecheck && npm test`
- **Build:** `npm run build` — required before anything touching routing; Next enforces rules that only appear at build time.
- **E2E:** `npm run test:e2e`. Signed-in specs skip unless `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL` / `E2E_ADMIN_PASSWORD` are exported.
- **Accessibility:** `npm run test:a11y` — axe-core over every public route in both themes, plus the overlays' keyboard behaviour.
- **Performance budget:** `npm run test:perf` — image bytes per route at a mobile viewport, plus a check that no `<img>` is served more than 2.5× the width it is painted at.
  ⚠️ **These two are the Playwright suites that run in a session container**: neither needs credentials, only a built and seeded site. `test:e2e` skips itself silently without `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL`. (This line used to say "the one suite"; `test:perf` joined it.)
- **Integration:** `npx supabase start && npm run test:integration`.
- **Seed / admin:** `npx tsx scripts/seed.ts`, `npx tsx scripts/create-admin.ts` (both idempotent).
- **Screenshot-diff loop** (Track A definition of done): drive the running app with the preinstalled Playwright, capture at 1440 / 909 / 375 + dark, diff against `design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/handoff/screenshots/*.png`.
- **Proving a refactor is render-safe:** build before and after, then diff the prerendered HTML in `.next/server/app/*.html` after stripping build IDs and chunk hashes. This is how the Prettier pass was verified to leave all 12 pages byte-identical.

### A new session's first actions

1. Read this handoff.
2. Read `design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/CLAUDE.md` — the design baseline, binding every session.
3. Check live state: `node design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/starter/scripts/status.mjs`.
4. For front-end work: the relevant `03_PAGES_AND_COMPONENTS.md` section + its `design_files/MG *.dc.html` prototype + matching screenshot.
5. Cut a fresh branch from `main`, run the four gates, and **update this file** before finishing. It no longer carries a branch name to update — the hook prints that — so what needs writing is the decisions log and the honest account of what was and was not verified.
6. If you need to exercise a **jobs route** (`/api/jobs/publish-scheduled`, `/api/jobs/run-imports`), add `JOBS_SECRET` to `.env.local` too — any string. Without it both routes answer `503` and refuse every request, which is the designed fail-closed behaviour and reads as a broken route. ⚠️ A *run* additionally needs `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`, which no container has: with the secret right and the key absent, the route answers `503` naming the variable, and that is the furthest a container can get.
7. If you need `npm run build`, `npm run test:visual`, **`npm run test:a11y` or `npm run test:perf`**, write a gitignored `starter/.env.local` first — **with real values, not placeholders.** All four start a web server against a built site, and `middleware.ts` builds a Supabase client on every request, so without credentials the server never becomes ready and Playwright dies on a 120s timeout whose message names neither. (This step used to list only `build` and `test:visual`; the a11y and perf suites need exactly the same three variables.) Three variables: `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL` and `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` (both readable from the Supabase MCP), and `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` (any absolute origin; `http://localhost:3000` is fine).

   ⚠️ **`get_publishable_keys` returns two keys and the first one is dead.** The MCP hands back a legacy `anon` JWT (`eyJ…`) carrying **`"disabled": true`**, and beside it the live **`sb_publishable_…`** key. Take the publishable one. Picking the JWT because it looks like the anon key everyone remembers gives a value that is present, well-formed and rejected, which `lib/db/env.ts` cannot flag because there is nothing malformed about it. ⚠️ **This step said "placeholder Supabase values" until 2026-08-09 and that was a trap** — placeholders have failed since Phase 7a, because the site layout reads the database on every public route. Missing the third variable fails the build with `Failed to collect page data for /_not-found`, which names neither the variable nor the cause.

> ⚠️ **The Supabase project is shared with a live human, and a probe must not assume otherwise.** Mid-session this time, `templates` held a row this session did not create — a `page`-kind draft named "Testing", made in the live admin while work was in progress. It was found before anything was touched and left alone. **Read the table before writing to it**; "I left it empty" is a fact about the past. Probe rows get a `zz-` prefixed key so the two can never be confused, and every probe this session was deleted with its counts confirmed back at baseline.

### ⚠️ A leftover dev server makes every Playwright suite test the wrong build

`playwright.config.ts` sets `reuseExistingServer: !process.env.CI`. So **any
`npm run start` you left running on port 3000 is what the visual, a11y and perf
suites connect to** — they will not start their own, and nothing says so. If that
server has been running since an earlier build, all three suites silently audit
stale output.

It gets worse rather than better with time: `rm -rf .next && npm run build`
between runs replaces the build directory **underneath** the running server, and
Next then 500s on routes it has not already cached. The symptom is a suite
failing on a route you did not touch, with `#__next_error__` and complaints about
a missing `<title>` and `lang` — which reads as a real accessibility regression
and is not one.

**This happened in this session and cost a wrong conclusion**: `/checkout` failed
two a11y assertions, and `curl` against a fresh server returned the page happily
at 200. The suites were connected to a server started several builds earlier.

**Before running any Playwright suite, check the port is free**, and kill what is
on it:

```bash
fuser -n tcp 3000            # anything listening?
fuser -k -n tcp 3000         # take it out
```

⚠️ **This is a local-only trap** — CI sets `CI`, so `reuseExistingServer` is
false there and every job starts its own server. Which is exactly why it survives:
it cannot be reproduced in the one place that would catch it.

### Proving a structural refactor is render-safe (updated)

> ⚠️ **This recipe is written for a *code* change, and it silently lies about a *data* one.** `npm run build` reuses prerendered output from `.next/cache` when the code has not changed — it has no way to know a row moved. The archive-templates slice spent an hour chasing a bug that was not there: a published template, a rebuild, and the frame missing from **four of five** category pages while the fifth had it. `rm -rf .next` between the two builds whenever the thing you changed is a row. CI and Railway both build from a cold checkout, so this is a local-verification trap only — which is exactly the kind that survives.


`design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/starter` has no `node_modules` in a fresh container — `npm install` first. Then, **before** touching anything: `npm run build`, archive `.next/server/app/`, and run `npm run test:visual` to write the public screenshot baselines. Afterwards, rebuild and compare.

Compare the rendered `<body>` with `<script>` bodies emptied and runs of them collapsed, the build ID stripped (it appears twice — an HTML comment after the doctype *and* inside the flight payload), and chunk hashes folded (JS carries a `-<hash>` suffix, CSS is the bare hash). A raw byte diff of the files will **not** match across a layout change, and that is expected rather than a regression. `tests/visual/public.spec.ts` is the second net and the stronger one for anything CSS-shaped.

## Deviations & decisions log
_Record anything you could not reproduce exactly, ambiguities, or choices made (e.g. which article templates were built, SHOP vs STORE route naming). One line each._

### Four hardening slices, three of which reversed something this file had written down

Grouped because they share one shape: **each was deferred by a paragraph that was right about the danger and wrong about a number.** None of the reversals came from new information — the facts were all checkable when the original decision was taken, and nobody checked them.

**1. The newsletter rate limit.** Known issues said doing it properly "needs shared state this deployment does not have". The deployment has Postgres. What was true in that sentence is the half everyone remembers — an in-process counter is worthless when Railway can run more than one container — and it is what made the fix obvious once the wrong conclusion was dropped: a fixed-window counter, one row per key, one `on conflict do update`, which is also what makes it correct under concurrency.

⚠️ **The two-bucket design is the part worth carrying forward.** The per-caller bucket keys on `x-forwarded-for`, and that header is attacker-supplied. `app/auth/_lib/publicUrl.ts` faces the same problem for `x-forwarded-host` and solves it by checking the header against a host we already know is ours — **there is no equivalent for an IP**, because any address is a plausible one. So the per-caller limit is a courtesy to honest visitors and the **global** bucket is what actually bounds the table. A limiter with one bucket keyed on a forgeable header is a limiter that reads as working and stops nobody.

Two smaller decisions inside it. The limit is spent **before** the address is validated — validating first makes a malformed address a free request, the cheapest thing an abusive caller can send — which is why the per-caller limit is 10 rather than 5: a visitor correcting a typo pays for it. And the counter **fails open** at both layers, deliberately: this guards a sign-up form, and a broken counter refusing every visitor is worse than briefly not limiting. Something costlier would want the opposite, and would need this decision taken again.

**2. The current-password gate.** Known issues asked for GoTrue's `reauthenticate()` nonce, "applied only when the session did not arrive from a recovery link". **The second clause was the design and the first was never required for it.** The gate needs to distinguish a recovery session from any other; it does not need a second email to do that. `/auth/callback` is the only route that exchanges a one-time code, and a code only reaches a browser through a link sent to the account's own inbox — so a successful exchange there is already stronger evidence than a password, and it sets a short-lived `httpOnly` marker. Everything else is asked.

The tension is worth restating because it is what stalled this for phases: `/admin/password` is *both* the recovery landing and the change-password screen, and an unconditional check does not harden recovery, it removes it. ⚠️ **The check runs on `createPublicClient()`, not the caller's own client** — `signInWithPassword` on the session client would re-issue the session and rotate its cookies underneath a request that is about to change the password anyway.

⚠️ **And the marker's `secure` flag keys on the site's scheme, not on `NODE_ENV`** — the obvious version was written first and is wrong for a reason this file has already recorded once. A `secure` cookie sent over http is **discarded by the browser with no error**, and `next start` sets `NODE_ENV=production` on a laptop exactly as it does on Railway, which is the same root as the `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` build failure. So a local production build over http would have set a flag the browser then honours by dropping the cookie — and the symptom would not read as a cookie problem, it would read as *"recovery is asking me for the password I do not have"*. `secure` follows `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL`'s protocol, which is what the flag actually means, and falls back to `true` if the origin cannot be read: a dropped cookie is recoverable by asking for the password, a cookie sent in the clear is not recoverable at all.

**3. The Shopify 429.** The old comment said backoff "belongs with the queued runs" and that a silent retry "would turn one visibly failed run into a four-minute one". Shopify refills its bucket at two requests a second and answers `Retry-After: 2.0`. **Four minutes was never on offer** — the estimate was invented and then used as a reason. Against it: a 429 on page seven of twelve discarded six successful pages, and the operator's only remedy was to re-run at the same page size into the same limit.

The bounds are what make honouring the header safe, and `totalDelayBudgetMs` is the one that matters: without a ceiling on the **sum** of the waits, "honour `Retry-After`" hands the provider control of how long a request is held open. ⚠️ And `retryAfterMs` returns null rather than `NaN` for anything unreadable — `setTimeout(fn, NaN)` fires on the next tick, so a malformed header would not slow the retry, it would turn the backoff into a hot loop against a server already refusing.

**4. The media search index, and `products.status`.** Two latent traps, both found by reading the schema against the code rather than by anything failing. `0002` built `media_assets_search_idx` over a `to_tsvector(...)` *expression*; PostgREST can only apply `fts` to a **column**, so the index has never been readable and the library has scanned four columns with a leading-wildcard `ilike` for four phases — paying write cost on an index nothing has ever used. A stored generated column fixes both halves.

⚠️ **But swapping `ilike` for full-text would have been a downgrade, and this was measured rather than reasoned about.** A filename becomes **one token**: `to_tsvector('english','hero-cover.jpg')` is `'hero-cover.jpg':1`, so `hero:*` finds it and **`cover:*` finds nothing**, where the substring match found it either way. Prefix matching recovers the front of a filename and never the middle. So full-text runs first and uses the index, and the substring query runs only when it matches **zero** — tested on the exact count, not the returned page, because page two of a match legitimately returns no rows and re-running there would swap result sets underneath the pagination.

`products.status` allowed `'scheduled'` with no `scheduled_for` column, no `SCHEDULABLE_TYPES` entry and nothing in `run_due_publishes` that could see it. Unreachable from the admin today, which is the argument for removing it rather than leaving it: **a vocabulary that promises a behaviour nothing implements is a trap with a delay on it**, and the next person to add a status dropdown pays for it.

⚠️ **One process note, recorded because it nearly shipped.** `0026`'s first draft indexed a `description` column on `media_assets`. There is no such column — the four are `file_name`, `title`, `alt_text` and `caption` — and it was caught by querying `information_schema` before applying, not by review. The column list was in `searchFilter`, eight lines from where the migration was being written. **Read the column list, not the mental model of it.**

### Auditing the launch-gate paragraph, and two defects it was sitting on

The newsletter entry below records that a plausible line in a status document forecloses investigation. So the rest of that paragraph was audited the same way — each clause checked against the code rather than read. **Two of the three were understated, and one sentence was outright false.**

**Checkout came out well, and the detail is worth keeping because it is the counter-example.** It says "Demo checkout — no real card is charged" above the payment fields *and* again on the confirmation, the card number is never transmitted or stored, and the card inputs deliberately carry no `autoComplete` while the address fields do. Somebody thought about this. The one false line — "A confirmation is on its way to {email}", when nothing sends email — is fixed here; the demo disclaimer beside it did not make the sentence true. **The residual is a launch gate rather than a bug: a live public page asks for a real card number**, and that input should not exist by the time real money can be taken.

⚠️ **Membership is a client-side flag, which is a sharper fact than "a flag rather than a subscription".** `cart.setMember(true)` writes `localStorage.mg-member` and makes **no server call anywhere** — grepped, not assumed. The 15% discount is therefore self-service: any visitor can grant it from devtools. Impact today is nil because checkout charges nothing, but it is a hard constraint on the Stripe slice, and the kind that is expensive to discover late. **The discount must be recomputed server-side; the client flag is a hint, never an authorisation.**

⚠️ **The live homepage hot-links a commercial film trailer from someone else's CDN.** "Every image and video is a placeholder with no rights cleared" is true and does not convey that. `pages.published_data` on the live project carries `media.gamereactor.dk` — an F1 / Apple TV official trailer — plus three `upload.wikimedia.org` clips. Three problems in one: a rights exposure, bandwidth taken from a third party on a URL that can break or be swapped without notice, and a third-party request on every homepage view. **`lib/media.ts` says all of this plainly at the source.** The handoff paragraph did not, and the handoff paragraph is what gets read — which is the same failure as the newsletter line, one level less severe.

**The transferable part: a summary is not a substitute for the thing it summarises, and it decays in one direction.** Every one of these understatements made the situation sound *better* than it was. Nobody writes a status line that overstates a problem.

### `0025`, and a delete-then-insert that had been showing up as flakiness

Two defects, unrelated except in when they were found.

**`site_settings` was `for select using (true)`** — every row, to `anon`, since `0001`. Empty, and read by nothing anywhere in `app/`, `components/` or `lib/`, both checked. That is precisely why it was worth closing now: nothing to migrate, no screen to break, no decision to negotiate. **A table named "settings" is the kind that acquires an operational value nobody meant to publish** — an API base URL, a flag naming an unshipped product — and the policy was written before anyone knew what would go in it. Reads are `is_staff()` now. If a genuinely public setting arrives later, the right move is a narrow policy naming those keys, not restoring `using (true)`; that is a smaller decision with a real key in front of you than the blanket one taken in the abstract.

**`replaceMappings` was a DELETE followed by an INSERT, two PostgREST round trips with no transaction.** Between them the source had zero mappings; if the second never arrived — closed tab, dropped connection, a 500 — it stayed that way, and **no error reached the editor**, because nothing failed on their side. A source that mapped ten fields silently mapped none. It had been announcing itself as E2E flakiness, which is how a non-atomic write usually does: intermittently, and looking like a test problem.

Both statements now live in one `plpgsql` function. ⚠️ **`security invoker`, not `security definer`, and that is the whole design** — a definer function runs as the owner and bypasses the caller's RLS, which would quietly break this repo's oldest standing rule that admin writes go through the editor's own session. As an invoker function the `feed_field_mappings` policies still decide, so `integration.write` is required exactly as before; all that changed is that the two statements succeed or fail together.

**Proven by making it fail, which is the only assertion that distinguishes this from the old code.** On the live project, inside a rolled-back transaction: a valid replace swaps three mappings; a payload with a null `target_field` raises **23502 and rolls back**, leaving the previous **three intact** — where the old path would have left zero; an empty array clears them, because `[]` is a legitimate payload meaning "no mappings" rather than an error; and `anon` sees no `site_settings` rows. The regression test asserts the count after a *failed* replace, so losing atomicity later reads as 0 and fails.

### The newsletter captured nothing, and the backlog line is why nobody noticed

`PROGRESS.md` carried "the newsletter captures to Supabase and reaches no ESP" for several phases. Checked rather than trusted, on a question about the ESP: **there was no table, no route handler, no repository and no service.** `Newsletter.tsx` read the address into React state and discarded it on submit; `CtaBand.tsx`'s input had no `value` and no `onChange`, so the address was never read at all. Both then rendered a confirmation — "Thanks — you're on the list." and "SUBSCRIBED ✓" — on **seven live pages**: the homepage, all five category pages and `/membership`.

⚠️ **The wrong backlog line is the finding, not the missing code.** "Captures to Supabase, reaches no ESP" reads as a known, benign, half-finished integration — an item you skip past — rather than as a false statement shown to every visitor. **A plausible lie in a status document is worse than a gap**, because a gap invites investigation and a plausible lie forecloses it. It survived several phases for exactly that reason. When writing one of these, name what you checked.

**What shipped is the capture layer only, and the split is deliberate.** Consent and double opt-in are a decision (UK GDPR/PECR wants a recorded lawful basis, wording beside the button, a privacy notice) and the ESP adapter is a slice after that. But neither is a reason to keep lying to visitors in the meantime, so rows land as `pending`, nothing writes `confirmed`, and the copy says "we've got your address" rather than claiming a subscription nothing has confirmed.

**`0024` is the first table an anonymous visitor may write to**, which inverts every other table here and makes the grants rather than the policies the interesting half. `anon` holds INSERT on `(id, email, source)` and **nothing else** — no SELECT at the grant level, because `0020` already recorded that a policy cannot take back a privilege a role was granted, and a subscriber list `anon` can read is a subscriber list that has leaked. Probed five ways inside a rolled-back transaction: anon inserts; anon is refused SELECT (42501); anon is refused a forged `status='confirmed'` (42501, by the column grant rather than by validation); a signed-in account holding **no roles** sees nothing; staff read it.

⚠️ **Three client-call shapes failed before one worked, and each failed for the same reason.**

1. `.upsert(...).select("id")`, to report whether a row was written — `.select()` asks PostgREST for `return=representation`, which needs SELECT. **42501.**
2. `.upsert(...)` with no select — PostgREST's upsert path needs SELECT on the table regardless of what it returns. **42501 again.**
3. A plain `.insert()`, treating the unique violation as success. **Works.**

**The general trap: on a table where `anon` may write but not read, every convenience that reads something back is closed to you.** `.select()`, `.upsert()`, `.single()`, returning counts — the idiomatic client call is the one that does not work. And none of this was catchable by the test suites: a unit test mocks the client, and the integration suite runs as a role that *can* read. **It took starting the built server and POSTing to it.**

**The fix made the privacy property stronger rather than weaker.** `alreadySubscribed` came out of the response type entirely, so the endpoint cannot distinguish a new address from a known one — "we do not disclose whether you were already subscribed" stops being a promise the code has to keep and becomes a fact about what it can observe. Same reasoning as `/forgot-password` not confirming whether an account exists.

⚠️ **And a fourth failure, in the schema.** The first `0024` enforced case-insensitive uniqueness with `create unique index on (lower(email))` — the obvious construction, and wrong here: a **functional** index cannot satisfy `ON CONFLICT (email)`, which answers **42P10**. Replaced with `check (email = lower(email))` plus a plain `unique (email)`: no other casing can exist, so ordinary uniqueness *is* case-insensitive, and there is a conflict target a client can name. Normalisation became a property of the data rather than a habit of one caller — asserted by an integration test that inserts mixed case and expects **23514**.

**Two side-findings worth keeping.** `npm run db:types` **destroys `database.types.ts` when it fails**, because `>` truncates before the command runs — it needs `SUPABASE_ACCESS_TOKEN`, which no session container has, so it wiped the file on the first attempt. It writes to a temp file and moves it now. And the generated types **cannot express a column-level grant**: TypeScript believes `status` is insertable by anyone, and only the database knows better — which is precisely why that gate belongs in the schema and not in a Zod schema a caller could bypass.

**Verification honesty.** Ran here, green: the four gates (**1323 unit tests**), cold `npm run build`, **16/16 visual**, **27/27 a11y**, **14/14 perf**, and the route exercised end to end against the live project on a built server — 201 for a new address, 201 and no second row for a repeat, mixed case stored lowercased with `+tag` preserved, an unknown source falling back to `unknown`, 400 for a bad address and for malformed JSON, 405 for GET, and byte-identical bodies for a known and an unknown address. Every probe row deleted afterwards and the count confirmed back at 0. The `rls.test.ts` additions **have not run here** (no Docker) and await CI.

⚠️ **A correction about that run's provenance, kept rather than quietly re-stated.** Those three browser-suite figures were first collected while a server left over from an earlier probe still held port 3000, and `reuseExistingServer` meant the suites connected to *it* rather than starting their own — so they audited a build several steps old. The numbers were re-collected against a genuinely fresh server and are the same (16 / 27 / 14), so no conclusion changed. But **they were not evidence at the moment they were first quoted**, and the trap that made that possible is now written up under "A leftover dev server makes every Playwright suite test the wrong build".

### Contrast reaches zero, and the bug that had been flattering the audit

The remaining brand decisions were taken and implemented. The headline is that **every WCAG A/AA rule now passes on all 11 public routes in both themes, contrast included, with no exceptions list and no disabled rules** — but the number that teaches something is the one in the middle.

**The path: 204 → 175 → 34 → 6 → 110 → 0.**

⚠️ **The 110 is not a regression, it is a measurement finally taken against the real site.** `fg`, `bd` and `bg` were bare `var()` in the Tailwind palette, so Tailwind dropped every alpha-modified utility for them: **~413 `text-mg-fg/NN` classes across 129 files compiled to no CSS at all**, and had since Track A. Muted text therefore painted at **full strength** — and full-strength text passes contrast comfortably. So the a11y suite had spent three phases auditing a site that was not rendering its own design, and reporting a flattering number. Repairing the utilities took the count **6 → 110** in one commit.

**The general lesson: a bug can flatter an audit.** Every gate was green about text that was the wrong colour, because the wrong colour happened to be the *safe* colour. Nothing in the suite could see it, because the suite measures rendered pixels and the pixels were consistent with a design nobody had specified.

**The repair and the re-tune are one change, not two.** `alphaUtilities.test.ts` carried precise inversion instructions — twin, `CHANNEL_TWIN`, palette entry, re-record baselines — and following them exactly still left the site failing AA in 110 places. The floor is arithmetic rather than taste: `--mg-fg` needs alpha **0.59** to clear 4.5:1 on `#f4f4f4` (light is the binding ground; dark only needs 0.47), so **177 call sites below `/60` were raised to it**. That flattens the bottom of the opacity ramp — `/25` through `/58` all become `/60` — which is a real loss of hierarchy and the honest price of the design's lightest steps never having been legible.

**`surface` was a latent fourth**, converted with the others: zero alpha usages today, one keystroke from the same silent bug.

⚠️ **`muted` and `faint` stay bare `var()` deliberately and permanently.** A channel twin can only be derived from a hex and both are `rgba()` in dark — "flat greys in light, translucent paper in dark" — so converting them would force both to hex and lose that. Their safety is that nothing uses them with an alpha, which is now **asserted** rather than assumed: write `text-mg-muted/50` and the test goes red.

**The light grey ramp did not have to collapse, and three separate notes in this repo said it did.** `PROGRESS.md`, the design baseline and the a11y spec all carried "`--mg-muted` and `--mg-faint` both need `#707070`, so AA collapses the light-mode two-step ramp into one step". That assumed moving each token to the **minimum** passing value. In fact anything **at or below** `#707070` clears 4.5 on both `#f4f4f4` (4.50) and `#ffffff` (4.95) — so keeping muted *darker* than faint preserves a real ramp. `#5a5a5a` / `#707070`. The step narrows from 0.180 to 0.060 in luminance, but it survives. **Read an accessibility constraint as a range, not a point** — the "design cost" that had deterred three sessions was mostly an arithmetic error.

⚠️ **The sixteen baselines were re-recorded this time, and the contrast with the previous slice is the useful part.** Two changes ago the eyebrow colours moved and the baselines were deliberately left, because all 16 still passed at `maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.01` and re-recording would have swapped one container's antialiasing for another's with no assertion gained. Here **all sixteen genuinely moved** — two of them by a pixel of *height*, so the layout itself reflowed — and leaving them would have meant the baselines documented a design that no longer exists. `--update-snapshots` alone does not do it: Playwright only rewrites a snapshot whose comparison *fails*, and at 1% these still passed, so the PNGs had to be deleted and regenerated. **The rule that emerges: re-record when the design changed on purpose, leave them when only the renderer moved.**

### The "From" price, and a rule reused rather than reinvented

The last of the three defects the variant picker exposed. A `/shop` card read £145 for a product whose only in-stock size costs £159.99.

`cardPricePence` quotes the **lowest sellable** variant — deliberately the same rule `productJsonLd`'s `AggregateOffer` uses for `lowPrice`, and **asserted against it in a test** rather than merely written the same way twice. A card and the structured data behind it disagreeing about the lowest price is precisely the mismatch a shopping feed is penalised for, so the two are pinned together.

It says "From" **only when the buyable sizes actually differ in price**: a product whose three sizes are all £145 reads "£145", because "From £145" implies a choice the shopper does not have. Baseline-safe by construction — no seeded product carries a variant row, so every card renders exactly as before, asserted rather than hoped for.

### The contrast decision, and the repair that made it worse before it made it better

Two brand decisions were taken by the owner and implemented here. The permissions one is small and is below. The contrast one is worth the space, because **the fix this file had been recommending for three phases was wrong, and only measuring it showed that.**

**What this file recommended: "a theme-dependent accent".** Implemented literally — `--mg-accent` brightened to `#f7142e` in dark contexts, which is the nearest red to the brand hue clearing 4.5:1 on `#0d0d0d` (4.71) and `#131315` (4.50). The 15 accent-on-dark nodes cleared exactly as predicted. **The total went from 34 to 46.**

⚠️ **The accent is not only ink. It is also the red CTA band.** `bg-mg-accent` appears 59 times and `border-mg-accent` 30, against 93 `text-mg-accent` — and white text sits on that band. Lifting the token's luminance to make the *text* legible on dark lowered the contrast of *white on the fill*, from 5.88 to **4.12**, and took `text-white/90` from 5.03 to 3.52 — undoing the CTA-band repair a previous slice had made. **A colour used as both ink and fill carries two contrast requirements that pull in opposite directions.** An audit that reads foreground colours will show you one of them; nothing warns you about the other until you look at what the colour is a background *for*.

**So the answer was two tokens, not one better value.** `--mg-accent` stays `#c8102e` everywhere — it is the fill, and white must read on it. `--mg-accent-ink` is new, is the tenth theme token, and is `#f7142e` in `dark` and `darkBand`, `#c8102e` in `light`. All 93 `text-mg-accent` classes became `text-mg-accentInk` in one sweep, which is safe precisely because on light the ink resolves to the value they already had — so the light theme is unchanged by that half. `a:hover` and the big serif numeral, the two raw `var(--mg-accent)` *text* uses in `globals.css`, moved too; `.mg-underline` stayed on the fill, because it paints a rule rather than text.

⚠️ **`accent` stayed dark-only in `TOKENS_BY_CONTEXT` and `accentInk` is the three-context one**, which is the opposite of the first attempt. The `darkBand` declaration is the one doing the real work, and it is the case a delta model hides: **a dark band on a LIGHT page.** Without it the band inherits light's `#c8102e` and paints it on `#0d0d0d` at 3.30 — which is where 11 of the 15 failing accent nodes were, in *both* themes rather than only in dark.

**The other 12 nodes were never a token at all.** `text-[#ff4d5e]` was hard-coded at 14 call sites across 13 components, painting the *dark* serif accent onto light pages at 2.94. And the design bundle disagrees with itself about it: `design_handoff/CLAUDE.md`'s token table has always said the light serif accent is `#c8102e`, while the `design_files/*.dc.html` prototypes hard-code `color:#ff4d5e` inline with no theme scoping — and the components copied the prototypes faithfully. **A prototype is a rendering of one theme; the token table is the rule.** Resolved in the table's favour, which is free: `#c8102e` on `#f4f4f4` is 5.35.

**And one that cost nothing structurally.** `--mg-faint` on dark was `rgba(244,244,244,0.35)`, compositing to `#5e5e5e` at 2.99. At `0.5` it is `#818181` and 4.99. The dark ramp survives because its two steps are separate alphas — it is only the *light* ramp AA would collapse, since `--mg-muted` and `--mg-faint` both need the same `#707070`. That is why the light half was deliberately left open and is still the brand's.

**Result: 34 → 6 nodes (−82%), and all six are the light ramp.** `ACCEPTED_CONTRAST_GAPS` went from eight pairs to three, so contrast is now enforced on every route bar those.

⚠️ **The sixteen baselines were NOT re-recorded, and that was a measurement rather than a preference.** All 16 pass at the repo's `maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.01`. At zero tolerance 12 of them differ — the eyebrows really did change colour on light pages — but `--update-snapshots` rewrote nothing, because Playwright only rewrites a snapshot whose comparison *fails*. Rather than force it, the baselines were left: they were captured in a different container and already carry ~2,400px of cross-render noise on image-heavy routes (measured — `/shop`, untouched by any of this, differs by 2,396px at zero tolerance while `/about` differs by none), so re-recording would swap one container's noise for another's and buy no assertion. **What actually guards these colours now is stronger than a screenshot**: `theme.test.ts` reads `globals.css` itself and deep-compares every declaration against `DEFAULT_THEME_COLORS`, and the a11y suite enforces the ratios on every route.

### `0023`, and reading the catalogue rather than the previous migration's description of it

`0022` excluded `categories` and its header named the prerequisite precisely: reconciling two service paths that delete the same row on different permissions was "a product decision about what the taxonomy screen's delete means rather than a policy repair". The decision: **deleting a category destroys a layout document, its revisions and its publish history alongside the label, so it takes `category.delete` from both screens.** `author` keeps create and rename and loses delete — consistent rather than novel, because `author` holds **no `*.delete` permission of any kind**.

⚠️ **`0022`'s description of the defect was wrong for this table, and inheriting it would have produced a migration whose header lied.** It said `categories` had "the same OR-ed shape" — an `ALL` write policy plus an additive `x.delete` policy. `pg_policies` says otherwise: there are exactly two policies, `public read published` and `write`, and **no delete policy at all**. `category.delete` appeared in the RLS catalogue *nowhere* — a permission the application checks on every delete route and the database had never heard of. Strictly worse than additive, and only visible by querying the catalogue.

**The service change had to land before the policy, not with it.** `/admin/taxonomy` gated the delete on `taxonomy.write`; applying the restrictive policy first would have left a button that throws for `author`. So `deleteCategory` moved to `category.delete` and the screen gained `canDeleteCategory`, per-section because tags and authors are still rows rather than documents and still delete on `taxonomy.write`.

**Probed three ways on the live project, inside a transaction that rolled back.** A `taxonomy.write` holder is refused the delete; **the same actor can still rename that row**, which is the assertion that separates a working gate from a blanket `using (false)`; and a `category.delete` holder still deletes. Two throwaway `auth.users` rows were needed — `user_roles.user_id` does have an FK to `auth.users`, which `information_schema.constraint_column_usage` does not show because the `auth` schema sits outside that view, so the first probe failed on it. Rollback verified afterwards: 0 probe rows, 5 categories, 1 `user_roles` row, 0 probe users.

The `KNOWN GAP` test inverted in the same commit — **the third to complete that full cycle**, after `is_system` for `0018` and `draft_data` for `0020`.

**Verification honesty.** Ran here, green: the four gates (**1294 unit tests**), cold `npm run build`, **16/16 visual**, **28/28 a11y** with contrast enforced everywhere bar three named pairs, **14/14 perf**. `0023` applied to the live project and replayed idempotently by hand. The `rls.test.ts` changes — the inverted gap plus its positive half — **have not run here** (no Docker) and await CI.

### The PDP's two price defects, and a deferral that did not survive measurement

The variant picker's own entry recorded two things it made visible and left alone: the member price computed in pounds, and `productJsonLd` advertising a single `Offer`. Both are fixed here. **The interesting part is not either fix — they are eleven lines between them — but that the stated reason for deferring them was wrong, and wrong in a way this file has already recorded once.**

**The deferral said both "change rendered output on a baseline-verified page".** That was two claims. For the JSON-LD it is simply false: structured data is a `<script>` in the `<head>`, and no screenshot has ever contained it. For the member price it was true — "Members £123" really does become "Members £123.25" — but *true* and *expensive* are different, and nobody had checked which one this was. **Measured: all sixteen baselines pass untouched.** The perf slice recorded this exact lesson three slices ago ("*this will move pixels* is a measurement, not a deduction") and it was re-derived here rather than read, which is worth more than the fixes.

⚠️ **And the measurement needed a control, or it would have proved the opposite of what it looks like.** Re-running the PDP shots at `maxDiffPixelRatio: 0` reports **4,893 changed pixels** (light) and 4,903 (dark), scattered down the whole page rather than gathered at the price line — which reads alarmingly like the change moved far more than one string. It did not. **`/shop`, which this diff does not touch at all, differs by 2,396 pixels under the same zero tolerance, and `/about` by none.** So the great majority of that number is the pre-existing cross-render antialiasing noise the 0.01 tolerance exists to absorb, on the image-heavy routes only, and the price text is a small part of it. **A diff count with no control is not evidence** — the honest claim is "16/16 pass at the tolerance this repo has always used", not "nothing changed", and not "the page moved by 4,893 pixels" either.

**The baselines were deliberately not re-recorded.** The committed PDP shot therefore still contains the old `£123` inside a full-page image compared at 1% tolerance. That is the right trade: re-recording bakes *this* container's antialiasing into a baseline CI then runs on `ubuntu-latest`, buys nothing a test asserts, and puts an unreadable binary in the diff. What asserts the price is a unit test — `ProductView.test.tsx` now checks for `Members £123.25` **and** for the absence of `Members £123`, so the string is pinned by something a reviewer can read.

**The member fix is discount-then-subtract, mirroring the cart, not multiply-by-the-complement.** `memberUnitPrice` lives in `lib/domain/pricing.ts` beside `calculateTotals` rather than in the PDP, because the PDP is where putting it went wrong. The form matters: `1 - 0.15` is 0.85 exactly, but `1 - 0.075` is not, and the two forms round to different pennies in general. 3p at 50% is the smallest case that shows it — the discount rounds 1.5 → 2 and leaves **1**, the complement form rounds the same 1.5 and answers **2**. A test asserts both numbers so the shapes are visibly different rather than described as different.

⚠️ **A unit price times a quantity is still not the cart's member total, and that is correct rather than a residual bug.** The cart rounds the discount **once**, over the whole subtotal; the PDP rounds one unit. Three units whose 15% lands on half a penny can differ by a penny between the two. The PDP quotes what *one* costs, so the unit is the honest figure there, and the checkout stays the single authority on what is charged. Asserted as an equality for qty 1 across seven prices spanning the catalogue, which is the property that actually had to hold.

**The JSON-LD fix is an `AggregateOffer`, and the single-`Offer` branch is byte-identical to what shipped.** That is load-bearing, not tidiness: **no seeded product carries a variant row**, so the whole live catalogue's structured data is unchanged by this. Asserted as a whole-object `toEqual` rather than field by field, because anything added to that branch changes structured data on sixteen live pages.

⚠️ **The price range spans the *sellable* variants, and deciding that needed a case rather than a rule.** A discontinued small at £99 beside an in-stock large at £159 would otherwise advertise `lowPrice: 99.00` for money nobody can spend — the precise shape of mismatch a feed gets penalised for. So unsellable variants are **excluded from the range and still listed as offers**, each with its own honest `availability`; a crawler sees them, a shopping surface does not price on them. When *nothing* is sellable the range falls back to every variant, because an out-of-stock product still has a price and an absent `lowPrice` is invalid rather than honest.

**Variant availability governs and the product's own column is not consulted, which is a deliberate agreement with the page.** Once a product has variants the PDP's add-to-bag button reads the selected variant's availability and nothing else. Structured data disagreeing with the button would have been the worse bug.

**Proven end to end against the live project, because unit tests could not reach the branch that mattered.** `product_variants` holds **zero rows**, so every existing suite exercises only the single-`Offer` path — the new one would have shipped never having run against real data, the ingestion-images situation exactly. So: three `zz-probe` variants inserted on `travel-watch-roll` (a discontinued £135, a null-priced one, an in-stock £159.99), **`rm -rf .next` and a cold rebuild** — the data-change trap this file records, and skipping it would have reused the prerendered page and proved nothing — then the prerendered HTML read directly. It carried `AggregateOffer` with `lowPrice 145.00` (the discontinued £135 correctly outside the range), `highPrice 159.99`, `offerCount 3`, the null-priced variant resolved to the product's £145, `sku` present on two and **absent rather than null** on the third, and the picker rendered in the served markup. All three rows deleted afterwards and the count confirmed back at 0 against 16 published products.

**Verification honesty.** Ran here, green: the four gates (**1292 unit tests**, +15), `npm run build` cold twice with every rendering mode unchanged, **16/16 visual baselines**, **28/28 a11y**, **14/14 perf**. The integration assertion added to `publicCatalog.test.ts` — that the SEO read returns `variants: []` and the JSON-LD therefore still takes the single-`Offer` branch — **has not run here**, the usual Docker constraint, and awaits CI.

### The variant picker, and the five gaps under it

- **CI found the one thing this container structurally cannot: a renamed control that an E2E spec still clicked.** `patterns.spec.ts` waited for a button named "Rename" and timed out on all three retries — not a flake, a real breakage. The patterns row control became **"Edit"** when the dialog grew `description` and `category_id` alongside the name and the key, and its toast became "Pattern updated". **The general shape is worth remembering: renaming a user-visible control is a silent API change to the E2E suite**, and no container without `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL` can catch it. After fixing it, every other label this slice changed was audited against the specs by hand — the publish dialog's `Slug` → `URL`/`Key` row, the new Theme History button, the two added fields in the create-pattern dialog, the PDP's picker and SKU line — and none is asserted anywhere, nor introduces a locator ambiguity. Nothing else was broken.

- **Reading the diff adversarially after CI went red found a second bug CI could not have.** Widening the rename dialog moved `error={error}` off the Key field and onto the newly-added category select, so a duplicate-key message would have rendered under an unrelated field. The E2E assertion is a dialog-wide text match, so it passed either way — the *test* was fine and the *UI* was wrong. Moved back. **A green test is not evidence the thing it covers is right, only that it still behaves the way the assertion describes.**

- **The integration suite — the half no session container can run — passed on the first attempt**, including `publicCatalog.test.ts`'s new variant read executing against real RLS policies for the first time. `0019`'s parent-status policy does let an anonymous caller read a published product's variants, which was the assumption the whole public read rested on and the one thing that could not be checked locally.

- **The cart's line key is the slug when there is no variant, and that is load-bearing rather than tidy.** Two sizes of one product have to be two lines, so a cart keyed by slug could not survive variants. But every bag already in a shopper's `localStorage` holds `{ slug, qty }`, and every call site says `cart.remove(l.slug)`. Making `cartLineKey(slug, null)` **equal** the slug means the stored shape stays valid, old bags price exactly as they did, and only the lines that genuinely need a second dimension get one. The alternative — a migration of everyone's bag, or a version field — would have been more code for a worse outcome.

- **A card's quick-add resolves the default variant in the provider, not at the call site.** `ProductRow` and `/shop` call `cart.add(p.slug)` and have no picker to read a size from. Resolving `defaultVariant` inside `CartProvider.add` left all those call sites untouched and correct, and makes it impossible for a variant product to enter the bag as an unnamed, unpriced line. Pushing the decision outward would have meant the same three lines in three components and a fourth that forgot.

- **`Product.variants` is absent rather than empty when a product has none.** `tests/integration/publicCatalog.test.ts` deep-compares the database read against `lib/demo/catalog.ts` field by field, and that comparison is the assertion the whole of Phase 7b rests on. An always-present `variants: []` would have failed it on a difference that is not one. Absent also happens to be what makes the picker provably baseline-safe.

- **A `pricePence` field on `Product` was written and then reverted, which was the right call twice over.** Carrying the product's canonical pence alongside its pounds looked tidier than converting at the boundary. It would have broken the same demo-fixture equality, and `poundsToPence` is *designed* to be exact (it collapses to 4 decimal places before rounding, precisely so `145.15 * 100 = 14514.999…` does not round the wrong way). The existing boundary was already correct; adding a second representation would have bought a second thing to keep in step.

- **The line total moved from pounds to pence, and the test for it asserts a raw number rather than a formatted one.** `product.price * qty` is exact only while every price is a whole number of pounds, which is true of all sixteen seeded products and false the moment a merchant types £159.99. The trap in testing it: `formatGBP` uses `toLocaleString`, which rounds to three decimals, so `479.96999999999997` *prints* as "£479.97" and a string assertion sails past the very error it was written for. The test reads `l.lineTotal` directly.

- **A `KNOWN GAP` is the honest answer when a bug turns out to be a design decision.** Fixing `mg.accentSerif` was a one-token change until the guard test written alongside it reported `bd`, `bg` and `fg` as well — 417 usages, site-wide, on a pixel-verified design. Fixing those is not a session's call, and *describing* them in prose is how a gap goes unnoticed for another three phases. Asserting the broken state with inversion instructions is the fifth time this file records the technique paying off. **The general lesson: write the guard test as a rule over the source rather than an assertion about the token you came to fix.** Naming `accentSerif` would have fixed today's instance and left the next one to be found the same way — by accident, years later.

- **"This is broken" was measured against Tailwind's actual output, not reasoned from the docs.** The premise that a bare `var()` kills an alpha utility came from a comment in `tailwind.config.ts`; when the guard test flagged `fg` — used 312 times on a site that looks correct — the honest move was to run Tailwind over a probe file and read the CSS rather than trust either the comment or the appearance of the site. It confirmed the comment and enlarged the bug. Same shape as the performance slice's lesson one phase earlier: *"this will move pixels"* is a measurement, not a deduction.

- **Four of the five smaller gaps were the same defect in different clothes: a column the schema had, the repository accepted, and no caller ever wrote.** `patterns.description` (accepted by `createPattern` since Phase 4), `patterns.category_id` (five rows seeded by `0003`, reachable from nothing), `media_assets.width`/`height` (accepted by `insertAsset` since Phase 5a), and the theme's revision history (written by `publish_document` since `0017`, read by no screen). None needed a migration. **Worth a standing check: when a feature looks half-built, read the repository before the schema — the gap is usually one layer above where it appears to be.**

- **The `media_assets` dimensions are read by a header parser rather than an image library.** It adds no dependency, reads a few dozen bytes instead of decoding a 20 MB photograph, and is pure enough to live in `lib/domain` under the layering rules — with no `node:` import, which that rule makes a build failure rather than a style question. Its tests use **real encoded images** rather than hand-written header bytes, because a fixture assembled from the same reading of the same spec as the parser can be wrong in the same direction and the two would agree with each other while disagreeing with every real file. 37 × 19: two different primes, so a transposed width and height cannot pass.

- **A defect found on the way to something else was fixed on the way past.** All five actions in `catalog-actions.ts` revalidated only the admin screen. For variants that was invisible — no public surface — but `product_media` has fed the public PDP since Phase 7b, so adding a photograph left the live page stale for up to an hour. `revalidate.ts` states the rule in its own header ("anything that changes what a visitor sees calls this") and this was the one file that never called it. **A shared helper does not enforce anything; only a caller does.**

- **The integration test that writes rows is gated on `isLocal`, matching the rule this file already had.** The Supabase project is shared with a live human. An unpublish probe that fails halfway leaves a product hidden; a *variant* probe that fails halfway leaves a picker showing on a live PDP — visible to shoppers rather than merely wrong. Same gate, stronger reason.

- **The theme's history goes direct to the repository, for the same reason its publish goes direct to the RPC.** `lib/services/revisions.ts` is typed `DocumentType` and `theme` deliberately is not one — widening `DOCUMENT_TYPES` would open the block-validation and scheduling paths to something with no blocks and no `scheduled_for`. The repository now takes a `RevisionEntityType` that is `DocumentType | "theme"`, which is an honest description of what `revisions.entity_type` holds: plain `text`, no foreign key, no CHECK, and `theme` in it since `0017`.

- **The theme rollback does not revalidate `/`, unlike every other action in that file.** A rollback lands in the draft; the site is still serving the last published palette. Revalidating would be a lie about what changed and an expensive one — the token `<style>` block is in the *root* layout, so it rebuilds all 65 static pages plus the admin.

- **`HistoryView` passes an id and the theme action ignores it.** The component is shared with six document types so it sends `{ id, version }`, but there is exactly one theme row and the service finds it by key. Accepting a client-supplied id and writing to whatever row it named is how a shared component becomes a way to edit something else.


- **Contrast: 81% of it was a repair, not a decision — and finding that out took one measurement.**

  This file, `design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/CLAUDE.md` and the `KNOWN GAP` test all said the same thing: the palette fails AA, fixing it means changing tokens or type sizes, that moves pixels on a pixel-verified site, and **it invalidates the sixteen baselines by construction** — so it is the brand's call. The first half was true. **The second half was a deduction nobody had checked, and it was wrong**, which is the second time in one session that a "this will move pixels" claim in this file dissolved on contact with a measurement.

  **What the violations actually are.** Re-audited across 9 routes × 2 themes: **175 nodes**, and the distribution is nothing like "four token-level causes" implies —

  | nodes | pair | ratio | what it is |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | **128** | `#696969` on `#0d0d0d` | 3.54 | dark-band mono labels |
  | 24 | the red at small sizes | 2.94–3.30 | `#c8102e` on dark, `#ff4d5e` on light |
  | 14 | `#f4cfd5`/`#f1c3cb` on `#c8102e` | 3.75–4.12 | the CTA band's whites |
  | 9 | `#8a8a8a`, `#b0b0b0` on light | 1.97–3.45 | `--mg-muted` / `--mg-faint` |

  **The 73% case is not a design token at all**, which is what everything written about this had missed. `#696969` is `text-[#f4f4f4]/40` composited over the dark band, and it occurs in **three places** — two in `Footer.tsx`, one in `LatestGrid.tsx`. The footer is always dark and renders on every route, so three class usages produce 128 violation nodes, and they appear in **both** themes because `[data-darkband]` keeps the band dark in light mode too. Likewise the pinks: `#f4cfd5` is `text-white/80` over the red, `#f1c3cb` is `text-white/75`. **Four "token-level causes" were really two opacity steps and a brand colour.**

  **The repairs, and what they cost.** `/40 → /50` on the three dark-band sites (**3.54 → 4.99**), and on the seven red-band sites `/80 → /90` (**4.13 → 4.95**) and `/75 → /85` (**3.76 → 4.53**), which keeps the two-step ramp's direction — 85 is the *minimum* that passes on the red at all, so the range 85–100 is all that was available. Result: **175 → 33 violations**, four gates green, **16/16 baselines unmoved**. The cost was zero. It was always zero.

  **The structural half is the more valuable one.** `color-contrast` had been `disableRules`d on every per-route audit — correct while 175 nodes failed for one undecided reason, because a suite that fails everywhere reports nothing about anything. It stopped being correct the moment the decidable part was fixed: with the rule off, a **new** contrast bug anywhere was invisible, which is the same "green check that checks nothing" shape this file has now recorded four times. It is replaced by `ACCEPTED_CONTRAST_GAPS` — eight named colour pairs, each with its measured ratio and why it is still there — so **contrast is now enforced everywhere else**. Matching is on the colour pair rather than the element, deliberately: the pair is what the brand has to decide about and it does not move when content does, where a selector-keyed list would make every editorial change look like a regression.

  **Proven to bite before being trusted**, like the perf budget: putting a single `/40` back on the footer failed the homepage audit in *both* themes, naming `color-contrast`. And the `KNOWN GAP` test now asserts two things rather than one — that the remaining gaps are still failing (so it inverts when settled) *and* that nothing has regressed **into** the accepted list.

  ⚠️ **What is left is the real decision, and the arithmetic is why.** `#c8102e` reads 3.30 on the dark band; lightening it ~25% toward white reaches 4.69 there and then **drops to 3.77 on the light page**, where the current red passes at 5.35. **No single red clears 4.5:1 at small text in both themes** — so the choice is a theme-dependent accent or an accent reserved for large/non-text use, and that is brand identity, not implementation. The light grey ramp is the other 9: `--mg-muted` and `--mg-faint` both need `#707070`, i.e. AA collapses a two-step ramp into one step.

  ⚠️ **One caveat on the red-band fix**: `--mg-accent` is editable through `/admin/theme`, so these ratios hold for the default palette and an editor can move them. That is true of the whole palette and is not specific to this change — but the accepted-gaps list is written against the shipped values.

- **The performance budget: the pass was images, and only images — and it did not cost a single baseline.**

  Phase 7's last third. This file predicted it would touch "image sizing, font loading and a Core Web Vitals budget" and would "invalidate the sixteen baselines by construction". **Two of those three turned out to need nothing, and the baselines never moved.** What follows is what measuring first bought, because none of it was deducible.

  **Fonts and CLS were already correct.** `next/font/google` self-hosts, preloads and generates a metric-adjusted fallback, so the whole font-loading item was already done in `app/layout.tsx` — 57 KB of fonts per route with no third-party connection. **CLS measured 0.000 on every route in both viewports** before any change, because the design's fixed-aspect containers (`aspect-[4/5]`, `h-[300px]`, `absolute inset-0`) reserve every image's box in CSS. There was nothing to fix in either, so the pass reduced to page weight.

  **The measurement, taken before touching anything.** The homepage shipped **3,936 KB of images to a 375px phone — byte-identical to what it sent a 1440px desktop**, and that was 83% of page weight. `hero-cover.jpg` alone was 1,637 KB at 3840×2160, fetched in full on every visit. A 1440×1800 JPEG was being painted into a **208×255** card: 48× more pixels than the slot can show. Every one of these was invisible to every existing gate — 1190 unit tests, 16 baselines, 28 a11y checks and a green build, all passing while this was true.

  **Why the budget is in bytes and not Core Web Vitals.** LCP on this hardware reads 90–350 ms because the server is on loopback; the same page on a phone is a different number, and a timing budget would have been measuring the container and flaking on a busy runner. Bytes are the part the code controls and they reproduce exactly. `tests/perf/budget.spec.ts` asserts image bytes per route at a mobile viewport, plus a second net — **no `<img>` may be more than 2.5× the width it is painted at**, checked at desktop DPR 1 — because a lost `sizes` attribute is caught structurally there even where it slips under a byte budget. **The gate was proven to bite before being trusted**: setting `IMAGE_SIZES.quarter` to `100vw` turned both `/shop` assertions red with a readable message. That check exists because this file records three separate cases of a green step that was checking nothing.

  **Two families of image, and only one of them is an `<img>`.** `MediaImage` (over `next/image`, `fill` + a per-slot `sizes`) replaced all 23 `<img>` sites — each of which carried its own `eslint-disable-next-line @next/next/no-img-element`, so the rule had been asking for this since Track A. **That alone left `/style` at the full 3,936 KB**, because the design renders most decorative imagery as CSS `bg-cover` layers, and the heaviest asset of all is a `<video poster>`. Neither is reachable by `next/image`. `optimizedImageUrl` / `backgroundImageUrl` (over Next's supported `getImageProps`) covers those seven background sites and three posters **in place**, leaving the CSS exactly as it was rather than restructuring `aria-hidden` scrim layers into elements.

  **A defect in the first version of that helper, worth recording because nothing warned.** `getImageProps().props.src` is the **largest** candidate it generated — the `2x` entry — so asking for a 1920px poster fetched a **3840px** file. The hero came back at 421 KB when ~200 KB was intended, and the URL simply carried a `w=` twice what was asked for. `oneXFromSrcSet` takes the `1x` instead (147 KB). Its first parser then split `srcSet` on commas and chopped a URL containing one in half; anchoring the match on the descriptor rather than the separator is what makes it correct. A second sizing error in the same helper: the FilmStills posters were requested at 1920 for a **320px** strip cell — 352 KB for three images that need 68.

  **`unoptimized` is a fallback, not a switch, and this is the part with teeth.** `next/image` **throws** on a host it is not configured for, and on a public route that is a 500 rather than an ugly picture. Public image URLs come out of the database and `MediaUrlControl` lets an editor type any URL they like, so "every src is a path we control" is a fact about the seed data, not a property of the system. `lib/domain/images.ts#isOptimizableImageSrc` decides, and refuses SVG too (the optimiser answers 400 without `dangerouslyAllowSVG`, and both site logos are SVGs). `OPTIMIZABLE_IMAGE_HOSTS` and `next.config.mjs`'s `remotePatterns` are a pair that can drift silently into exactly that 500, so a unit test **reads the config** and asserts they agree rather than commenting that they must.

  **The results**, mobile image bytes: `/` **3,936 → 300 KB (−92%)**, `/shop` **3,936 → 67**, `/style` **3,936 → 159**, `/article/speed-considered` **2,681 → 100**, the PDP **3,355 → 79**. Warm LCP on `/` went 248 ms → **216 ms**. 1202 unit tests (+12), four gates green, build green with `/` still `○ (Static)` and `/[category]` still `●`, **16/16 baselines unmoved**, 28/28 a11y, 14 perf.

  ⚠️ **One real trade-off, recorded rather than buried.** A `<video poster>` and a `background-image` carry no `srcset`, so each takes **one** width for every viewport — those layers are no longer pixel-identical at every zoom level, and the baselines only see them at 1440px (and mask the videos entirely). That is a weaker guarantee than the baselines usually give. ⚠️ **And a deployment note**: `/_next/image` optimises on demand and caches to disk, so the *first* request for each variant is slow — cold LCP on `/` measured 1,068 ms against 216 ms warm. Railway serves this with no CDN in front, so the first visitor after a deploy pays it. That is a caching question, not a code one, and it is not addressed here.

  ⚠️ **Three public `<img>` sites are deliberately left raw, and this is the list.** The **cart line thumbnails** (`app/(site)/bag/page.tsx`, `components/chrome/BagDrawer.tsx`) and the **search-result rows** (`components/chrome/SearchOverlay.tsx`). None of them ships on any route's initial load — the bag is empty until something is added and the overlay opens on demand — so they are worth nothing against the budget, while converting them *would* cost something: each sizes the image on the element itself rather than on a parent box, so `fill` needs a wrapper, and **none of the three has a visual baseline**, the bag and the search overlay being interaction states the 16 shots never reach. That is the wrong trade — restructuring unprotected surfaces for no measurable gain. The three SVG logos (`Header`, `Drawer`, `Footer`) stay raw for a different and permanent reason: the optimiser refuses SVG, and vector needs no `srcset`. **The admin's own previews stay raw too**, as `components/admin/media/AssetThumb.tsx` and `MediaUrlControl.tsx` have documented since Phase 5a — the same third-party-URL reasoning this slice generalised into `isOptimizableImageSrc`.

  **The contrast decision was deliberately left alone, and the advice to bundle them was wrong.** This file recommended taking the 204 contrast failures in the same pass, on the reasoning that both change pixels and both would force re-recording the 16 baselines — do it once, not twice. **The performance half moved no pixels at all**: every baseline passed untouched, WebP re-encodes included, because `maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.01` sits above the per-pixel noise a re-encode adds. Bundling would have spent a brand decision to buy a re-record that was never needed. **"This will move pixels" is a measurement, not a deduction** — and the contrast decision remains open, and remains the brand's.

- **Apply runs in bounded batches — and the fix is not the queue this file predicted, which is the point of the entry.**

  The hole read: *"a run holds a server action open for its whole duration rather than queueing, which `import_jobs.status` already has a `'queued'` value for."* The problem statement was right and **the prescribed mechanism was wrong**, and it took reading the apply path rather than the note to see it.

  **Most of a queue was already there.** `applyJob` selects only items with status `approved`, and marks each one `applied` or `failed` the moment it is written. So it has *always* been idempotent and resumable: calling it twice applies each item exactly once, and the second call picks up precisely what the first did not reach.
