What it lacked was a **bound** and a way to report **"there is more"** — two lines and a loop, against a route, a workflow, a service and a permission model.

  **Three things bounding-instead-of-queueing keeps, all of which a worker would have cost:**
  - The editor sees progress as it happens — the button reads "Applying — 47 of 120 written" — instead of a job that is silently somewhere.
  - **The apply still runs as the editor.** `product.write` and `media.write` are checked against a real session. A worker has none, so it would run service-role, which outranks both.
  - No new route, no new workflow, and **no latency floor**: a queued apply on a five-minute tick means clicking Apply and waiting.

  ⚠️ **The permission question is what actually settles it, and it is worth stating because it is not obvious until you try to build the queue.** A background apply has to answer *"may this job import images?"* with no session to ask. `uploadAsset` requires `media.write`, which `applyJob` does not otherwise need — a merchandiser can legitimately hold `product.write` and `integration.run` without it. The honest options were a new column on `import_jobs` recording the queuer's rights at queue time, or a service-role client that quietly grants everyone `media.write` by proxy. **A privilege escalation is a strange price for a progress bar.**

  **So `'queued'` stays unused, and that is now a decision rather than an oversight.** It remains the right value for a **webhook**-driven run, which genuinely has no session and genuinely needs one — `import_jobs.trigger` has accepted `'webhook'` since `0006` and nothing sends one yet.

  **Two caps that must not fight, asserted rather than assumed.** `APPLY_BATCH_SIZE` (25) and `MAX_IMAGE_DOWNLOADS_PER_RUN` (60) bound different things, and a batch larger than the image budget would mean the last items of *every* batch silently importing no photographs while reporting success — the "run apply again" message firing forever. A test asserts the ordering, and the constant was moved into `lib/domain` to sit beside the budget it is paired with.

  **The layering rule caught that move for me.** The test first imported `APPLY_BATCH_SIZE` from `lib/services`, and `no-restricted-imports` refused it: `lib/domain` must stay pure. The right response was not to relax the test's location but to notice the constant was in the wrong layer — it is a policy number with no I/O, and it belongs next to the other policy numbers. **The lint rule found a design mistake, not an inconvenience**, which is what this file's "if it complains, the design is wrong, not the rule" note has always claimed and this is the first time it has been recorded happening.

  **A loop needs a ceiling even when it cannot spin.** `MAX_APPLY_BATCHES` allows 2,500 items — far past anything these feeds produce — and exists only so that a future bug stopping `remaining` from falling ends in a message rather than an unbounded loop against the database. The partial total is reported on failure too: "0 written" after nineteen products landed would send an editor hunting a problem that is not there.

- **Scheduled feed runs — a coarse vocabulary on purpose, and the reader and the writer landed together.**

  **1. `sync_schedule` is a four-option vocabulary, not cron, and that is the whole design decision.** The column is free text and would hold a quarter-hourly cron expression happily. The thing firing it is GitHub Actions, which **this repo has already measured drifting over ninety minutes on a quiet repository** — recorded in the scheduled-publish phase, and the reason this file warns against building anything that assumes tighter timing. A cron field would let an operator write a precision the platform cannot deliver, and the first person to find out would be the merchant whose fifteen-minute sync ran twice that morning. `hourly` / `daily` / `weekly` / off promises only what is true, and the selector's own label says "roughly, whenever the runner next fires".

  **2. The column was stored by nothing *and* read by nothing, and both halves are fixed here.** `sync_schedule` has existed since `0005`; the repository had a setter; no screen wrote it and no code read it. That is the `template_assignments` shape exactly — an abstraction complete except for the entry point — met two slices after learning it. **The selector is in this slice rather than a follow-up for that reason**, and the check that catches this class is cheap: grep for the column and see whether *both* a writer and a reader exist outside the repository.

  ⚠️ **3. The grace window is not a rounding convenience, it is the fix for a specific bug.** A poller firing every N minutes and asking "has a full hour elapsed?" answers *no* when a tick lands seconds early, and the source then waits another whole period — an hourly schedule silently running two-hourly, which looks exactly like the feature working at half its configured rate. `SYNC_DUE_GRACE_MS` lets a source be due slightly before its interval closes. A test asserts the grace stays under half the shortest interval, so no schedule can ever run twice in one period because of it.

  **4. `last_synced_at` records the last *attempt*, not the last success**, and the due check uses it as-is. A source whose feed is down would otherwise be retried by every tick, hammering a server that is already struggling. A failed run waits its interval like any other; `last_status` is what tells an operator it failed, and the workflow raises a notice rather than a red X because one merchant's CDN being down is not a failure of the runner.

  ⚠️ **5. A scheduled run stages and never applies.** It ends at `review` exactly as the manual button does, and `applyJob` — the only thing that writes `products` — still needs a person. This is `FEED_TARGET_FIELDS` refusing `status` ("a feed that can reach the live store without a human is a feed that can take the store down without one") arrived at from the other direction: a *schedule* must not become the way around that rule.

  **6. No permission check in the runner, and it is not an oversight.** `runImportCore` has no session — that is what a schedule is. The right to run on a schedule is asserted when the schedule is *set*: `saveSourceAction` requires `integration.write` before it will store one. Checking again in the runner would mean checking against nobody. Same reasoning `runScheduledPublishes` records for `schedule_document`, and the two runners are deliberately structural twins so that there is one failure model to learn rather than two.

  **What the trap caught before it cost anything:** the vocabulary went into `lib/domain/ingestion.ts` and **not** into `lib/domain/jobs.ts`, where the other job constants live. `jobs.ts` imports `node:crypto`, and `SourceEditor.tsx` is a client component — so the natural placement would have been `UnhandledSchemeError` at build time, with `tsc`, ESLint and the whole unit suite green beforehand. This file's own standing rule named it in advance; it is now the second module the rule has applied to, and the first where it was avoided rather than discovered.

  **A comment terminator did cost something, briefly, and is worth one line:** a JSDoc block explaining "deliberately not cron" contained a literal cron expression, whose `*` + `/` closed the comment and produced a parse error forty lines further down. Prettier named the line; `tsc` would have been more cryptic. **Do not put a cron expression in a block comment.**

  ⚠️ **Verification, split honestly.** The **auth boundary was probed end to end** against a real build: no header → 401, wrong secret → 401 with an identical body (a 401 that explains itself tells an attacker which half they got right), correct secret → **503 naming `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`** — which is itself the proof the correct secret passed the constant-time compare before hitting the env boundary — and `GET` → 405. **The run itself is unverified and cannot be from a container**: it is a service-role caller with no session, and `integrations.spec.ts` deliberately never runs an import. So the due decision was pulled out whole into a pure function with fourteen assertions covering the grace window, a future timestamp, an unparseable one and a schedule the column predates. What is untested is the loop around it.

- **Ingestion imports images — four decisions, and an honest gap.**

  **1. `images` is a target that is not a column, and it says so.** Every other entry in `FEED_TARGET_FIELDS` maps to a `products` column; this one stages a list of URLs that `applyJob` turns into `media_assets` rows and `product_media` links *after* the product is written. `columnsForApply` copies every field it is not told to skip, so `images` is excluded **by name** — copied blindly it reaches `products` as a column that does not exist, and the failure lands on the apply of a run that validated cleanly. There is a unit test whose only job is that exclusion.

  **2. Two caps, and the per-run one is the load-bearing half.** `applyJob` holds a server action open for its whole duration — a cost recorded since the ingestion phase — and images multiply it: fifty products with five photographs each is 250 downloads inside one request. `MAX_IMAGES_PER_PRODUCT` bounds a pathological record; `MAX_IMAGE_DOWNLOADS_PER_RUN` bounds the request. **Truncating is safe here in a way it usually is not, and that is what makes a budget the right answer rather than a queue**: the import is idempotent end to end — `uploadAsset` deduplicates on a SHA-256 of the bytes and `attachProductMedia` upserts on `(product_id, asset_id)` — so re-running apply costs nothing for what already landed and picks up exactly what was skipped. The message says "run apply again", because "47 images not imported" on its own reads as data loss.

  **3. An import attaches and never detaches.** Same stance as the rest of ingestion: an import does not publish, does not rename, does not rewrite the block tree — and does not curate a gallery. A merchant dropping a photograph from their feed must not silently remove one an editor chose to feature. The cost is real and stated: a gallery accumulates images the source no longer lists, and pruning is an editor's job on `/admin/products`.

  **4. Images are imported after the item is marked applied, outside its try/catch.** The product row is written and correct; a photograph that 404s is not a reason to mark the item failed and re-stage a product the catalogue already has. Image trouble is reported in the run's errors and the item stays applied. Likewise `media.write` is checked **once, up front** rather than discovered per image — `applyJob` needs `integration.run` and `product.write`, so a merchandiser may legitimately lack it, and left to fail per photograph it produces one "the database refused the write" per image, naming neither the permission nor the reason.

  ⚠️ **SVG is refused from a feed, and that is a security decision rather than a format preference.** `media_assets` serves from a public bucket, so an SVG a merchant controls would be stored XSS on our own origin. A person uploading one through the library has made a decision; a feed has not. Non-http(s) protocols are refused at coercion for the same class of reason — these URLs are fetched *by the server*, so `file:` and `data:` are the boundary, not a tidiness rule.

  **What cost nothing, and is worth recording because it is the interface holding:** the mapping editor needed **no change at all**. It builds its options from `FEED_TARGET_FIELD_NAMES`, so `images` appeared by itself — the opposite of the Shopify adapter's lesson, where the abstraction held and every screen above it was shaped like the first provider.

  ⚠️ **The gap, stated plainly: the fetch → upload → attach path is exercised nowhere.** `tests/e2e/integrations.spec.ts` deliberately never runs an import — "a real merchant feed means making a stranger's server part of this suite, and a fixture served from the test process would be testing Playwright's ability to serve a file" — and that reasoning covers image downloads unchanged. So the response to it was to **move as much as possible out of the I/O**: `imageImportPlan`, `imageTypeProblem`, `imageFileNameFrom` and the `urlList` coercion are all pure and unit-tested (30 assertions), and `fetchBinaryCapped` takes an injected `fetch` so its streamed cap, its lying-`content-length` case and its timeout are all covered with no network. What remains untested is the thin part: three awaits in `imageAssetFor` and `importImages`. **That is a real gap and not a claim of coverage** — the first person to run a feed with images is the first person to exercise it.

- **Assigning a template — the first writer `template_assignments` has ever had, and three decisions plus one bug.**

  **1. Only the scopes the public site reads are offered.** `taxonomy` is a real column with a real unique index, and `resolveTemplateId` — the *admin* resolver — implements it. `publishedTemplateArea`, which is what the public routes actually go through, does not. Offering it would let an editor make a choice that changes nothing on the site, which is the exact shape of the `visibility.devices` trap and of the three phases synced patterns spent "supported but silently lossy". **Withheld in code, not merely undocumented** — the same stance that stopped anyone shipping a page that lost a section. The dialog says so in words rather than leaving the absence to be discovered.

  **2. Reassignment replaces rather than refuses.** `0003` puts a unique index on `content_type where scope='content_type'` and another on `entry_id where scope='entry'`, so a bare insert fails with `23505` the moment an editor points "every page" at a second template — which is the ordinary thing to want. `assignTemplate` clears the target first, and the picker names the current holder in the option text (`Every page — currently Editorial`) so that displacing one is a visible choice made *before* the write, not an error afterwards. ⚠️ **Delete-then-insert is not transactional** — PostgREST has no multi-statement transaction — so an interrupted save leaves the target assigned to *nothing* rather than to its previous holder. Stated rather than hidden: it is visible on the next screen and recoverable in one click, and the alternative shape (insert first) would need two rows racing one unique index. `replaceMappings` carries the identical caveat.

  **3. The target string is parsed, not trusted, and the pairing is what matters.** `parseAssignmentTarget(value, contentType)` takes the content type from the *template's own kind* and refuses a value that names a different one — so a `page` template cannot be pointed at `content_type:category` by anyone willing to edit a form value. RLS would permit that write: `template.write` is a permission over the table, not over which row makes sense. An entry id must match a uuid before it reaches `.eq("entry_id", …)`, or Postgres answers `22P02` instead of the screen answering in words.

  ⚠️ **The bug found on the way, and it was live: deleting an assigned template revalidated nothing.** `template_assignments` cascades on the template id, so the assignment vanished correctly — but every page the template framed kept serving its *framed* prerendered HTML until the hourly backstop expired. An hour of pages rendering a layout that no longer exists, with nothing in the admin hinting at it. Fixed by collecting `publicPathsForTemplate` **before** the delete, which is the only moment those paths are still knowable. **The general shape is worth carrying: a cascade is a data fix, never a cache fix.**

  **On the N+1 that nearly shipped.** The screen first called `listAssignmentTargets` and `currentAssignments` once per template — four round trips per row over the same three tables. Templates are few, so it was never going to be slow enough for anyone to notice, **which is exactly why it would have stayed**. `assignmentBoard` reads once and shares; the per-template exports were deleted rather than left beside it, because two ways to answer one question is how they drift.

  ⚠️ **Verification honesty: none of this can be exercised from a session container, and the E2E spec is the only check that will ever run.** Assignment is a staff write under RLS through the editor's own session, so — unlike the render half, which a live-project probe could prove — there is no script, no `curl` and no service-role shortcut that exercises it without being the wrong thing. The spec assigns, reloads to prove it is a row rather than component state, then **unassigns inside the same test**: the visual suite runs after E2E in the same job against the same stack, so a template left framing every page would move the homepage baseline and fail an unrelated suite. The `cleans up` test would cascade the row away, but a cascade revalidates nothing — which is the bug above, met from the other direction.

- **`archive` templates — the render was one function, and the two things worth reading are what the verification turned up.**

  **The change itself was as small as the entry predicted.** `composePublishedCategory` is `composePublishedPage` with the two arguments it already had plus bindings, and `/[category]` calls it. The only genuinely new decision: **the binding resolver is injected, not imported.** `supabaseBindingSources` lives in `lib/services/bindingSources.ts`, and importing it into `lib/services/publicContent.ts` — the module *every* public route reads through — for one caller's benefit would be the wrong dependency. The route already holds both halves, so it passes the resolver in. The category's own `$bind` descriptors are consumed by the first pass and the second pass is free (`resolveBindings` returns its input unchanged when no requests remain); what the second pass is *for* is the template's own blocks, which can hold an `articleGrid` just as easily.

  ⚠️ **`npm run build` does not re-render a static page when only the database changed, and it cost an hour of chasing a bug that was not there.** The probe template was published, the build re-run, the page served — and the frame was missing from four of five category pages while `film` had it. The code was correct the whole time; a direct `tsx` call through `composePublishedCategory` produced the framed tree immediately. Next reuses prerendered output from `.next/cache` when the *code* has not changed, and it has no way to know a row moved. `rm -rf .next` and rebuild: all five framed.

  **This invalidates part of this file's own verification recipe.** "Proving a structural refactor is render-safe" says to build before and after and diff `.next/server/app/*.html`. That is still right for a **code** change, which is what it was written for. For a change whose effect comes from **data**, the second build can silently hand back the first one's output. **Delete `.next` between the two builds whenever the thing you changed is a row.** CI and Railway are unaffected — both build from a cold checkout — so this is a local-verification trap only, which is precisely the kind that survives.

  ⚠️ **And the finding that matters more than the slice: `template_assignments` has no writer anywhere in the app.** Established by grep, not by inference — `lib/db/repositories/templates.ts` holds `resolveTemplateId` and no insert; `app/` mentions the table twice, in a delete-warning sentence and a comment. So **an editor cannot assign a template to anything without SQL**, and every template feature built since the renderer landed sits on top of an entry point nobody can reach from the admin.

  **How it went unnoticed for three slices is the reusable part.** Each was verified end to end against the live project — and each verification created the assignment *by hand*, with an `insert into template_assignments`. That step felt like fixture setup. It is in fact the one action a real editor cannot perform, and doing it by hand made every subsequent assertion true and beside the point. **When a live-project probe needs a row that no screen can create, that missing screen is the finding** — not a detail of the harness. Written up as item 0f in the next-phase list.

  ⚠️ **A live template belonging to the user was found in the project mid-probe and left alone.** `templates` read 1 rather than the 0 this session left behind: a `page`-kind draft named "Testing", created while this work was in progress. Checked before touching anything, and the probe was given a `zz-`prefixed key so the two could never be confused. **Read the table before writing to a shared project** — "I left it empty" is a fact about the past.

- **Previewing a template area — three decisions, and the first one is the reusable finding.**

  **1. The area is a query parameter, because `resolve_preview` returns five columns and `context` is not one of them.** `preview_sessions` has a `context jsonb` column, defaulted to `{}`, written by `createPreviewSession` and read by nothing. It looks exactly like the designed home for "which area is this link for", and the Known-issues bullet that asked for this slice effectively assumed it was. It is unusable: an anonymous previewer cannot read `preview_sessions` at all — that is the whole point of `0010`'s `security definer` function — so the only values that reach the route are the five in the function's `returns table (...)`. Widening it means `drop function` (Postgres refuses `create or replace` when the OUT parameters change) plus re-issuing the `anon`/`authenticated` grants, in a migration, for a value that **gates nothing**: the token already hands over the template's whole `draft_data`, every area of it. So which area gets drawn is a view concern, and the query string is where view concerns go.

  **The general lesson, which this file keeps meeting from new directions: read the function, not the table.** The column existed, was writable, and was useless. Wherever a `security definer` function is the only read path, its return type is the schema — the table behind it is not.

  ⚠️ **A consequence worth keeping: the parameter is untrusted and is checked twice, deliberately.** `createPreviewAction` checks *shape* only (`AREA_NAME_PATTERN`) because it would need a read it does not otherwise need to know which areas exist; the route checks *membership*, against the payload it has already loaded. Neither is redundant — a hand-edited `?area=` never passes through the action at all, which is why the route is where the real check has to live. `resolvePreviewArea` applies `isAreaName` **before** the key lookup so an inherited key (`constructor`, `__proto__`) cannot select anything; `Object.hasOwn` already refuses those, and the pattern check is the belt to that braces.

  **2. Which page a template frames: exactly one, or none.** A template previewed alone is a header band sitting on a footer band, and what an editor is deciding about is how it looks *around a page* — so `soleFramedPage` resolves the published pages the template is assigned to and substitutes that page's real sections at the marker. **It refuses to choose.** A `content_type` assignment frames every published page there is, and putting one of several in front of an editor and calling it a preview is exactly the arbitrariness `findContentArea` refuses when two areas hold a marker and `validateTemplateAreas` refuses when a template holds two. Two rows are fetched where one is wanted (`.limit(2)`, then `length !== 1`), because a `.limit(1)` would make three pages look like one.

  **3. `documentContent` renders `null`, so "the marker rendering as the visible gap it already is" was not true** — the gap is *invisible*, and an editor cannot tell a marker from a missing one by looking, which is the state publish validation refuses. So `documentContentGap` exists: a `hidden` block, never offered and never authorable, spliced in **through `applyTemplate`** rather than by a renderer special case. Going through the same substitution the live site uses is what makes a marker nested inside a `columns`/`column` land in the right place here for free, instead of by a second rule that could drift from the first. It is a real registered block with a manifest for the reason `DocumentContent`'s own header gives: `validateBlock` refuses an unregistered `_type`, and the day a composed tree reaches publish that failure lands on the last screen with an editor to tell. `DOCUMENT_CONTENT_GAP_TYPE` is a constant at both ends and `conformance.test.ts` asserts it is registered — `SectionRenderer` renders **nothing** for an unknown type outside development, so a typo would silently restore the exact bug the block removes.

  ⚠️ **The guard that is easy to delete: `applyTemplate` is called only when the area actually holds a marker.** It returns its second argument for a tree with none — the right answer for a page (render it unframed) and precisely the wrong one here, where it would replace a `header` area's blocks with the stand-in content. The E2E asserts the *absence* of the gap band on a marker-less area for this reason.

  **Verified against the live project, with the probe template kept `draft` on purpose.** `publishedTemplateArea` filters on `status = 'published'`, so a draft cannot reach the production site no matter what it is assigned to — which made an entry assignment to `home` safe to create and delete. What the probe showed: no assignment → the band; the assignment → the homepage's real sections spliced in and the bar reading "Framing: Home"; `?area=footer` → the footer area with **no** band; a trimmed or bogus `?area=` → back to the marker's area. All probe rows deleted, counts confirmed at baseline.

  ⚠️ **One locator risk was settled by measurement rather than by argument.** The area chips are uppercased in CSS, and `getByRole("link", { name: "body", exact: true })` is case-*sensitive* — the shape of bug that has cost this repo several CI runs. Driving a fixture page through Playwright showed `text-transform` does not reach the accessible name in Chromium (`"body"` matches, `"BODY"` does not), so the spec's locators stand. **A five-line fixture is cheaper than a CI round**, and it is available in a container that cannot run the suite itself.

  ⚠️ **A pre-existing crash found in passing and deliberately not fixed here.** A `masthead` node whose `settings` lack its array field throws `Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'map')` and takes the render down, rather than degrading the way `SectionRenderer`'s header promises ("content that fails its schema renders with its props untouched"). It surfaced only because the first probe payload was hand-written; publish validation refuses that shape, so nothing an editor does reaches it. Recorded rather than repaired — it is a manifest-default question across every block with an array field, not a preview one.

- Phase 0 setup: generated & committed `starter/package-lock.json` (was missing) so Railway's `npm ci` build succeeds.
- **Homepage exact-match pass (vs the bundled `Modern Gentlemen Homepage` prototype).** Text-run geometry (box, font, weight, tracking, colour) now matches the prototype at 1440 / 1024 / 909 / 768 / 414 / 375 in both themes. What it took, and what it did NOT match:
  - `html` gained `scrollbar-gutter: stable` + `overflow-x: clip` + `overscroll-behavior: none` + the theme background, and `body` gained `max-width: 100vw`. The gutter is load-bearing: without it the 1320px column centres on 60px gutters instead of the prototype's 52.5px, shifting every section 7.5px.
  - `text-rendering: geometricPrecision` on `body`. Hinted advances were inflating small text ~1px/glyph (~8% at 12px), which re-wrapped every dek and pull-paragraph off the design's line breaks. Space Grotesk also moved to the variable font (dropped the explicit `weight` array).
  - Hero rebuilt as the prototype's real default — `heroVariant: 'Cover bottom'` at `heroSize: 'Full screen'`: 100vh full-bleed cover, ONE bottom-rising scrim (no left-to-right wash, no 1px divider), card at left 44 / bottom 72, and the issue meta + vertical SCROLL rail as separate bottom-corner rails. The cover is now the prototype's **video** with the still as its poster.
  - Chrome: nav zone at `top:-2px` (content centres on y=34.5), `color:#fff`, burger 24×22 flush to the gutter with a 4px gap to the logo, icon buttons 38px and fully transparent (`iconBubbles` is off), half-filled-circle theme glyph, frost on hover as well as scroll, 0.45s 'Frost only' timing, hide-on-scroll thresholds 90/4. Mega-menu panel moved into flow with its own 1320 cap.
  - Added `--mg-muted` / `--mg-faint` / `--mg-band-border` tokens (the prototype's `tMuted` / `tFaint` / `bandBorder`). Section eyebrows are muted grey on light bands, not accent red; inset dark bands take a hairline only in dark mode.
  - `line-height` was the single biggest source of drift — the prototype leaves it `normal` in most chrome and card text where Tailwind imposes a fixed value. Where a size had to win over `Eyebrow`'s own class, the override is `!`-prefixed.
  - Breakpoints are now the prototype's (**680 / 820 / 1024**), replacing the invented ones: cards 6-up ≥1025 → 2-up ≤1024 → 1-up ≤680 (both The Latest and MG Film), two-up ≤820, section inset 22px / nav 20px ≤680.
  - Tile min-heights are the prototype's **content-box** 210px (150px 6-up) converted to border-box per tile: 268 padded+ruled, 266 padded, 210 plain. Missing this made every tile 58px short and dragged the whole page up 115px below the grid.
  - **Deliberately NOT reproduced (prototype defects):** its footer nav overflows the content column at ~768px, and its newsletter SUBSCRIBE button is pushed off-edge and clipped below ~400px (the app keeps `min-w-0` on the input so the button stays visible — this is the only remaining diff at 375/414). Also unmatched: a 2px vertical offset on the decorative ▶ glyph, whose line box comes from Arial in the prototype (an unstyled `<button>`) and Space Grotesk in the app — same fallback glyph either way.
  - **Header bag icon — user decision:** the homepage prototype and `handoff/screenshots/homepage-desktop.png` show 2 icons (search · theme) while `MG Header.dc.html` / `04_CHROME.md` specify 3 (with bag). Resolved as **bag on the store journey only** (`/shop`, `/product/*`, `/bag`, `/checkout`); editorial routes match the upload exactly. `BagDrawer` is gated on the same predicate so it can't outlive its trigger.
  - ⚠️ The hero cover and film previews now point at the prototype's **third-party placeholder videos** (see `starter/lib/media.ts`) — a studio trailer on a publisher CDN and a CC Wikimedia clip. They reproduce the design's moving cover but MUST be swapped for owned footage before launch (tracked under Phase 7 "Production imagery/video rights").
  - Seeded `speed-considered` as a real article so the hero CTA resolves (the prototype links to `MG Article.dc.html?a=speed-considered`).
- **Chrome follow-up pass — overlays, burger, hide-on-scroll, sharp corners.**
  - **Search overlay rebuilt** to the prototype: SEARCH / ESC rail (centred ≤680), red 26px magnifier, fluid `clamp(22px,6.5vw,34px)` field with the round clear button, POPULAR SEARCHES chips when empty, and EDITORIAL / STORE result groups with 64px thumbs (52px ≤680), per-group counts, hairline rules and row hover. Search index is now the prototype's 13-entry `searchIndex()` verbatim; results resolve to real routes. Scrolling the list blurs the field (phone keyboard), Esc and a >450ms-guarded scrim click close, and the query resets on close. **0 text-run mismatches** empty and with results.
  - **Drawer rebuilt** to the prototype: `sidebarEntry: 'Fade'` panel entry, EST. 2026 + serif tagline block, numbered accordion whose sub-lists are the mega-menu columns flattened (Curtain in on a per-link stagger, Collapse out over 300ms), secondary mono links carrying the same grow-underline, pinned BECOME A MEMBER and the FOLLOW ring row. **0 mismatches** closed and expanded.
  - **Mega-menu rebuilt**: 2-up grid columns (not a flex row), 500-weight 17px links with the translate-x hover, and the feature card as a background-image block with its own hover lift. **0 mismatches.**
  - **Burger hover** = `burgerHover: 'Staircase'`: the two 6px squares stretch to 12px and 17px bars on the springy .3s curve. The mark does **not** scale — the prototype's generic `[data-burger]:hover{scale(1.06)}` is overridden inside the nav by its header-scale rule.
  - **Hide-on-scroll fixed on mobile.** `navHover` was being set by hovering anywhere in the nav zone, so a stationary cursor at the top of the screen — or a tap on a touch device, which fires mouseenter but often no mouseleave — pinned the bar open and frosted for the rest of the session, silently disabling hide-on-scroll. Now scoped to nav *links* and the open panel exactly as the prototype's `_megaOver` does (hovering the logo/icons/gaps unfrosts but never closes an open panel — that gap bridges the nav and the dropdown), plus the prototype's document-level pointerdown guard. Verified step-for-step against the prototype at 375 and 1440.
  - **Sharp corners applied site-wide** (`sharpCorners: true` is the prototype's chosen default, and CLAUDE.md calls for minimal radius): every non-circular radius is now 0 across all 9 routes — article/category cards, membership tiers and billing toggle, CTA bands, spec tables, hairline grids, search chips, the bag count badge. Genuine circles keep 50%, matching the prototype's exception list (icon buttons, play buttons, socials, `data-circle`). Swept and confirmed clean on `/ /style /about /membership /shop /bag /checkout /article/* /product/*`.
  - Along the way: several elements were sized against the prototype's **content-box** boxes (it has no global `box-sizing` reset, so only form controls are border-box). Corrected the drawer panel (380 content → 471 rendered), mega-menu cap (1320 → 1416) and feature card (340 → 342), footer/drawer social rings, the search ESC ring, and the search input's UA 1px/2px padding.
  - Focus traps moved out of the removed `OverlayScrim` into a shared `lib/useFocusTrap.ts`, so the overlays keep their own prototype markup without losing WCAG behaviour.

- `[data-darkband]` now pins the full dark token set (`--mg-bg/--mg-surface/--mg-accent-serif`) so shared token-driven components (e.g. `Button`) render correctly on dark bands.
- Hero renders the cover **image** (`hero-cover.jpg`) — no licensed video asset yet; imperative muted autoplay + IntersectionObserver is wired for when a `videoUrl` is supplied.
- Footer socials use text letter-marks (I / X / Y / in) pending real brand icon SVGs.

- `formatGBP` de-duplicated — `CartProvider` re-exports catalog's, so shipping now formats as **£4.95** (was rounding to £5).

- PDP not-found is a **styled client fallback** (pixel-matches the reference PNG) rather than server `notFound()`, since the page stays client for `useCart`; real-404 status deferred to SEO/Track B.
- Checkout payment is **demo-only** (no card charged); real Stripe checkout + the `cart.checkoutUrl()` seam is Track B.

- Category demo articles live in `lib/editorial.ts` (verbatim from `MG Category.dc.html`, all 5 categories); `culture`/`film` have no store products so category pages are **article-driven**, not `productRow`. Article cards link `/article/{slugify(title)}` (Article page still scaffold).
- Category page uses server `notFound()` for unknown slugs (real 404) — cleaner than the PDP's styled client fallback, since the category page is a server component (no `useCart`).
- Editorial mono hero-eyebrows/kickers/tags are the bright accent **#ff4d5e** (hardcoded, matching the prototypes/screenshots), not the light-theme `--mg-accent-serif` (#c8102e).
- About/Membership sections use the prototypes' **22px** side gutters (via inline `paddingInline: max(22px, calc((100% - W)/2))` for W = 1320/1180/900/820), not `.container-mg`'s 48px (which Category correctly uses, matching its prototype).
- Membership polished **in place** as a bespoke `"use client"` page (billing toggle drives all 3 tiers); featured "MEMBER" CTA keeps the `useCart().setMember(true)` seam (15% off store-wide). Copy fix: tiers Reader/Debrief/Concierge → **Reader/Member/Patron**, "save 25%" → **SAVE 20%**.
- `ctaBand` is one block with 3 variants (`split` Category newsletter / `centered` Membership join / `link` About join) + a `gutter` prop (48 default, 22 for About/Membership); CTAs are dark pills (`#0d0d0d`), not the red `Button`. Email/newsletter submits stay demo-only (label flips to ✓).

New namespace `components/article/*` (dispatcher `ArticleHero`/`ArticleBody` + inline variants — kept separate from `components/sections/` because `Timeline`/`Manifesto`/`Interview` already exist there with different designs). Data + template map in `lib/articles.ts`; body content is fixed per body-variant (lives in the components), matching the prototype's own model.
- Article stubs seeded from `lib/editorial.ts` titles/slugs (35 category links) + a canonical showcase per template (20), so every template is reachable and every category link resolves; unknown slug → `notFound()`. `assignTemplate` replicates the prototype `defaultTplFor` (explicit map + hash), **fixing the prototype's apostrophe-slug bug** (`the-coachbuilder-s-floor`, `inside-the-watchmaker-s-bench`).
- Article kicker uses each template's **semantic category** (The Debrief / Motoring / Watches…) rather than the prototype preview's blanket `category="Culture"` default — so the Letter kicker reads `THE DEBRIEF · NO. 042` vs the PNG's `CULTURE · NO. 042` (the only diff from the one article screenshot; consistent across all 20 templates). Only 1 of 20 templates has a reference PNG; the other 19 are **prototype-verified** (diffed against `MG Article.dc.html`).
- Article "Video" hero (Film Feature) falls back to its **poster image** (no external/licensed video asset) — same stance as the homepage hero; a self-hosted clip supplied via `DEFAULT_HERO_VIDEO`/per-article `videoUrl` enables playback. Reading-progress bar reuses the global reduced-motion rule (no per-component gate needed). KEEP READING related is category-aware (3 same-category siblings, falls back to any).

- Phase 7 responsive pass (subagent-audited, mobile-only): (1) **Search overlay** — dropped the illegal `container-mg` + `max-w-3xl` combo (CLAUDE.md forbids both on one element) for `mx-auto max-w-3xl px-6 sm:px-8`, and added `min-w-0` to the input (its intrinsic ~20ch width at `text-3xl` was forcing horizontal overflow). (2) **`.container-mg`** gets a `padding-inline: 24px` at ≤680px so phone content isn't over-inset by the flat 48px gutter. (3) **Spec-comparison table** (`BodySpec`) switched `overflow-hidden`→`overflow-x-auto` with `minmax()` columns so it scrolls within its card instead of crushing/overflowing the page ≤400px. (4) **Newsletter + CTA-band forms** stack (`flex-col`) with `min-w-0` inputs below ~360px instead of overflowing. (5) **Touch targets** bumped to ≥40–44px (WCAG 2.5.8): header burger 14px→44px, icon buttons 38px→40px, drawer/bag close 32–36px→40px, qty stepper `py-2`→`py-2.5`. (6) **Bag line items** — smaller image + `min-w-0` content + `shrink-0` line-total + `flex-wrap` qty row so the price stays visible ≤468px. (7) Article dek/`CtaBand`/`FeaturedLead` headings and the drop-cap get `min-[681px]:` clamps; grid gap breakpoints normalized `min-[1025px]`→`min-[1024px]`. **Desktop (≥909px) is unchanged** — every fix is gated behind a mobile breakpoint. Two benign residuals left as-is: a 26px phantom scrollWidth on the 768px PDP (masked by `overflow-x:clip`, no visible cut, no scrollbar) and the MOST-POPULAR tier notch reading red-on-red at narrow widths (by design).

- **Track A Phase 7 (SEO) — decisions.**
  - **The site went public before it was indexable.** One `metadata` export covered every route, there was no sitemap, no `robots.txt`, no canonical tags and no structured data — a finished storefront search engines could not read properly. `lib/domain/seo.ts` is the pure half: `pageTitle` (idempotent, so content mentioning the brand cannot produce "Modern Gentlemen — Modern Gentlemen"), `metaDescription` (cut at a word boundary, no ellipsis on text that was never cut), `canonicalUrl`, and Product/Article/Organization JSON-LD builders. 19 tests.
  - **`canonicalUrl(base, "/")` returns the bare origin, and that is load-bearing.** A trailing double slash is a *different URL* to a crawler, which would split the homepage's ranking between two addresses.
  - **JSON-LD prices come from integer pence through `penceToPounds().toFixed(2)`, never `formatPence`.** The latter gives "£145.00"; schema.org wants a bare number with the currency in its own field, and a currency symbol inside `price` is the commonest reason a product feed is rejected. `Article` rather than `NewsArticle`, which carries expectations about timeliness and corrections this magazine does not meet.
  - **The sitemap is built from the same public services the pages read**, so an entry cannot outlive its content: 78 URLs today (4 static, 5 categories, 53 articles, 16 products). `robots.ts` disallows `/admin`, `/api`, `/preview`, `/bag` and `/checkout` — **`/preview` is the one that matters**, because those links render a document's *draft* to whoever holds the token and get pasted into Slack; a crawler indexing one would publish unpublished work. `/sign-in` is deliberately left indexable.
  - **⚠️ `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` now fails a production build when unset, deliberately.** The first build here emitted `<loc>http://localhost:3000</loc>` — harmless locally, ruinous live, and worse once canonical tags follow: a canonical pointing at localhost tells Google every page's real address is a machine it cannot reach, which is a slow deindexing nobody connects to a deploy. `canonicalSiteUrl()` in `lib/db/env.ts` refuses the fallback in production. `siteUrl()` keeps its localhost default for preview links, which are clicked by a human in their own browser and outlive nothing.
  - **Four of the routes needing metadata are `"use client"`, so their head lives in a `layout.tsx`.** `/shop`, `/membership`, `/bag`, `/checkout` and the PDP all hold state — a filter driven by `useSearchParams`, a billing toggle, a cart, a four-step form, a gallery — and **Next reads `metadata` / `generateMetadata` only from server modules**. The alternative was splitting each page into a server shell around a client body. The layout wins for a reason that outranks taste: **these pages are pixel-verified**, and a sibling layout adds a `<head>` and a `<script>` without touching a line of them. A script tag has no box, so the baselines cannot notice.
  - **`/shop`'s canonical is `/shop` unconditionally — the one decision here worth defending.** The category filter writes `?cat=Grooming` into the URL, so the store has as many addresses as it has categories, every one serving the same page with a subset of the same products. Left alone a crawler treats each as a separate page and splits the store's ranking across six near-duplicates. It is a static `metadata` export rather than `generateMetadata` precisely so it *cannot* vary with the query. Verified on the built server: `/shop?cat=Grooming` returns `<link rel="canonical" href="…/shop">`.
  - **`schemaAvailability` maps four values, not a boolean — the first version of this file was wrong.** It took `inStock: boolean` and said guessing a fulfilment story would be inventing a claim the catalogue does not make. **The catalogue does make it**: `products.availability` has carried a CHECK over `in_stock`/`out_of_stock`/`preorder`/`discontinued` since `0005`. Collapsing that to in-stock-or-not states something false to a shopping feed — `OutOfStock` means "come back later", `Discontinued` means "do not", and `PreOrder` means "buy it now, it ships later". A test iterates `PRODUCT_AVAILABILITIES` so a fifth value added by a migration fails typecheck on the switch rather than silently falling through.
  - **`getPublishedProductSeo` is a separate read, not a `find` over the catalogue.** It fetches one row for one page's head, and it carries `price_pence` and `availability` — neither of which `Product` has, since `price` on it is *pounds* and it has no availability at all. `Product` is the pixel-verified store contract; widening it to feed a meta tag would be the tail wagging the dog. **Pence stays pence all the way to `productJsonLd`.** An integration test asserts the two reads agree field for field, because the failure mode is not a crash — it is a `<head>` quietly advertising a different price from the page below it, which Google treats as a manipulation signal.
  - **`categories.intro` became the category meta description, and needed its own test.** Nothing else reads that column, so a read that silently dropped it would leave five landing pages with no description and no visible symptom. The canonical is `publicPathForCategory(doc.slug)`, **not the requested path** — `getPublishedCategory` lowercases before it queries, so `/Style` and `/STYLE` both resolve, and echoing the request back would hand a crawler three URLs for one page, each claiming to be the original.
  - **`/sign-in` stays crawlable, and the earlier note in `robots.ts` had the reasoning backwards.** The page has set `robots: { index: false }` since Track A. **A disallowed URL is never fetched, so its noindex is never read** — disallowing it would make indexing *more* likely, not less, because a crawler that finds the link elsewhere may list the bare URL it was forbidden to look at. Allowing the crawl is what lets the page say "do not index me". Same reason `/bag` and `/checkout` carry a meta `noindex` *as well as* a disallow: the disallow is the cheap first line, the meta tag is what survives being linked to from outside. `/bag` is `noindex, follow` and `/checkout` is `noindex, nofollow` — the bag links to the shop and to every product in it, and wasting those links buys nothing.
  - **JSON-LD escapes `<` to `<` before it reaches `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`.** A product blurb containing the literal text `</script>` would otherwise close the element early and spill the payload into the document as markup. JSON treats the two as the same character, so every consumer reads the same value; only the HTML parser is fooled. The content is editor-supplied, which is what makes this the injection that matters.
  - **⚠️ No `datePublished` on Article JSON-LD, and this is a known gap rather than an oversight.** `articles.published_at` exists, but `ResolvedArticle` does not carry it, and adding it would break the whole-object deep-compare in `publicEditorial.test.ts` — which compares the service's output against `lib/demo/articles.ts`, a fixture that cannot know a timestamp the publish action writes. Threading the date through means giving the fixture a way to express "whatever the publish wrote", which is a real change to how that test works. Inventing a date to fill the field would be a false claim about when something was written, so the field is absent instead.
  - **The canonical domain is the Railway subdomain, and that is a decision with a follow-up attached.** Asked and answered before merging: canonicals, the sitemap, `robots.txt`'s `Host` and every JSON-LD `url` now declare `modern-gentlemen-website-production.up.railway.app` to be each page's true address. Moving to a custom domain later is **one Railway variable and a redeploy** — `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` is the single source for all of them, which is the whole reason `canonicalUrl` takes a base rather than reading the environment itself. What is *not* free is the indexing that happens in between: pages indexed under the railway domain will want 301s to their new addresses. The longer the gap, the more there is to redirect.
  - **Verified on the live deploy, not only in CI** — a stronger check, because CI only ever proves its own throwaway stack while this proves the rows actually in production. After `0555031` deployed (~100s): `robots.txt` serving its five disallows with an absolute `Host` and `Sitemap`; `sitemap.xml` at **78 URLs** (16 products, 53 articles, 5 categories, 4 static) built from the live database; absolute canonicals on `/`, `/style` and `/shop`; **`/shop?cat=Grooming` collapsing to `/shop`**, which is the duplicate-content fix doing its job against real query strings; `noindex, follow` on `/bag` and `noindex, nofollow` on `/checkout`; and all three JSON-LD types present, with the PDP emitting `"price":"145.00"` alongside a separate `priceCurrency` and fully absolute image URLs.
  - **Also observed, not acted on: `/product/[slug]` is the only public route still `ƒ (Dynamic)`.** It has no `generateStaticParams`, so product pages server-render on demand while categories and articles prerender. Confirmed as pre-existing by building the branch point — **the new layout changed no route's rendering mode.** Adding the 16 slugs would prerender them like the rest; it is a separate change and out of this phase's scope. (**Done later — see "The public-site correctness trio" in the decisions log.** The route is `●` with 16 prerendered slugs.)
  - **⚠️ The guard fired on CI before it ever fired on a deploy, and the lesson is a third instance of one this file already records twice.** `canonicalSiteUrl()` refuses its localhost fallback when `NODE_ENV === "production"` — and **`next build` sets that on a runner and a laptop exactly as it does on Railway.** CI run #55 died on `Failed to collect page data for /_not-found` (the root layout's `metadataBase` is the first thing to ask for a canonical URL). The local build had passed only because a gitignored `.env.local` happened to carry the variable, so the guard had been verified against the one environment where it could not fire. Two declarations were missing at once: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` never set it — now at the **top level**, not a ninth per-step `env:` block, so a step added later cannot forget it — and **`.env.example` never declared it either. Third instance of "a variable nothing reads is a variable nobody notices is undeclared"**, after `JOBS_SECRET` and `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`. The error message named only Railway, which sent the first reader to the wrong dashboard; it now names all three places and starts with the `Missing environment variable` prefix the jobs route keys its 503-vs-500 answer on.
  - **`canonicalSiteUrl()` had no tests, which is why this surfaced three CI jobs deep instead of in the unit suite.** Six added — and they caught a bug in their own helper on the first run: **`process.env.X = undefined` stores the string `"undefined"`**, which is truthy, so the guard never fired and two tests passed vacuously. Unsetting an environment variable means `delete`. Worth remembering the next time a test reaches for `process.env`.
  - **Verification honesty — the SEO phase.** Ran here, all green: the four gates (**639 unit tests**, up from 610), `npm run build`, and **all 16 visual baselines pixel-identical** — the gate that proves the metadata and the two JSON-LD blocks moved nothing. The build's route table was compared against a build of the branch point: every public route kept its mode (`/` ○, `/[category]` ●, `/article/[slug]` ●, `/about`·`/shop`·`/membership`·`/bag`·`/checkout` ○, `/sitemap.xml`·`/robots.txt` ○) with the 1h revalidate intact. Spot-checked on the built server rather than assumed: titles, descriptions and canonicals on eight routes, `noindex` on bag and checkout, the `?cat=` canonical collapse, Organization JSON-LD on the homepage, Product JSON-LD emitting `"price":"145.00"` with `priceCurrency` in its own field and absolute image URLs, Article JSON-LD with its author and image, `robots.txt`'s five disallows, and a 78-URL sitemap. **Not run: the integration suite** — the three new `getPublishedProductSeo` assertions and the category `intro` assertion need a seeded stack this container cannot provision, so CI is their first execution.
- **Phase 6b (scheduled publishing) — decisions.**
  - **A background job cannot use the ordinary publish path, and the reason is one line deep.** `publish_document` asserts `has_permission(<type>.publish)`; `has_permission` resolves through `auth.uid()`; **`auth.uid()` is null for the service-role key.** A job holding that key is not an admin, it is nobody, so every call would raise `42501`. There is no session to borrow — a scheduled publish happens precisely when nobody is signed in. Hence `run_due_publishes` in `0016`, **the second `security definer` function in the codebase** after `resolve_preview`, which this file had described as deliberately the only one.
  - **The capability is narrow by construction, not by promise.** Granted to `service_role` alone and revoked from `public`, `anon` and `authenticated`, so nothing reachable with a browser key can call it — asserted in the integration test for both anon and a signed-in editor. And it can only publish rows already at `status='scheduled'` with a past `scheduled_for`: to abuse it you would first have to schedule the document, which goes through `schedule_document`, which *does* check the permission. **The authorisation happens at scheduling time**, which is also why the runner writes a null actor. `publish_events.actor_id` and `revisions.created_by` were nullable all along; this is the actorless case they were left open for.
  - **The write sequence is duplicated on purpose, and a test holds the two together.** Sharing a body between `publish_document` (invoker, runs as the caller) and the runner (definer, runs as the owner) would require a shared function executable by `authenticated` — which is an unchecked publish handed to every signed-in user. So `tests/integration/scheduledPublishing.test.ts` publishes one page each way and asserts the row, the revision and the event match **but for the actor**. Behavioural equivalence rather than shared code, checked every CI run. If someone changes what publishing means and updates one side only, it fails.
  - **`FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, because overlapping runs are ordinary.** A five-minute cron and a run that outlasts its interval is the usual way this breaks; skip-locked means a second runner steps over what the first has claimed instead of publishing it twice. The test asserts a second run is a no-op.
  - **The runner returns `category_slug`, which is not decoration.** Publishing an article changes two pages — its own, and the category page whose lead and grid are bound to `articles`. The admin's `publicPathsForArticle` resolves that pair through a service that reads the caller's session, and a job has none, so the SQL returns the slug instead and `pathsToRevalidate` stays pure. Revalidating only the article is the failure that looks like success: live at its URL, missing from its section.
  - **The clock lives in GitHub Actions**, every five minutes. Railway has no cron; the alternatives were a third-party pinger (another account, another secret, invisible from the repo) or `pg_cron` in Supabase (closer to the data, but needing its own copy of the URL and secret, and firing even when the site is down). **Five minutes is the resolution of the whole feature** — 09:00 means somewhere in 09:00–09:05, and Actions treats cron as best-effort under load, so that is a floor rather than a promise. The builder's help text now says so instead of the old line about nothing firing it.
  - **⚠️ `JOBS_SECRET` was missing from `.env.example` entirely.** This file has said for four phases that it was "an env var no code reads", which was true, and the natural assumption — that it was at least declared — was not: anyone copying `.env.example` to `.env.local` would have got a route that refuses every request, with a 503 that names the variable but no example line to copy. Added. **The general shape: a variable nothing reads is a variable nobody notices is undeclared.**
  - **Verification honesty — scheduled publishing.** Ran here: the four gates (**610 unit tests**, up from 597, 13 new over `lib/domain/jobs.ts`) and `npm run build`, which registered `/api/jobs/publish-scheduled` as dynamic and left all 70 pages prerendering. **Not run: anything against a database.** `0016` is unapplied everywhere, `tests/integration/scheduledPublishing.test.ts` has never executed, and no scheduled document has ever fired. CI applies the migration to its own stack and runs that suite, so the PR's checks are the first real test — the same position the navigation phase was in, and it found three defects there.
- **Phase 6b (navigation) — decisions.**
  - **The chrome was the last hardcoded thing on the public site, and it is now one tree.** `header-primary`'s top level is the header nav; each entry's children are the mega-menu, grouped into columns; the drawer renders those same children flattened. `Drawer.tsx` has claimed since Track A that it "can never drift apart" from the mega-menu — that was true only because both lists had been typed the same way by hand, and it is now true by construction. Four menus in total: the two `0007` seeded plus `footer-legal` and `drawer-secondary`, because leaving two of the chrome's four link lists in the components would have made "navigation is editable" half-true in the way the next session discovers the hard way.
  - **A column heading is not a row, it is `options.group` on the child.** `menu_item_target_shape` refuses a row holding neither a `url` nor a `target_id`, and a heading is neither — modelling headings as items would mean writing `url: "#"` to satisfy the constraint. A lie in the data, bought to gain a shape the renderer does not need. The renderer groups by the string, preserving first-appearance order.
  - **Labels are stored in title case, and the header uppercases in CSS.** The header's constants were literal `"STYLE"` while the drawer showed `"Style"`; one tree cannot hold both, and storing the header's form would have visibly changed the drawer. `text-transform: uppercase` emits identical glyphs — the footer's nav already did exactly this. **This is the one markup change in the chrome**, two `uppercase` classes, and the 16 visual baselines are what settle whether it was free.
  - **`visibility` is parsed, stored and deliberately not applied at render.** Honouring `auth` or `member` means reading the session, and any route awaiting `cookies()` is opted out of static rendering — the chrome is on every public page, so applying it there would cost the *whole site* its static build with no error and no failing test. The admin says so in the dialog rather than pretending. Same stance the builder already takes with `visibility.devices`.
  - **A menu is not a document, so an edit is live.** `menus` has `status` and `version` but no `draft_data`/`published_data`, and `document_table()` does not list `'menu'`. Rather than invent a second publishing path, item writes go straight to the rows and `revalidatePath("/", "layout")` publishes them — the layout is what renders the chrome, so revalidating a single path would leave the other sixty-four pages serving the old nav for an hour. Making menus properly publishable is a schema change and belongs on its own.
  - **⚠️ `0007` left `menu_items` publicly readable regardless of its menu's status, and `0015` fixes it.** The policy was `using (true)` while `menus` was gated on `status = 'published' or is_staff()` — so a draft menu's row was hidden and every one of its labels was readable by anon. An unreleased navigation was enumerable with one request. Harmless while both tables held nothing; this phase is what arms it. The integration test asserts it against the *policy* rather than the service, because `getPublishedMenu` returns `[]` either way and would have proved nothing.
  - **A link resolves to its target's slug at read time.** That is what `link_type` + `target_id` are for, and it means renaming a category's slug moves every menu entry pointing at it. `collection` is in the CHECK, has no public route, and is therefore deliberately absent from the admin's picker — offering it would only let an editor build a dead link. Targets resolve through one query per table used, not one per item, and the lookups run as `anon`, so an item pointing at a *draft* page finds no slug and is dropped rather than published as a link the public cannot follow.
  - **Ordering is position, then label, then id.** `position` defaults to 0, so a menu inserted by hand has every row tied — and Phase 7c already recorded what an untied ordering costs when two articles shared an issue number. `id` is the final tie-break because it is the one value always present and always distinct.
  - **The seeder replaces a menu's items rather than upserting them.** `menu_items` has no natural key — two entries may legitimately share a label under different parents — so there is nothing for `onConflict` to key on. A re-seed therefore discards navigation an editor has changed. Harmless on a fresh database and in CI, which is what the script is for; the same caveat the category and product documents already carry, and worth knowing before running it against a project someone is editing.
  - **Verification honesty — Phase 6b, and what settled it.** Ran locally: the four gates — `format:check`, `lint`, `typecheck` and **597 unit tests** (up from 577, 20 of them new over `lib/domain/navigation.ts`). Everything requiring a database was left to CI, because every Supabase MCP call in the authoring session — including read-only ones — returned "requires approval", so nothing could be applied or seeded here and the build fails by design without a published `header-primary`. **CI run #54 (PR #20) is green on all three jobs**, and it is what proves this phase: all fifteen migrations applied and then replayed idempotently with `0015` among them, `seedMenus()` wrote its rows, `publicNavigation.test.ts` deep-compared the resolved menus against the fixture, `npm run build` prerendered against a database, the E2E suite passed, and — **the 16 public visual baselines passed with the chrome rendering from Supabase.** That last one is the answer to the open question this phase carried: the two `uppercase` classes are pixel-free, demonstrated rather than argued.

    Then the live project was seeded by hand, and **the build and all 16 baselines were re-run against `qnfoztnyxhubnnulpfwt` itself** — a stronger check than CI can make, because CI proves the seeder and the render path agree on *its own* stack while this proves the rows actually in production do. Confirmed there: every public route still `○ (Static)` / `● (SSG)` with the 1h revalidate (a flip to `ƒ` would have meant the navigation read picked up a cookie), the header's six labels and hrefs resolved from `menu_items`, the footer's nav and legal rows likewise, and the mega-menu's columns, feature cards and the drawer's flattened sub-links all present in the client payload. **Nothing about this phase is now unproven.**
  - **The CI run (PR #20, run #53): two jobs green, and every failure was the spec's fault.** `Lint/typecheck/unit` passed; `Integration` passed, which means `supabase start` applied all fifteen migrations, the idempotency replay survived `0015`, `seedMenus()` wrote its rows and `publicNavigation.test.ts` deep-compared them against the fixture. The E2E job went red — **34 passed, 1 failed, 1 flaky, 1 did not run** — on three locator mistakes in `tests/e2e/navigation.spec.ts`, none of them an application bug. (1) **`"E2E Column"` was a literal where every other identifier was minted per run.** Playwright retried the test, each attempt added another child carrying that heading, and the assertion found three. Phase 7c already recorded that retries leave the failed attempt's rows in the database; the lesson was applied to the labels and missed on the column. **Any string a test writes to the database is a per-run identifier, without exception.** (2) **`getByText(/sub-link/)` can never be unique here** — the confirmation says "Its N sub-links will go with it" and every entry behind it carries an "Add sub-link" button. (3) **`.last()` is not a way to find a dialog's button.** Every row has a Delete; `.last()` depended on where the dialog mounts in the DOM. All three are now scoped to `getByRole("dialog", { name: … })`, which is what "exact" should have meant.
  - **⚠️ A broken E2E spec silently cost the visual gate, and the workflow now prevents it.** `Visual regression` carried no `if: always()`, so the E2E step's non-zero exit **skipped the 16 baselines entirely** — a chrome change reached review with its single most important gate unexecuted, and the job's red tick gave no hint which of the two had actually failed. The step is now `if: always()`. The two answer different questions and the baselines are the more expensive answer to lose.
  - **The most valuable test in the file ran last, behind a credential gate it did not need.** "The public header renders the seeded menu" is the assertion the whole phase rests on, needs no session to make it, and sat inside a `serial` group gated on `E2E_ADMIN_*` — so a failure five tests earlier meant it never ran at all. It is now its own ungated `describe`. **Order a spec by what a failure would cost, not by the story it tells.**
  - **The credentials lesson, corrected in both handoff files.** They both said placeholder values were enough for the visual suite because "public routes render from demo modules and never reach the database". That stopped being true at Phase 7a, when the site layout began reading `products` on every route — with placeholders the layout throws and all 16 baselines fail. The advice had turned into a trap. What does work, and is what this session used: the project URL and the **publishable anon key**, both readable from the Supabase MCP. ⚠️ **Since the SEO phase a third is required — `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL`** — so "two are enough" is now itself the stale version of this correction. Only the E2E suite and the seeder need the service-role key.
- **Phase 7c (the editorial migration) — decisions.**
  - **The binding source could never have lived where this file kept promising.** `sources/demo.ts`'s header, the "Next phase" list and Phase 2's notes all said `lib/blocks/sources/supabase.ts`. ESLint bars `lib/blocks/**` from importing `@/lib/db/*` — that is what makes it a pure leaf — and the rule's own message states the resolution: *binding sources are injected*. It is `lib/services/bindingSources.ts`. **Nothing in `binding.ts` changed to allow it**, which is the evidence the Phase 2 design was right; only the filename this file repeated was wrong. The two sources' row shapes are kept identical field for field, so a `$bind` descriptor means the same thing against either.
  - **A category page is a document whose listing is bound, not stored.** The four blocks live in `categories.published_data`; the hero and newsletter are literal, the lead and grid are `$bind` descriptors over the `articles` table. Seeding literals would have passed the same integration test and then silently frozen: publishing an article would never reach the page. The test therefore asserts **both** — that the resolved props equal the demo composition, *and* that what is stored is still a descriptor.
  - **`offset` was added to the binding query, and the category page is why.** The lead takes the newest story and the grid takes the next six from the same ordering. Without an offset the grid's `limit` starts at the same row and the newest story appears twice — as the lead and again as the grid's first card. Optional and non-negative, so no existing descriptor changes meaning; `0` is permitted and means absent, so a control writing zero is not an error.
  - **The twenty template showcases are filed under nothing, and so is the cover story.** Several carry display labels matching a real category — two are "Culture" at issue `042`, the *same issue as the Culture lead*. Filed, they tie in the `issue desc` ordering the page reads and can displace the lead, non-deterministically. Unfiled, each category holds exactly seven stories with no ties. This is also the faithful reading: the demo category pages never listed them either. **A tie in an ordering is a bug that only shows up sometimes** — worth remembering before adding a "newest N" query anywhere else.
  - **A card's tag is its subcategory; the kicker is its category.** The grid prints "TAILORING · 040" while the article's own rail prints "STYLE · NO. 040", and a category name alone cannot produce the first. The subcategory is seeded as a **tag** (`tags` + `article_tags`, both empty until now) and the source falls back to the category name when an article has none — which is exactly what each category's lead needs. This is what gave `tags` its first rows and its first purpose.
  - **The display label is stored on every article's payload, filed or not.** Only the thirty-five filed articles have a category row to derive a label from, and the showcases print "The Debrief", "Motoring", "Opinion" — labels no category row has. Storing it uniformly avoids a rule with an exception; the join remains the fallback, so an article created in the admin still prints its category.
  - **KEEP READING is seeded into `article_relations` rather than derived.** The demo picks three same-category siblings *in module insertion order*, which no column reproduces — and `/article/speed-considered` has a visual baseline, so "close enough" would have been a failing screenshot. The curated trio is exact; the category-derived fallback stays behind it for articles nobody has curated. `article_relations` had carried a schema and zero rows since `0004`.
  - **Publishing an article now changes two pages.** Its own, and the category page whose listing is bound to it. `revalidatePublicArticle` does both, and the metadata save collects paths on **both sides of the write**, because re-filing an article from Watches to Culture changes two listings and only the before-set knows about the first. The products equivalent — publish revalidated, delete did not — was caught by a visual baseline coming back one grid row too tall; this one was written that way from the start.
  - **Verification honesty — Phase 7c. Every check ran, and two real defects were caught by them.** The four gates (**577 unit tests**, up from 536), the integration deep-compare, `npm run build` and all 16 visual baselines — the last two executed against the live project with the editorial content seeded. `/style` and `/article/speed-considered` are pixel-identical to their Track A screenshots while rendering from Supabase. Also confirmed on the built server: unknown category and article slugs still 404. **Not** run: the E2E suite, which needs an admin session this container cannot provision.
  - **⚠️ Do not hand-carry SQL containing non-ASCII text.** The seed was applied by pasting generated SQL into the dashboard's SQL editor, and the em dashes and typographic apostrophes arrived as `â` — UTF-8 bytes decoded as latin-1 somewhere between the file and the browser. The generated files were valid UTF-8; nothing in the repo was wrong. **11 of 53 rows were damaged and it was completely silent** — `Feature â Standard` is still a plausible-looking string. The fix is to emit **pure ASCII**: Postgres `U&'…'` escapes for text (`\2014`) and JSON `\uXXXX` for jsonb, both decoded server-side where the encoding is known. `scripts/seed.ts` was never exposed to this — it sends JSON over HTTPS with a declared charset. The lesson generalises: a clipboard and a browser have no encoding contract, so anything travelling that path should be 7-bit.
  - **A sensible fallback turned bad data into a plausible page.** One casualty of the encoding damage was `articles.template`, which became `Feature â Standard`. That matches none of the twenty template names, so `layoutFor()` fell back to the default and the article rendered with **the wrong hero variant** — no error, no warning, just a different design. The fallback behaved exactly as specified. Worth remembering when judging defensive defaults: they convert loud failures into quiet ones, which is the trade being made.
  - **⚠️ Seeded content broke an E2E locator, and it will do so again.** CI run #49 failed on `articles.spec.ts`: `getByRole("link", { name: "History" })` resolved to two elements, because the seed introduced *A Brief History of the Aperitivo* and *…of the Chronograph* and **Playwright matches an accessible name as a substring by default**. The spec was written when `articles` held nothing but its own row, so a bare name was unambiguous; 53 rows later it is not. Fixed with `exact: true`, applied to all three specs that share the pattern (`articles`, `builder`, `products`) rather than only the one that went red — the other two are one well-named page or product away from the same failure. The exactness also removes a race for free: an exact locator matches nothing on the list page, so Playwright's auto-wait carries the click through to the detail page instead of throwing mid-navigation. **The general rule: content-derived test data makes every substring locator a hostage to the content.**
  - **The deep-compare earned its keep, twice.** Neither defect was visible to a screenshot: mojibake is legible enough to skim past, and a wrong hero variant looks like a design choice. Both were caught by comparing every field of all 53 articles against the seed source. This is the argument for `lib/demo/` being seed **input** rather than runtime data — the comparison is only meaningful because the two sides are genuinely independent.
  - **The seeder is the maintained path and covers everything.** `scripts/seed.ts` gained `seedAuthors`, `seedTags`, `seedArticles`, `seedArticleTags`, `seedArticleRelations`, and `seedCategories` now writes the category documents and `hero_asset_id`. It also derives its media list from the editorial *and* product images rather than products alone — the seven files coincide today, and depending on that coincidence would strand an article's hero the moment a photograph left the catalog.
- **Phase 7a (the public render path) — decisions.**
  - **⚠️ `lib/blocks/sources/supabase.ts` cannot exist, and this file has been promising it since Phase 2.** `sources/demo.ts`'s own header says Phase 7 adds it; the "Next phase" list says the same. But ESLint bars `lib/blocks/**` from importing `@/lib/db/*`, and the rule's message states the resolution: *"binding sources are injected."* `demo.ts` is legal there only because `lib/catalog.ts` and `lib/editorial.ts` are pure data modules. **The interface belongs in the leaf; a database-backed implementation belongs in `lib/services`.** Nothing about the design is wrong — only the filename this file kept repeating.
  - **No Supabase binding source was written, deliberately.** `DEMO_SECTIONS` contains **zero `$bind` descriptors**, so an implementation shipped now would have nothing exercising it — the same "code with nothing behind it" trap this file records for templates and patterns, which have existed since Phase 3 with no row ever behind them. It belongs with 7c, where articles give it something to bind to.
  - **`lib/db/public.ts` exists for one property: it reads no cookies.** `server.ts` calls `cookies()`, and **any route awaiting `cookies()` is opted out of static rendering by Next** — correct for the admin, wrong for a public site that has been 65 static files since Track A. The public client is plain `supabase-js` rather than `@supabase/ssr`, because that package exists to move a session between cookies and the client and there is deliberately no session here. RLS still applies as `anon`; `using (status = 'published' or is_staff())` is what makes it safe, and an anonymous caller is not staff.
  - **The homepage throws rather than falling back to the demo module.** A silent fallback would render a plausible page from a broken read and nobody would find out for a week. Static rendering already provides what a fallback was reaching for: the page is built once, and if a later revalidation fails Next keeps serving the last good output. A blip cannot blank the site — only a failed build can, and a failed build is meant to be loud. `notFound()` was rejected for the same reason: it would quietly prerender the 404 page as the site's homepage.
  - **⚠️ The build now reads the database, which makes seeding a build dependency.** CI provisions an admin but never seeded, so `supabase start` left `pages` empty and `npm run build` would have failed on "No published page with slug home". A `Seed content` step now runs `scripts/seed.ts` before the build. **Anywhere that builds this app now needs a reachable, seeded database** — Railway included, though it already has the credentials mirrored.
  - **Publishing revalidates the public path, and `snapshot` deliberately does not.** `revalidatePath` was already used for `/admin/*`; publish, unpublish and rollback now also revalidate the route the page is served at, so publishing takes effect without a deploy. Snapshot writes history and touches no published payload. `publicPathForPage` lives in `lib/domain/routes.ts` because `revalidatePath("/home")` would succeed, revalidate nothing, and leave the homepage stale with no error anywhere — exactly the failure a shared helper and its test exist to prevent.
  - **Verification honesty — Phase 7a.** The stored `published_data.sections` was deep-compared against `DEMO_SECTIONS` field for field **before** the switch: identical. Then the build was run against the **live project** — 70/70 pages generated, `/` still `○ (Static)` with a 1h revalidate — and **all 14 public visual baselines passed with the homepage rendering from the database**. That is the strongest evidence any phase has produced for a render-path change. Not done: nothing was written to the live project, and no scheduled or rollback path was exercised end to end in a browser.
- **Phase 6a (products admin) — decisions.**
  - **A product is the fifth document type, and that was one line of SQL.** `0005_commerce.sql` gave `products` the full versioning column set — `draft_data` / `published_data` / `version` / `status` / `published_at` — identical to what `0003` gave pages and `0004` gave articles. **Nothing had ever used it**, because `document_table()` in `0010` is an allowlist and `'product'` was not on it: every publish, snapshot or rollback against a product raised "not a publishable entity type". `0014_product_documents.sql` adds the one line. Everything else was already true — `revisions` and `publish_events` key on `entity_type` text with *no* foreign key, and `0001` already seeds `product.read` / `.write` / `.publish` / `.delete`, which `publish_document` asserts inside the function. **This is the third document type to fit the builder unchanged**, after `article`; `BLOCK_TREE_KEY.product` is `sections`, one ordered list. `template` is still the one that would need an area switcher.
  - **The alternative was plain CRUD, and it would have cost the media safety net.** Treating products like taxonomy would have thrown away four live columns and meant a product page could never be composed — but the sharper reason is deletion. `documents.deleteDocument` is what calls `clearEntityMedia`, and `media_usages.entity_id` carries **no foreign key**, so nothing in the database would have noticed a bespoke product delete skipping it. Every asset a deleted product referenced would have become permanently undeletable. Being a document type is what makes that correct by default rather than by remembering.
  - **`schedulable_document_table()` was deliberately left alone, and it exposed a loose end in `0005`.** `products.status` permits `'scheduled'` but the table has **no `scheduled_for` column** to hold the date.
