Keeping `product` out of `SCHEDULABLE_TYPES` is what makes that unreachable — the status never reaches a UI, and `[id]/actions.ts` ships no `scheduleAction`. Recorded rather than repaired: fixing it means either dropping a value from a live CHECK constraint or adding a column for a feature nothing asks for.
  - **Money crosses exactly one boundary, in `ProductDetails.tsx`.** The columns are integer pence, the action's Zod schema insists on `.int()`, and an editor thinks in pounds. `poundsToPence` / `penceToPounds` are the only conversion and nothing does arithmetic on a pounds value — the mistake that once discounted a £145 subtotal by £22.00 instead of £21.75. `products.test.ts` asserts every demo price converts to whole pence without loss, which is the thing Phase 7's migration will depend on.
  - **The cross-field rules live in `productMetaSchema`, not in the action's input schema.** "An affiliate product needs a merchant URL" and "a compare-at price must be a reduction" are facts about a product, not facts about one form, so they sit in `lib/domain/products.ts` and the action calls them. The issue path is kept precise (`["affiliate","merchant_url"]`) for the same reason `lib/blocks/validate.ts` went to trouble over `BlockIssue.path`: a form can focus the control that is wrong.
  - **The database's own affiliate check is weaker than it looks.** `affiliate_needs_merchant_url` uses `affiliate ? 'merchant_url'`, which only asks whether the **key** is present — `{"merchant_url": null}` satisfies it. The check that means something is `z.string().url()` in the domain schema. The 23514 translation in the service is the backstop for anything that bypasses the form.
  - **A variant's `price_pence` is nullable and that null is load-bearing.** It means "whatever the product costs". Writing the product's price onto every variant would fork one number into as many rows as there are sizes, and the next price change would update one of them. The dialog leaves the box empty by default and the table renders `inherits`.
  - **The gallery stores asset ids; a block's `image` field still stores a URL.** Same split Phase 5b made for `featured_asset_id`, same reasoning: `product_media.asset_id` has a real foreign key with `on delete cascade`, so referential integrity is free and the gallery cannot outlive its assets. A block field has to keep working when it holds a third-party CDN URL the library does not own.
  - **Verification honesty — Phase 6a.** Ran in this container: the four gates (**536 unit tests**, up from 520), `npm run build` (all five product routes present), and `npm run test:visual` — **all 14 public baselines passed**, confirming nothing in this phase touched the public site. `0014` was applied to the live project via the Supabase MCP and verified: `document_table('product')` returns `products`, an unknown type still returns null, and `schedulable_document_table('product')` correctly returns null. **No product row was written to the live project.**
  - **The CI run (PR #11): green in three rounds, and every round was the spec's fault, not the app's.** Phase 5's lesson held — none of the three was an application bug. (1) The spec waited for `Created “<name>”`, the wording the *taxonomy* dialogs use, while `CollectionsList` pushes the literal `"Collection created"`. It now asserts the table row instead, which is the better assertion regardless: **a toast proves an action returned; a row proves it persisted.** (2) The fix for (1) added an inline `role="alert"` beside the save button while a toast still carried the same text, and CI reported a strict-mode violation — a real duplication, one message twice, inches apart. The toast was dropped for errors; success still toasts, because nothing else on screen says so. (3) **`getByRole("alert")` is never unique in a Next app.** The framework mounts `<div role="alert" aria-live="assertive" id="__next-route-announcer__">` into every page and leaves it empty, so the locator always resolves to at least two elements. Filtering the role *by its text* is what distinguishes them — neither half suffices alone. Worth remembering the next time an accessible-role locator looks obviously specific.
  - **⚠️ The visual suite needs credentials in this container, and that is worth knowing before the next session loses an hour to it.** `npm run test:visual` first failed with Playwright's `webServer` timing out after 120s: `middleware.ts` constructs a Supabase client on *every* request and threw "Your project's URL and Key are required" on all of them. There is no `.env.local` in a fresh container. **A gitignored `.env.local` holding syntactically valid placeholder values is enough** — the public routes render from the demo modules and never reach the database. This is the same technique Phase 2 recorded for a manual server run; it had not been connected to the visual suite before.

    ⚠️ **SUPERSEDED — do not act on the sentence above.** It was true when written and stopped being true at Phase 7a, when the site layout began reading the database on every public route. Placeholders now make all 16 baselines fail. See the corrected guidance in "A new session's first actions" and in `design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/CLAUDE.md`. Left in place rather than deleted because it is the record of *why* the trap existed, but it needed this marker beside it.
- **Phase 5 — the CI run (PR #8). Green, in two rounds.**
  - **Run #15 settled three things that had never been tested anywhere.** (1) `supabase start` succeeded in both jobs that run one, so the committed `config.toml` did **not** disturb the CLI's defaults — the minimal-file bet was right, and it is now proven rather than asserted. (2) **`0013_media_storage.sql` applied cleanly against a real Postgres**: `supabase start` runs every migration, so the bucket insert and all four `storage.objects` policies executed for the first time. The privilege problem flagged as plausible for `create policy` on `storage.objects` did not materialise. (3) **All five article and taxonomy specs passed on their first run**, including the three near-identical taxonomy dialogs whose scoping could not be verified without a real DOM.
  - **Both failures in run #15 were the tests' fault, not the app's — and that is the notable part.** Phase 4's equivalent step found five real application bugs; this one found none. The difference is that Phase 4's lessons were applied while writing rather than after: action references not closures, locators by role not by text, per-run identity minted inside the first test.
  - **The hard failure was a copy error with a general lesson.** Every confirmation dialog in the admin names the thing it destroys — "Delete page", "Delete article", "Delete asset" — and `media.spec.ts` asked for `"Delete"` with `exact: true`, which can never match. `articles.spec.ts` got it right and passed, which is precisely why only one of the two went red. **When two specs share a pattern and only one fails, suspect the spec, not the app.**
  - **The flake was an assertion racing a round trip.** One upload is a file read, a SHA-256, a storage PUT, a catalogue insert and a revalidation; on a cold CI server that outruns Playwright's 5s default, so the details panel had not rendered when the assertion gave up. The asset was arriving the whole time. Given its own 30s timeout — **a slow round trip and a missing element look identical to a locator**, and the default timeout is tuned for the former only by accident.
  - **What green means here.** The whole of Phase 5 has now executed against a real database: upload, usage reconciliation, the delete refusal, the polymorphic delete that clears stranded usage rows, article create/file/compose/publish/rollback, and taxonomy CRUD. The integration suites also ran, having never executed in this container.
  - **Still not proven:** the four admin visual baselines remain uncaptured and their shots still skip, and `0013` is applied on a *local* stack only — the live project has not run it.
- **Phase 5b (articles & taxonomy) — decisions.**
  - **The builder needed no changes, exactly as Phase 3 predicted.** `BLOCK_TREE_KEY.article` is `sections` — one ordered list — so `/admin/articles/[id]/builder` renders the same `Builder` component with `type: "article"` and nothing else. The polymorphic document repository, `publish_document`, the revision counter and the preview route all served a second document type without a line changed. That prediction had been sitting in this file untested since Phase 3; it now has a route behind it.
  - **An article's details screen is not the builder, and pages' is.** `/admin/pages/[id]` opens the canvas; `/admin/articles/[id]` opens the metadata form, with the canvas a click away at `./builder`. Two reasons. An article's primary editing surface genuinely *is* its metadata — which of the twenty templates renders it, who wrote it, what it is filed under — and the block tree is the optional extra. And `Builder` is `h-screen` and owns the viewport, so a metadata panel could not have sat beside it anyway.
  - **`featured_asset_id` stores an asset id; a block's `image` field stores a URL.** This looks like an inconsistency and is not. A column with a real foreign key gets referential integrity and `on delete set null` for free. A block field has to keep working when it holds a third-party CDN URL the library does not own — which `lib/media.ts` demo media and every `/public` file still do. Different constraints, different representations; the picker returns the whole `AssetView` so a caller can take whichever it needs.
  - **`HistoryView` moved to `components/admin/history/` and takes its rollback action as a prop.** It had been importing `rollbackAction` from the pages route directly, which is the only thing that made it a pages component. Revisions and publish events are one polymorphic table each, so articles get the identical screen — the action reference is passed in, the same pattern the builder and the media library already use.
  - **The article actions are written out, not generated from a factory over the document type.** A `"use server"` module may export only async functions and Next assigns each export an action id at build time; a factory-produced export would *probably* work. "Probably", combined with a failure mode that only appears when a real browser opens a real page, is exactly the combination that broke every builder load in Phase 4 and that neither `next build` nor the unit tests could see. Eight obvious wrappers beat one clever one.
  - **Taxonomy reads are gated on `article.read`, because there is no `taxonomy.read` to gate them on.** 0001 seeds `taxonomy.write` and no matching read permission — the three tables are public-read at the RLS level, so one was never needed. Writes use `taxonomy.write` as the policies require. Recorded in `lib/services/taxonomy.ts` as well, at the layer that enforces it.
  - **Editing an existing taxonomy row never re-derives its slug from its name.** The slug is a live URL; quietly changing it because someone fixed a typo in a category name would break every link to it. Slug auto-fill is a create-time convenience only.
  - **`ARTICLE_TEMPLATE_NAMES` is declared in `lib/domain/articles.ts`, not derived from `lib/articles.ts`.** The latter is demo content and does not belong in `lib/domain`; a conformance test asserts the two agree instead. Same stance `lib/blocks` takes towards `components/sections/registry.ts`, and for the same reason — co-location would look safer while checking nothing. The test also pins the em dash in "Feature — Standard", which a silent substitution would turn into a template that does not exist.
  - **Verification honesty — Phase 5b.** Ran in this container: the four gates (**520 unit tests**), `npm run build` (all nine admin routes present), and `npm run test:visual` — all 14 public baselines passed again. Did **not** run: anything against a database. `tests/e2e/articles.spec.ts` is written for CI and has never executed. **No article, author or tag has ever existed in the live project** — the tables were empty at the start of this session and nothing was written to them.
- **Phase 5a (media library) — decisions.**
  - **⚠️ The `media` bucket had never been created.** 0002_media.sql catalogues objects in a bucket named `media` (`bucket text not null default 'media'`) and 41 tables' worth of schema was built on top of that assumption, but `storage.buckets` on the live project was **empty**. Nothing could ever have been uploaded, and any catalogue row would have pointed at an object that could not exist. `0013_media_storage.sql` creates it and adds the four `storage.objects` policies, each scoped `bucket_id = 'media'` and gated on the same `media.write` / `media.delete` permissions the catalogue tables use — so one permission change moves the bytes and the rows together. Following 0012's precedent, it says **nothing** about function privileges.
  - **`image`/`video` fields still store a URL string — this was a decision, not an omission.** The alternative was an `asset` field kind holding a uuid, which is more precise and touches every image-bearing manifest, `normalize`, `validate` and the public renderer. Keeping URLs meant **zero manifest changes and zero risk to the pixel-verified public site**, and it made the Phase 4 hook work exactly as designed. The cost is that usage tracking has to reverse a URL to a storage path (`storagePathFromPublicUrl`), which is exact for our own bucket and silent for anything else. Worth revisiting in Phase 7, where the render path is being rewired anyway.
  - **The URL reversal is deliberately tolerant of the host and of the transform route.** The same object is reachable through `*.supabase.co` and through any custom storage domain in front of it, and `/render/image/public/` is the same object as `/object/public/` with a `?width=` on it. A matcher keyed on the origin, or one that missed the transform route, would report an in-use asset as safe to delete the moment someone resized an image in the builder. The bucket-scoped path is the identity.
  - **Usage reconciliation inserts before it deletes, and that ordering is the whole safety argument.** The two writes are not a transaction, so one can fail after the other. Deleting first leaves a window — and on a crash a permanent state — in which an asset that *is* on a page has no usage row, so the library calls it unused and lets an editor delete it: exactly the mistake `media_usages` exists to prevent. Inserting first can only ever strand a stale row, which makes the library over-cautious and is corrected by the next save. **Every failure mode in this feature is arranged to leave a stale reference rather than a missing one.**
  - **⚠️ A real gap found while writing the E2E, and fixed: deleting a page stranded its usage rows forever.** `media_usages.asset_id` carries `on delete cascade`, so removing an asset takes its rows with it. `entity_id` carries **no foreign key at all** — 0002 made it polymorphic on purpose, so a new content type needs no schema change — which means the database has no way to notice the page on the other end is gone. Left alone, every asset a deleted page referenced would have been permanently undeletable, blocked by a page that no longer existed and that no editor could navigate to. `documents.deleteDocument` now calls `clearEntityMedia`. **Any future entity type that grows a delete path has to call it too; there is no trigger that will cover for it.**
  - **Usage identity is the (asset, field path) pair, not the path alone.** Swapping one image for another on the same control keeps the path and changes the asset, so a path-only comparison keeps the old asset's row and reports it as still in use. The comparison is extracted as `staleUsageIds` — pure, exported and unit-tested — precisely because it is the only real logic in the reconciliation and everything around it is I/O.
  - **Reconciliation never throws out of a draft save.** A save that succeeded has succeeded; derived data must not be what loses an editor their work. Same reasoning as "a draft save does not validate" from Phase 3.
  - **`media_assets_search_idx` is unreachable from PostgREST, and search uses `ilike` instead.** 0002 created a GIN index over a `to_tsvector(...)` **expression**; PostgREST's `fts` operator can only target a *column*, and a leading-wildcard `ilike` could not use that index either. Reaching it needs a stored generated column to hang the index on — a `public` schema change plus a types regeneration. At the scale this library will hold for a long while a sequential scan is not the bottleneck, so the honest fix is recorded rather than the mismatch hidden. **The index as written has never been used by anything.**
  - **Deduplication happens before the upload, not after.** `checksum` is computed from the bytes, and an asset already holding them is returned instead of uploading a second copy. 0002 put the column and its index there for this; without it a production week ends with six copies of the same cover.
  - **An upload whose catalogue insert fails removes its own object.** An object with no catalogue row is invisible to the library and unreclaimable by anything short of a storage audit. Deletion runs the other way round — catalogue row first, object second — because the opposite risks a row pointing at bytes that no longer exist, which renders as a broken image on a published page. Orphaned bytes are harmless; an orphaned row is not.
  - **The picker reaches the builder through a context, not through four prop signatures.** Threading a server action from the builder route through `Builder` → `PropertiesPanel` → `FieldControl` → `MediaUrlControl` would have widened four components that otherwise have no interest in media. `MediaPickerProvider` is mounted once in the admin layout; `useMediaPicker()` returns `null` outside it and the control degrades to exactly the Phase 4 URL box — the same stance `useToast` already takes.
  - **The URL text box stayed.** It is not a leftover: blocks store URLs, so a field can legitimately hold something the library does not own — the demo media in `lib/media.ts` are third-party CDN URLs and `/public` files are still valid content. Removing it to force everything through the library would have made those unrepresentable.
  - **Alt text is not copied into the block when an asset is picked.** It lives on the asset, which is where it is authored; copying it would fork one description into as many places as the image appears. The grid flags an image that has none, because that is where an editor sees forty at once.
  - **Verification honesty — Phase 5a.** What actually ran in this container: the four gates (**515 unit tests**, up from 446), `npm run build` (`/admin/media` present), and `npm run test:visual` — **all 14 public baselines passed**, confirming nothing in this phase touched the public site. What did **not** run: the migration was never applied and no live-project exercise took place, because applying it and querying the project both required an approval this session could not obtain. **No row was written to the live project in this session**; the counts below are the ones read at the start and are unchanged. The E2E spec (`tests/e2e/media.spec.ts`) is written for CI and has never executed anywhere — treat its selectors and timings as unproven, exactly as Phase 4's were before CI settled them over four rounds.
  - **Live table counts at the start of Phase 5a** (unchanged by it — nothing was written): `storage.buckets` **0**, `media_assets` 0, `media_folders` 0, `media_usages` 0, `articles` 0, `authors` 0, `tags` 0, `categories` 5, `products` 16, `pages` 1, `templates` 0, `patterns` 0.
- **Phase 4 (admin UI & builder) — decisions.**
  - **⚠️ Bug found and fixed before it could ship: no page carrying a binding could ever be published.** `validate.ts` ran each manifest's `strictSchema` straight over `blockProps` with no binding awareness, so `latestGrid.items` holding `{ $bind: … }` failed `z.array(…)`, `validateDocumentPayload` collected the issue, and `publish` threw. All seven bindable fields were affected. It stayed latent only because the seed data is literals throughout — the binding engine had unit tests, but **nothing had ever tried to publish a bound block**. Found while planning the properties panel's literal/dynamic toggle, which would otherwise have shipped a guaranteed publish failure. `validateBlock` now lifts bound fields out before parsing and checks their queries separately.
  - **The binding fix is deliberately not a `z.union`.** Making each bindable field `z.union([literal, descriptor])` validates correctly but reports a failure inside a bound group as `invalid_union` at the *group's* path, collapsing `article.href` to `article`. `BlockIssue.path` exists precisely so the panel can focus the offending control, so the union was reverted in favour of omit-and-check. **If you touch this, keep the paths precise** — `validate.test.ts` asserts it.
  - **Binding detection at validation uses `hasBindingShape`, not `isBindingDescriptor`.** A field bound but left with an empty source is not a *valid* descriptor, so the strict check would fall back to reading it as an ordinary wrong-typed literal and tell the editor "expected array, received object" instead of "source is required". Resolution still uses `isBindingDescriptor` — only a well-formed descriptor should ever reach a source.
  - **The descriptor schema moved to `lib/blocks/bindingDescriptor.ts`** to break the cycle `binding.ts → manifests → defineBlock → binding.ts`. `binding.ts` re-exports it. Note the re-export must be `import` + `export {}`, not `export … from`: a pure re-export does not bind the name in module scope, and `binding.ts` uses `isBindingDescriptor` internally.
  - **"Byte-identical prerendered HTML" does not survive a route-group split**, so the technique this file records for the Prettier and normalize passes had to be adapted. Next embeds the RSC flight payload in `<script>` tags, that payload encodes the segment tree (which gains `(site)`), and the *number* of payload chunks tracks the module graph. The honest comparison is the rendered `<body>` with script bodies emptied and runs of them collapsed: **64 of 65 pages byte-identical**, backed by a new 14-shot screenshot suite in both themes. Do not claim the raw files match; they do not, and for good reasons.
  - **The 65th page was a real regression the diff caught.** With the chrome moved out of the root layout, Next's built-in 404 rendered bare — body content fell from 10,090 to 873 characters. `app/not-found.tsx` restores it, composing the chrome explicitly because a root `not-found.tsx` renders inside the *root* layout, not the site one. This is the entire argument for doing the split as its own commit behind a hard gate.
  - **The canvas must be wrapped in `CartProvider`, and this is not defensive.** All 22 sections are free of *server* dependencies — none async, none importing `next/headers`, `lib/db`, `lib/services` or `server-only` — which is what makes a live client canvas possible at all. But `ProductRow.tsx` calls `useCart()` and `CartProvider` throws without the context, so dropping a product row onto an unwrapped canvas would take down the whole builder. **Freedom from server dependencies is not freedom from context.** `Canvas.test.tsx` mounts one node of every registered type and is the test that catches this class of failure.
  - **Every block gets an error boundary, because `normalizeBlock` is forgiving by design.** On a failed `safeParse` it returns the *raw* props — the renderer must not be what decides a page is unrenderable. In an editor that means emptying a required list sends `items === undefined` into `LatestGrid` and `items.map()` throws, and without a boundary that one mistake blanks the builder *including the panel needed to undo it*.
  - **`insertDefaults` must be deep-copied, and the reason is worse than the freeze.** `Object.freeze` in `defineBlock` is **shallow**: `latestGrid.insertDefaults.items[0]` is not frozen and is the same object on every insert, so a shallow spread would leave two inserted blocks sharing one list item. There is a test that fails on exactly that.
  - **Editor chrome never touches a section's own markup.** Selection rings, toolbars and the hidden-state opacity are absolutely positioned siblings or classes on a wrapper; navigation inside the canvas is killed with a descendant selector on the wrapper. That is what keeps "do not restyle the design components" true in the literal sense the pixel verification depends on.
  - **Clearing an optional field deletes the key rather than writing `""`.** An empty string satisfies `z.string()`, so writing it persists a present value where the component expected `undefined` and its own fallback.
  - **A new list row carries only `fieldSetDefaults(of)`.** List items are validated `.strict()` at publish, so any extra seeded key becomes an issue the editor never typed. Related: `fieldSetDefaults` skips `list` fields entirely, which is why a block's initial list content comes from `insertDefaults`.
  - **Autosave does not decide when a revision is written.** `saveDraft` already throttles that to five minutes via `shouldWriteAutosaveRevision`; duplicating the rule client-side is exactly the class of mistake `0011_autosave.sql` exists to fix. The hook debounces 1500ms with a 10s ceiling, never overlaps two saves, and `markSaved` takes the tree that was *sent* so an edit made mid-flight stays dirty instead of being dropped.
  - **Rollback's wording is load-bearing**: "Restored v7 into the draft — publish to make it live". The service restores into the draft and publishes nothing, deliberately. The UI must not imply otherwise.
  - **The admin design system exists because the site's primitives structurally could not serve**, not because a new one felt tidier. `components/ui/Button` has no `disabled` (which is why `SignInForm` already hand-rolled its own submit button) and no size scale; `components/store/SelectField` takes `options: string[]` and therefore **cannot render a manifest's options at all** — `SelectOption` is `{value,label}` and `ctaBand.variant` offers "Split — heading left, email right" for the value `"split"`; `components/store/Field` renders no `name` and has no `help` slot. The site's own primitives were left untouched: they are tuned for checkout and used on pixel-verified pages.
  - **`NumberInput` holds a string edit buffer.** Driving a number input off a `number` makes the intermediate states of typing un-representable — `""`, `"-"`, `"1."` are all `NaN` — so a controlled input fights the editor's keystrokes.
  - **ESLint hole closed**: the layering override was scoped to `components/**/*.tsx`, so a **`.ts`** file under `components/` — the builder's zustand store, precisely — was free to import `lib/db/repositories` directly. The glob now includes `components/**/*.ts`, with tests excluded.
  - **`PermissionSet` does not survive the server→client boundary** (it is a class). The admin layout passes `user.permissions.toArray()`. The UI gate is cosmetic anyway — `requirePermission` in the services is the real boundary, RLS the one beneath it.
  - **`window.scrollTo` is now stubbed in the unit setup.** jsdom implements no scrolling and `lib/useScrollLock` restores position whenever a Dialog closes, so every admin dialog test printed a "Not implemented" stack without it.
- **Phase 3 (documents, publishing, revisions, preview) — decisions.**
  - **Publishing is a SQL transaction, not application code.** It writes three things — `published_data`, a `revisions` snapshot, a `publish_events` row — and over PostgREST those would be three requests, so a failure between them leaves a published page with no history. `0010_publishing.sql` does all three in one transaction. `security invoker`, so RLS still evaluates as the editor and the service-role key stays out of the request path.
  - **The publish permission is asserted inside the function.** RLS on `pages` only gates `page.write`; publishing is a distinct, higher act. Checking `page.publish` in SQL means a bug in a service cannot publish something the editor themselves could not.
  - **`version` now advances on publish, snapshot *and* restore** — amending the `0003` comment that said "on publish". `revisions` is unique on `(entity_type, entity_id, version)`, so an operation that wrote history without advancing the counter would collide with whatever already claimed that number. One counter, no divergence.
  - **Rollback restores into the draft and publishes nothing.** The editor reviews and then publishes. An undo that silently shipped would be a worse mistake than the one it was undoing. Gated on `revision.restore`, not `page.publish`.
  - **Unpublish keeps `published_data`.** RLS already hides a non-published row from the public, so clearing the payload would buy nothing and make re-publishing a restore instead of one step. No revision is written because no payload changed.
  - **`resolve_preview` is the only `security definer` function, and deliberately so.** A preview link must work for someone who is not staff and may not be signed in, which RLS cannot express: loosening the `preview_sessions` select policy to `using (true)` would let anyone enumerate every live token, and resolving the token with the service-role client would put a full RLS bypass on a public route. The definer function does the capability check inside the database and returns nothing but one document's draft. Verified against the live project with `auth.uid()` genuinely null: anon resolves a valid token, gets 0 rows for an expired one, and can read neither `preview_sessions` nor `revisions` nor any draft page.
  - **A draft save does not validate; publishing does.** An editor mid-edit routinely holds an incomplete block, and refusing to save would lose their work. `lib/services/publishing.ts` runs the Phase 2 manifests over every tree in the payload and refuses to publish an invalid one.
  - **Autosave revisions are throttled by age** (`shouldWriteAutosaveRevision`, 5 min) and go through `autosave_document` (`0011`), which takes its own version like every other history-writing operation.
  - ⚠️ **Bug found and fixed after the first Phase 3 commit — read this before touching revisions.** Autosave originally wrote at the *current* version and swallowed the resulting unique violation as "this version already has its checkpoint". But `publish_document` sets the entity's version to N *and* writes its revision at N, so from a document's first publish onward the current number was always already taken: the throttle correctly decided to checkpoint, and every write was silently discarded. Autosave history only ever worked on a never-published document. Fixed in `0011` by making autosave allocate its own version, in SQL, in one transaction. **The lesson is the swallowed error**, not the arithmetic — `if (error.code !== "23505") throw` looked like defensive coding and was actually a feature quietly not working. Verified on the live project: after a publish at v1, successive autosaves now land at v2 and v3, and `rollback_document` can still address either.
  - **Templates hold named areas, not one ordered list** (`BLOCK_TREE_KEY.template === null`), so `blockTreesOf()` returns a list of trees and validation covers every area without a special case per call site.
  - **Layering hole found and closed:** the preview route initially imported a repository directly. ESLint now bars `app/**` and `components/**` from `@/lib/db/repositories/*`. Scoped to repositories only, so the legitimate `lib/db/server` imports in the auth routes still pass.
  - **Writing the expansion tests found a real bug:** unresolved `_ref`s were reported with the pattern-internal key, which matches nothing in the returned tree — an editor could not have located the offending block. Fixed by threading the key prefix down through expansion rather than applying it afterwards.
  - **Known gap: `resolve_preview` has no rate limiting.** Tokens are 256-bit so brute force is impractical, but the endpoint is public and unthrottled. Worth revisiting if preview links are ever shared widely.
  - **A test that did not test what it claimed.** `tests/integration/publishing.test.ts` set up an `author` and commented "Holds page.write but NOT page.publish, to prove the gate is real." The seeded `author` role holds **only `page.read`** — no role ships with write-but-not-publish. The test would have passed while proving something much weaker (a reader cannot publish), and would still have passed had the gate checked nothing beyond write access. Fixed: `fixtures.createRole()` builds a throwaway role with exactly `page.read` + `page.write`, and the test now first asserts the actor *can* edit the page, so the refusal is demonstrably about publishing. Confirmed on the live project — such an actor gets `42501` from `publish_document` and succeeds at `autosave_document`.
  - **Verification honesty:** the integration suites are committed but were **never executed** — this container has no Docker daemon, so `npx supabase start` cannot run, and no MCP tool exposes the service-role key the fixtures need (nor should one be pasted into a transcript; see the rotation note under Known issues). They are written for CI, which does start a stack. What *was* run: the four gates (282 unit tests), `npm run build`, live-project exercises of publish/rollback/preview/autosave and the permission gate via the Supabase MCP, and a by-hand load of `/preview/[token]` against the live project. Every live scratch row — pages, revisions, events, preview sessions, the throwaway role and its auth user — was deleted and the table counts confirmed back at baseline.
  - **The gap this leaves, stated plainly:** no `lib/services/*` function has ever executed. They need a Next request context, and only `resolvePreview` ran, via the preview route. The SQL underneath them is well proven; the orchestration on top — validation gates, the autosave throttle, `compareVersions`, template assignment resolution, `expandPatternRefs` — is not. Templates and patterns in particular are code with nothing behind them: no template or pattern has ever existed in the database. **Phase 4 is where that gets exercised for real**; treat the first admin screen that calls these as the test.
  - **Verification honesty — Phase 4.** What actually ran in this container: the four gates (**446 unit tests**, up from 282), `npm run build`, the prerender markup diff against a pre-split baseline (64/65 byte-identical), the 14-shot public screenshot suite in both themes (all passing after the split *and* again after the whole phase), and the **built server pointed at the live Supabase project** — where `/admin` and `/admin/pages` both redirected to `/sign-in` with the destination preserved, all 7 public routes returned 200, `/preview/<bad-token>` returned its expiry page rather than a 500 and kept `noindex, nofollow, nocache`, unknown categories 404'd, and the deleted `/admin/builder` was still caught by middleware.
  - **The gap Phase 4 did NOT close, stated plainly.** The **signed-in** journey has still never executed. `lib/services/*` functions remain unrun under a real session: creating a page, saving a draft, publishing, rolling back and minting a preview all go through code paths that compile, typecheck and are unit-tested, but have not been exercised against the database as an authenticated editor. The reason is the same key this file already flags for rotation — `scripts/create-admin.ts` needs the service-role key to provision or reset an account, it is not in this container, and pasting one into a transcript is what created the outstanding rotation item in the first place. Minting a throwaway login in the live auth schema was considered and declined: creating a working credential in a production system is not something to do unasked.
  - **⚠️ CI had never closed it either, and now does — correcting an earlier claim in this file.** `.github/workflows/ci.yml` started a throwaway local Supabase stack but **never provisioned an admin account and never exported `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL` / `E2E_ADMIN_PASSWORD`**, and references no repository secrets at all. So every signed-in spec — the four in `auth.spec.ts` as well as the three in `builder.spec.ts` — **skipped in CI, and had done since Phase 1c**: the authenticated half of the suite was silently passing by doing nothing. A `Provision the admin account` step now runs `scripts/create-admin.ts` against the local stack and exports both variables. Roles and permissions come from migration `0001`, which `supabase start` already applies, so nothing else was needed.
  - **The CI admin password is a literal, deliberately.** It unlocks a stack that is created and destroyed inside one job and is reachable only from that runner. Putting it in a repository secret would imply it guards something.
  - **Expect the first CI runs on this branch to be informative rather than green.** The signed-in specs have *never executed anywhere*, so their selectors and timings are unproven, and the public visual baselines were captured on a different host. Both are now running in the one place that can actually settle them.
  - **⚠️ And the first run proved the point immediately: CI's integration and E2E jobs had never run at all.** Both call `supabase start`, which auto-loads `supabase/seed.sql`, which was written for the superseded `0001_init.sql` schema and inserted `products.price` against a table whose column is `price_pence`. The stack aborted with `42703` before a single test executed — so two of the three CI jobs had been failing at startup since Phase 0, and the green `verify` job was the only one anyone was actually reading. Fixed by deleting the orphaned seed. **The lesson is the same one the autosave bug taught: a job that never gets far enough to fail a test looks a lot like a job that has nothing to report.**
  - **And then it went green: the signed-in journey passes in CI.** All 13 e2e specs, including the full builder journey — create → compose → save → publish → history → rollback — against a real database, plus the four `auth.spec.ts` specs that had skipped since Phase 1c. **Phase 3's stated gap is closed**: `lib/services/*` has now executed under a real session, and the orchestration this file warned was unproven — the validation gate, the autosave throttle, the revision counter, `publish_document`'s SQL transaction — has run. Five real bugs surfaced getting there, all logged below. Templates and patterns remain the untouched part: no template or pattern has ever existed in the database, and nothing in this suite creates one.
  - **The visual job ran for the first time, and the cross-host question is settled.** It sits after `test:e2e` in the same job, so every red e2e run had stopped short of it since Phase 0. **All 14 public baselines passed on `ubuntu-latest`** despite being captured in this dev container — the antialiasing risk this file flagged did not materialise, and `maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.01` is the right tolerance for full-page editorial shots. The four admin baselines failed for a different and more structural reason; see Known issues.
  - **A ninth, and the last one standing: the cleanup test raced its own success toast.** With the publish fix in, CI ran **12 of 13 e2e green** — the whole journey (create → compose → save → publish → history → rollback) executed against a real database for the first time, plus the four `auth.spec.ts` specs that had skipped since Phase 1c. The one failure was `cleans up the page it created`, and it was the test's fault, not the app's: it asserted `getByText("E2E Builder Page")` had count 0, but the success toast reads `Deleted "E2E Builder Page"` and lingers for `DISMISS_MS = 5000` — the same five seconds as the assertion timeout. The row was gone the whole time; the confirmation of its removal was what kept matching. Now asserted on the table's link role. **A locator that matches your own confirmation message cannot prove a deletion.** The same commit moved the page's title and slug out of module scope into the first test: Playwright retries a serial group from its first test, the failed attempt's row is still in the database, and a fixed slug would collide on `pages_slug_key` the moment anything downstream failed.
  - **An eighth, and the E2E finally proved the publish path end to end.** The run reached `Published v1` — meaning the server action ran, `publishing.publish` executed its SQL transaction against a real database, and a version came back. The assertion that then failed was the status pill: it still read **draft**. `BuilderStoreProvider` seeds its store once and holds it in a ref (one store per document, deliberately), and `router.refresh()` re-renders the server component without re-seeding it — so after a successful publish the bar kept showing the old status and version until a full reload. `setDoc()` now applies both from the action's result. **Anything that changes a document's status or version server-side has to say so to the store.**
  - **A seventh, and an accessibility fix the E2E run handed us for free.** The required marker was rendered inside the `<label>`, so it became part of the control's accessible name: the field was called **"Quote *"**, and a screen reader would announce "Quote star". CI surfaced it because Playwright reported the element `aka getByRole('textbox', { name: 'Quote *' })`. The asterisk is now `aria-hidden` and requiredness is carried by `aria-required` on the control, where it belongs. The marker is still visible; only its place in the accessibility tree changed.
  - **A sixth, and this one was mine: the builder route passed unserializable closures to a Client Component.** `app/(admin)/admin/pages/[id]/page.tsx` handed `Builder` `saveDraft: async (payload) => saveDraftAction({ id, payload })` and three siblings. Those arrows are ordinary functions created in a Server Component, not `"use server"` references, and Next refuses them at render — so **every builder page load threw**. `next build` cannot catch it (it is a render-time serialization failure) and the unit tests could not either, because they hand `Builder` plain fakes. It surfaced only when a real browser opened a real page in CI. The route now passes the action references directly and `Builder` binds the document id on the client, where a closure costs nothing. **If you add a callback to the builder, pass the action itself.**
  - **A fifth, in the fixtures themselves: `prefixed()` was unique per run but not per call.** `RUN_PREFIX` separates one run from another and scopes teardown; it does nothing to separate two calls within a run, so `prefixed("page")` returned an identical slug every time and the second `createPage()` in any file died on `pages_slug_key` — `createUser` likewise got "already registered" from the auth API. A per-call sequence now follows the prefix, so scoping and cleanup are unchanged. Written in Phase 3, wrong since Phase 3, invisible until CI could run the suite.
  - **A fourth, once the tests could finally execute: the schema had never granted a table privilege to anyone.** Migrations `0001`–`0011` create 41 tables and enable RLS on all of them, but grant nothing. On the hosted project that was invisible, because Supabase's bootstrap sets `alter default privileges … grant all on tables to anon, authenticated, service_role` and every migration-created table picked it up silently — verified: all four roles already hold the full DML set on `pages` upstream. A `supabase start` stack does not reproduce it, so in CI no role held any privilege and every fixture died on `permission denied for table pages`. **The schema was depending on ambient environment configuration for its access control.** `0012_grants.sql` states the grants explicitly and is a no-op against the live project.
  - **`0012` deliberately says nothing about functions.** Postgres grants EXECUTE to PUBLIC on creation, which is exactly why `0009`/`0010`/`0011` close with *revokes* on the publishing functions. A blanket `grant execute on all functions` in `0012` would have silently undone them and handed an anonymous caller `publish_document`.
  - **A third, behind the second: the project declared a Node version its own dependency forbids.** With the quotes stripped, the integration suite got as far as constructing a client and threw `Node.js detected but native WebSocket not found`. `@supabase/supabase-js@2.111.0` declares `engines: node >=22`, but `.nvmrc` pinned **20**, `package.json` said `>=20.0.0`, and `nixpacks.toml` — the **Railway production runtime** — pinned `nodejs_20`. Nothing caught it because the dev container runs 22.22.2, so the only machine actually honouring the pin was CI. All three are now 22, which is also the floor Node itself sets: 20 is past end-of-life. Note this was a live production risk, not only a CI one.
  - **A second bug was hiding directly behind the first.** With the stack finally starting, the integration suite ran for the first time and every file died on `Invalid supabaseUrl`. `supabase status -o env` emits its values **shell-quoted** (`API_URL="http://127.0.0.1:54321"`), `$GITHUB_ENV` takes the right-hand side literally, and so every consumer received a value with the quotes still attached. Both jobs' export steps now strip them. Two latent CI bugs stacked in sequence is what a pipeline looks like when nothing downstream of step one has ever executed — worth remembering the next time a job is "passing".
  - **Live table counts at the end of Phase 4** (unchanged by it — nothing was written): `pages` 1, `revisions` 0, `publish_events` 0, `preview_sessions` 0, `templates` 0, `patterns` 0. The last two confirm that templates and patterns are still code with nothing behind them.
  - **A correction worth recording:** the first live anon check was invalid. It impersonated the admin via `set_config('request.jwt.claims', …)` then did `set local role anon` — but switching the role does **not** clear the JWT claims, so `auth.uid()` still returned the admin's id and the `created_by = auth.uid()` policy branch matched. Re-run with the claims cleared and `auth.uid() is null` asserted first. If you test RLS this way, clear the claims.
- **Phase 2 (block manifests) — decisions.**
  - **Manifests hold no component reference.** `lib/blocks` is a leaf and cannot import `components/`, so the `_type → component` map stays in `components/sections/registry.ts`. `conformance.test.ts` asserting set-equality is what actually keeps the halves in step — co-location would have looked safer while checking nothing.
  - **The Zod schema is derived from the field descriptors, not written beside them.** A hand-written schema is a second description of the same contract, and two descriptions drift; that is the failure this phase exists to remove. Same reasoning puts defaults on the *field* rather than in a separate manifest-level `defaults` object.
  - **Two schemas per block.** `schema` strips undeclared keys (render path); `strictSchema` rejects them (publish path). An undeclared prop nearly always means a manifest has fallen behind its component, and that should be an authoring-time error, not a silently dropped value.
  - **`insertDefaults` is separate from field defaults** — the field default is what the component falls back to (`mobileHeight: "fullscreen"`), the insert default is what an editor sees after dragging the block in (placeholder copy for required fields that have no component default). Conflating them would have made every required field either mandatory-on-insert or silently blank.
  - **`normalizeBlock` never throws and never fails a render.** Unknown `_type`, or content that fails its own schema, passes through with props untouched. The renderer must not be the thing that decides a page is unrenderable; `validateTree()` reports it where there is an editor to tell.
  - **Bindings are projected onto the target field's declared shape.** The demo source returns rich rows (`category`, `lead`) so queries can filter on them; without projection every bound block failed strict validation on undeclared keys, which would have forced a hand-written `map` onto every binding. The manifest already knows the item shape, so it does the trimming; `map` stays for genuine renames.
  - **`defineBlock` throws at module load** when insert defaults fail their own schema. Loud and early: such a manifest would have the builder writing blocks that publish validation then rejects, and the four gates run before anything is committed.
  - **Render-safety proof: all 65 prerendered pages have byte-identical markup.** The RSC flight payload *does* differ, in two ways, both intended: `_key`/`_type` are no longer spread onto components as unused props (they never reached the DOM — no `_key=` attribute exists in the baseline), and prop key order now follows manifest field order. Net effect on the homepage payload: 59,080 → 58,942 bytes. The comparison strips Next's build ID (present twice — an HTML comment *and* escaped as `"b"` inside the flight payload) and content-hashed chunk/CSS filenames.
  - **`SectionRenderer`'s `@ts-expect-error` stays.** A registry lookup by string cannot narrow to one component's props; claiming otherwise would need machinery that buys nothing, since the manifest's Zod schema is what actually validates them. The comment now says that rather than promising a future fix. The registry's `no-explicit-any` exemption stays for the same reason, with the stale "Phase 2 will restore type safety" note corrected.
  - **E2E was not run this session** — `middleware.ts` needs `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL`/`ANON_KEY` on every route and `.env.local` is gitignored, so a fresh container cannot start the server for Playwright. Covered instead by the 65-page markup diff plus a manual run of the built server against placeholder credentials: all 13 public routes returned 200 with no `MissingBlock` placeholders.
- Header **hide-on-scroll** added as `lib/useHideOnScroll.ts` (owns both `scrolled` and `hidden`): slides the bar to `translateY(-100%)` on scroll-down past 120px, reveals on any scroll-up — the one motion `EXECUTION_PLAN.md §10` sanctions on top of the frost ("optional slide-away-on-scroll-down is fine"); the 72px height still never changes. The hook **ignores scroll while pinned** (drawer/search/bag/mega-menu open) and re-baselines on un-pin, because `useScrollLock`'s fixed-body technique moves the document scroll position — without that guard the unlock `scrollTo(0, savedY)` restore reads as a large scroll-down and hides the bar on every overlay close (it also stops the frost from dropping out while an overlay is open, which it previously did). Focus entering the header reveals it; the global reduced-motion rule already zeroes the 320ms slide.

- [x] **The public visual baselines hold cross-host — settled, not assumed.** They were captured in this dev container and this file flagged the risk that `ubuntu-latest` antialiasing would blow through `maxDiffPixelRatio: 0.01` on full-page shots of text-heavy editorial pages. On the first run that got far enough to find out, **all 14 passed**. The tolerance is right and the baselines are portable; no re-baselining needed
- [ ] **The four admin visual baselines have never been captured, and cannot be captured from a checkout.** Unlike the public shots they need a *signed-in* session, so a host must have both a database and an admin account before it can take the first picture: CI has both but cannot commit what it captures, and this container has neither. `tests/visual/admin.spec.ts` now **skips** each shot whose baseline is absent instead of writing an actual and failing — a permanently red gate that no push can fix is worse than a visible skip. The guard exempts `--update-snapshots`, so the run that creates the baselines is not blocked by their absence. **To close this:** start a local stack, provision an admin, `npm run test:visual -- --update-snapshots`, commit the PNGs. The gate arms itself the moment they land
- [ ] ⚠️ **"`template` needs an area switcher" understates it by a phase, and the switcher is the least of it.** `article` and `pattern` are done. `template` is not one slice, and the reason is not the builder:
  - **Nothing consumes a template.** Verified by search: no route, component or service imports `lib/services/templates`, and no render path reads `templates.published_data` or resolves a `template_assignments` row. `createPage` accepts an optional `templateId` and stores it on the row; nothing ever reads it back. **So a template editor built today would edit a document that changes no rendered page** — the same trap as synced patterns, and the same failure shape: it looks right in the admin and does nothing on the site.
  - **Areas are arbitrary**, `Record<string, BlockTree>`, with no vocabulary anywhere in the schema or the domain. So the UI is not a tab strip over known names — it has to create, rename and delete areas, which is a design decision nobody has made.
  - **`is_global` and `locked` describe builder behaviour that does not exist.** `0003`'s own comment says locking "makes the builder require an explicit unlock before editing".
  - **`template_assignments` has no UI**, and its resolution order (entry > taxonomy > content_type) is implemented in the repository and called by nothing.

  **The order that would make this real**: a render path that resolves and applies a template first, then the editor. Building the editor first produces exactly the inert screen this file keeps warning about. **Better next candidate: category pages** — `/[category]` already renders `categories.published_data` live and has no admin screen at all, so the editor there has an immediate visible effect.

**Phase 6b — Theme editor** ✅ (code) · ⚠️ integration and E2E unrun in this container
- [x] `supabase/migrations/0017_theme_settings_documents.sql` — **applied to the live project** via the Supabase MCP. Two changes in one file because they are one event (see below)
- [x] `lib/domain/theme.ts` (+57 tests) · `lib/db/repositories/theme.ts` · `lib/services/publicTheme.ts` · `lib/services/theme.ts` · `lib/demo/theme.ts` · `seedTheme()` in `scripts/seed.ts`
- [x] `/admin/theme` with a `ColorInput` added to `components/admin/ui/Input.tsx`; one line in `AdminShell`'s `NAV`
- [x] `tailwind.config.ts` — `mg.accent` becomes `rgb(var(--mg-accent-rgb) / <alpha-value>)`; `globals.css` gains `--mg-accent-rgb: 200 16 46`
- [x] **All 16 public visual baselines pass, at every step, with no `--update-snapshots`.** 697 unit tests, up from 639
- [ ] `tests/integration/publicTheme.test.ts` and `tests/e2e/theme.spec.ts` are **written and unrun here** — see the verification note below

  **`theme_settings` had the full document column set since `0007` and nothing had ever read it.** Same shape as products before `0014`: `draft_data` / `published_data` / `version` / `status` / `published_at` all present, and `document_table()` simply did not list `'theme'`. `0017` adds the one line, and every precondition was already met — `publish_document` touches only columns the table has, and `revisions`/`publish_events` key on `entity_type text` with **no CHECK constraint anywhere in the sixteen prior migrations**. Publish, unpublish, snapshot, rollback and history therefore all work with no further SQL.

  **The second half of `0017` closes a leak an editor would have armed.** `0007` left the read policy at `using (true)` while every other publishable table is `status = 'published' or is_staff()` — so an unpublished draft theme was anon-readable. Byte-for-byte the gap `0015` closed for `menu_items`, and harmless for the same reason: the row held an empty draft. The moment a theme is editable is the moment its draft is worth hiding, which is why both changes are in one migration rather than two.

  **`DOCUMENT_TYPES` deliberately stays at five, and this is worth not re-litigating.** `permissionFor` in `lib/services/documents.ts` builds `` `${type}.${action}` `` and narrows it to `Permission`. There is no `theme.delete`, so adding `"theme"` to the union is an immediate type error, and forcing it cascades into `DOCUMENT_TABLES`, `BLOCK_TREE_KEY`, `createPreview` and `isDocumentType` (which gates `media_usages` entity types). `lib/services/theme.ts` passes the string literal to the RPC instead, and `documents.test.ts` locks the decision with the reasoning attached. A side effect worth knowing: `resolve_preview` now resolves `'theme'` too, closed at the application layer only — `createPreview` takes a `DocumentType`, so nothing in the app can mint such a token, but a hand-inserted `preview_sessions` row would work.

  **⚠️ The obvious way to make the accent editable would have broken ~21 utilities silently, and the compiled CSS proved it rather than the docs.** Flipping `mg.accent` to `var(--mg-accent)` looks like a one-line change. Tailwind 3.4's `withAlphaValue` runs `parseColor` over the theme value and **drops the utility entirely** when it cannot parse one — and `var(--x)` never parses. The evidence was already in the build: `mg.accentSerif` has been a bare `var()` all along, and **all eight of its alpha-modified utilities compile to no CSS at all today** (`bg-mg-accentSerif/10`, `border-mg-accentSerif/40`, …). That is a real latent bug in the committed baselines, deliberately **not fixed here** — fixing it moves pixels on `/sign-in` and across the admin, so it needs its own slice and its own baseline decision.

  The channel form (`rgb(var(--mg-accent-rgb) / <alpha-value>)`) is pixel-neutral, and was verified as such rather than assumed: every accent rule body is character-identical to the previous build once the variable is substituted back, all 65 prerendered pages are byte-identical, and the 16 baselines are unmoved. **The general lesson: when a change is claimed to be equivalent, read the compiled output — this project's own build contained the counter-example.**

  **The two token asymmetries are the thing a naive implementation gets wrong.** `--mg-accent` is the only token `globals.css` never overrides, and `--mg-band-border` is deliberately absent from `[data-darkband]` because the hairline is keyed off the *page* theme. Emitting all nine tokens into all three contexts breaks both, and breaks them *silently* — the site still renders, it renders differently. `TOKENS_BY_CONTEXT` holds them as data with the reasons attached, and drives the emitter **and** the admin form, so the two cannot disagree about which tokens exist.

  **The test that actually protects the baselines reads `app/globals.css` and compares against it.** A hand-transcribed expectation would have been written from the same reading as the code and could be wrong in the same direction; parsing the real stylesheet makes the contract mechanical. It independently confirms both asymmetries — had `accent` been included in the light context, the declaration-for-declaration comparison would fail.

  **The strict/forgiving split runs in both directions, on purpose.** `saveThemeDraft` validates with the Zod schema because an editor needs to be told which field is wrong; `parseThemeColors` on the read path deliberately does **not**, because one bad token must not discard all twenty-five — a payload written by `scripts/seed.ts` or by SQL never passes through the form. This is the same split `lib/blocks/normalize.ts` and `validate.ts` already draw. The first version of this conflated them and two tests caught it.

  **`safeCssColor` is a security boundary, not formatting.** The block ships through `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` in the root layout, so an allowlist is what stops anyone holding `theme.write` storing `red}</style><script>…` and getting stored XSS on every page of the site. Applied on read as well as write.

  **The public read falls back where `publicContent.ts` and `publicNavigation.ts` throw**, and the difference is principled: the fallback is not invented (it is the CSS still compiled into `globals.css`), a throw in the **root** layout would take down `/admin` including the screen needed to repair the bad theme, and it is already the live state since `0007` seeds `published_data` NULL. Verified both ways — unreachable host and real project — defaults returned and the error logged.

  **Emitted from the root layout, not `(site)`.** The admin renders in `mg.*` tokens too, so a theme scoped to the public site would leave the Theme editor lying about the change its own editor just made. That placement makes it the highest-consequence instance of the `lib/db/public.ts` rule in the codebase: a `cookies()` call there would opt all 65 static pages out of static rendering, with no error and no failing test.

  **What the editor cannot reach, said in the UI rather than left to be discovered:** 62 hardcoded hex literals across 24 components — the always-dark footer, the hero scrims, the red CTA bands. They are colours already outside the token system, and bringing them in genuinely *does* move pixels.

  **✅ Verification — and CI closed the gaps this entry was first written around.** Locally: the four gates (697 unit tests), `npm run build`, the compiled-CSS and 65-page prerender diffs, `npm run test:visual` (16/16) at four separate points, and a direct probe of the fallback path against both an unreachable host and the live project. What could **not** run in this container — no Docker daemon for `npx supabase start`, and no service-role key, so `scripts/create-admin.ts` cannot provision the admin the signed-in specs need — **has now run in CI on PR #24**, and is no longer outstanding:

  - **Integration: green, and not vacuously.** `tests/integration/publicTheme.test.ts (7 tests) ✓`. That is the only place `document_table('theme')` is proven to resolve, that `revisions`/`publish_events` accept an entity type with no CHECK behind it, and that an actor holding `theme.write` but not `theme.publish` is refused with `42501`. The same job's `Migrations are idempotent` step re-applied all seventeen migrations onto themselves, so `0017` is proven re-runnable rather than believed so.
  - **`seedTheme()` has executed** — the seed step logs `theme: 3 contexts`. Still **not** run against the live project `qnfoztnyxhubnnulpfwt`: the Supabase MCP refused every call after the migration was applied, including read-only ones, exactly as in the `0015` session, and per that session's recorded lesson it was not negotiated with. Nothing depends on it: the live row holds `{}`, which `parseThemeColors` maps to the defaults, so site and admin behave identically seeded or not.
  - **E2E: green**, after two rounds of locator fixes — the first execution these six specs have ever had. See the gotcha below; both failures were the spec's, not the feature's.
  - **Visual: 16/16 in CI on three consecutive runs**, including the two where E2E was red. That is the gate this whole phase was designed around, and it has now passed on a machine that is not this container.

  ⚠️ **This paragraph previously said the integration suite, the E2E specs and `seedTheme()` were all unrun.** True when written, false within the hour. Left as a correction rather than a silent rewrite because "the doc was true when written" is exactly how this file goes stale.

  **Extending this beyond colours — the map, so the next session does not re-derive it.** Colours came first because they are the **only** design tokens that are already CSS variables; the constraint is upstream of the editor, in the stylesheet. The payload was built to grow: `themePayloadSchema` is `{ version?, colors? }` with `.passthrough()` contexts, so a `typography` or `spacing` group is additive with no migration and no `db:types` regeneration, and `0007`'s own comment already sketches the ten-group destination. The recipe is the one Phase 1 proved: **(1)** introduce the CSS variable holding today's exact literal and prove *that* pixel-neutral — compiled CSS diff, prerender diff, 16 baselines; **(2)** then add the token to the payload, seeded with the same value so the emitted block is a no-op. Cost tiers: *cheap* — anything living in one `globals.css` rule (`.container-mg`'s 1320px column and gutters, `.mg-underline`'s thickness/offset/timing, the 85px scrim gradient, the drop-cap); *moderate* — overlay durations and motion timings, spread across components but few; *expensive* — the type scale, which is Tailwind classes on hundreds of elements plus ~18 `!important` overrides in two media queries; *out of reach* — font **families**, because `next/font` resolves at build time and Space Grotesk's omitted `weight` is load-bearing for its variable axis.

  - [ ] No Theme history screen. `listHistory` / `listRevisions` are typed `DocumentType`, so a UI needs those two signatures widened to `DocumentType | "theme"` — narrow and type-checked, touching neither `DOCUMENT_TYPES` nor `DOCUMENT_TABLES`. **History rows are written from now on regardless**, so the UI can follow later without having lost anything.

- **Phase 6b — XML ingestion (this session).** The last unstarted feature in Track B, and the **first slice in the project's history that needed no migration**: `0005_commerce.sql` and `0006_ingestion.sql` built `product_sources`, `feed_field_mappings`, `import_jobs` and `import_items` two phases before anything wrote to them, and everything the pipeline needed was already there — including `products_source_external_uniq`, `products.content_hash`, and the three `integration.*` permissions seeded by `0001`. `fast-xml-parser` had been an unused dependency since the scaffold, and the 0006 header's example paths (`item/title`, `@_sku`) are written in its attribute convention, so even the library choice was two phases old. The lesson worth keeping: **the schema was designed ahead of the code and it held.** Nothing had to be widened, and the one place the notes promised a path (`lib/integrations/commerce` for the transforms) is where they went.

  - **The shape: a run writes nothing to the catalogue.** It fetches, maps, compares and stages; `import_items` is a proposal and `applyJob` is the only function that touches `products`. That is `0006`'s own stated intent and it is what makes a wrong mapping cost a rejected job rather than a corrupted store.
  - **Three refusals built into the write path, each worth more than it cost.** An imported product lands as a **draft** — `columnsForApply` never writes `status`, because a feed that can reach the live store without a human can take it down without one. An update **never writes the slug**, because the slug is the PDP's URL and a merchant renaming "Oxford Shoe" to "Oxford Shoe (New)" would otherwise move a live page and break every link the SEO phase just earned. And nothing writes `draft_data` — the block tree is editorial work a sync must not discard.
  - **The pounds-vs-pence guard is the single most valuable thing here.** A feed quoting `145.00` means £145; read as pence it is £1.45, on a live storefront, with **nothing anywhere reporting an error**. So `coerceValue` refuses a bare decimal in any pence target and names the transform that fixes it, and the XML parser is configured with `parseTagValue: false` so the decimal point still exists by the time that check runs. With value parsing on — fast-xml-parser's default — `<price>145.00</price>` arrives as the number `145` and the guard never fires. Both halves are tested, and the adapter test asserts the coupling directly rather than the two properties separately.
  - **A bug the tests found before the feature ever ran.** `diffFields` and `columnsForApply` disagreed about `external_id`: the write skipped it (it is the dedupe key, owned by the repository) while the diff did not, so **every update proposed a change to a column it was never going to make**. A reviewer would have approved it and seen nothing happen. Fixed by giving both one shared `writtenOnUpdate` predicate, with a test asserting the diff is a subset of what an update writes — the drift, not just the instance.
  - **⚠️ The build catches what nothing else does, again.** `lib/domain/ingestion.ts` imported `node:crypto` for the content hash, and the client mapping editor imports that module for its vocabularies — so webpack refused the build with `UnhandledSchemeError: Reading from "node:crypto" is not handled by plugins`. **`tsc`, ESLint and all 791 unit tests passed**, because Vitest runs in Node and resolves it happily. Fixed by moving the one crypto-dependent function to `lib/domain/contentHash.ts`, which only the service imports. The general rule, now written in that file's header: *a `lib/domain` module reachable from a client component may not touch a Node built-in.* `lib/domain/jobs.ts` has done so since Phase 6b and never had the problem — the difference is the importer, not the module. This is the third distinct instance of `CLAUDE.md`'s "run `npm run build` before anything that touches routing".
  - **A security rule that came out of writing the credential path.** `product_sources.credentials_ref` names an environment variable, and resolving it means indexing `process.env` with **a string an operator typed into a form** — so without a restriction, someone holding `integration.write` could point a source at `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` and have the run send it, as a bearer token, to a URL they also chose. `resolveCredential` therefore refuses any name not matching `^FEED_[A-Z0-9_]+$`. It costs nothing (a feed credential is added to the deployment by hand anyway) and it is tested against that exact attack.
  - **And it makes one line of the env-checker's note false.** `scripts/check-env.mjs`'s entry below says a dynamic `process.env[name]` is invisible to it and that "nothing does that today". Something does now, and it is the only such read in the repository. The mitigation is the naming rule rather than a declaration, because a feed credential belongs to a *source row* and has no fixed name `.env.example` could carry; `.env.example` now explains that in a comment where someone adding a feed will meet it.
  - **What was verified here, and what was not.** Four gates green (791 unit tests, up 152). `npm run build` clean — the three new routes are `ƒ` like every admin route, and **no public route changed rendering mode**. All **16 visual baselines pass**, which is the honest check that an admin-only slice stayed admin-only. The admin visual baselines are still the four skips they have always been — none has ever been captured — so adding a nav item moved no picture. **The 5 new E2E specs had never executed** in this container: signing in needs an admin account, `scripts/create-admin.ts` needs the service-role key, and this container has not had one since it passed through a chat transcript.

    ⚠️ **This paragraph ended "CI is their first run, and given this file records the products phase and the theme phase each losing a CI round to locator traps, expect to spend one." That prediction was wrong.** CI on PR #26 was **green on all three jobs on the first run**, and the E2E job reports **`48 passed`, nothing skipped** — so the five new specs genuinely executed rather than quietly opting out, which is the failure mode this file has caught three times elsewhere. Left as a correction rather than a silent rewrite, for the reason the theme entry gives: "the doc was true when written" is exactly how this file goes stale.

    **What made the difference is worth keeping, because it is repeatable.** The two traps on this screen were identified from `PROGRESS.md` *before* the spec was written, not after CI failed: "Integrations" is both a nav link and the page eyebrow (so the nav locator is scoped to `getByRole("navigation")`), and the row-level "Delete" shares its name with the confirmation dialog's (so both are scoped through `getByRole("dialog")`). That is the "grep the handoff before concluding a trap is novel" rule from the gotchas list, applied in advance for once instead of in hindsight.
  - **The E2E spec deliberately never runs an import.** There is no URL a CI job may honestly request: a real merchant feed makes a stranger's server part of the suite, and a fixture served from the test process would be testing Playwright's ability to serve a file. The adapter is driven over a fixture feed with an injected `fetch` in `xmlFeed.test.ts` instead — the same injected-dependency stance `lib/blocks/binding.ts` takes to its sources, and the reason that layer takes its `fetch` as an argument at all.
  - **Unchanged records are counted, not staged.** `import_jobs.unchanged_count` exists for exactly this; writing one `import_items` row per unchanged product would grow the table by the size of the catalogue every night to record that nothing happened.

- **Phase 6b — the Shopify adapter (this session).** The second adapter, over the **Admin REST API** with a plain `fetch` and no SDK, and the slice that tested whether the ingestion phase's central bet paid off. It did: `lib/services/ingestion.ts` gained **one line** and `index.ts` one registry entry. `product_sources.kind` has permitted `'shopify'` since `0005`, so **no migration** — the second slice running to have needed none.

  - **The adapter was the cheap half, and this is the finding worth carrying.** Six of the eleven changed files are admin UI. `SourcesList.tsx` hard-coded `kind: "xml_feed"`, the create action's Zod schema required `url` and `itemPath` from *every* kind, and `[id]/page.tsx` parsed every source through `xmlFeedConfigSchema` unconditionally — so a Shopify source could not be created, and had one existed, opening its edit screen and saving would have written an XML-shaped config over it. None of that was visible while there was one adapter. **An interface can be genuinely provider-agnostic while every screen above it is still shaped like the first provider**, and nothing fails until the second one arrives. Both create and edit are now `z.discriminatedUnion("kind", …)`.
  - **The edit form must send every field of its arm.** `updateSource` replaces `product_sources.config` wholesale, so a field the form omits comes back from `parseSourceConfig` as its schema default. A Shopify config edited through a form that did not render `page_size` would silently reset it to 250. Written into the schema's own comment, because the failure is invisible and the next person adding a config field will meet it.
  - **Two security decisions, both about the same fact: the token rides in a custom header.** `fetch` strips `Authorization`, `Cookie` and `Proxy-Authorization` on a cross-origin redirect and **strips nothing else** — so `X-Shopify-Access-Token` would follow a 3xx to any host that asked. Hence `redirect: "manual"`. The same reasoning applies to pagination, which Shopify drives through a `Link` header: that header is remote input, so the `rel="next"` URL is parsed and its host checked against the configured `shop_domain` before it is followed. Refusing costs nothing, and the alternative hands a merchant's Admin API token to whoever wrote the header. Both are tested.
  - **The `Link` URL is used verbatim**, because Shopify refuses `page_info` combined with any other filter — rebuilding it from the config would drop the cursor or be rejected outright. And a run is bounded by `max_pages` (default 20 × 250 = 5,000 products), because a run holds a server action open for its whole duration; an unbounded walk over a large merchant's catalogue arrives as a timeout that explains nothing.
  - **The pounds-vs-pence guard survived the provider change, and that is not luck.** Shopify returns money as the JSON string `"145.00"` and `JSON.parse` preserves it, which is exactly what `parseTagValue: false` buys the XML adapter. `coerceValue` recognises pounds by the decimal point, so a price arriving as the number `145` would import a £145 jacket at £1.45 with nothing reporting an error. `shopify.test.ts` asserts the **coupling** — `expect(price).toBe("145.00")` beside `expect(coerceValue("price_pence", price).ok).toBe(false)` — rather than the two properties separately, copying `xmlFeed.test.ts`. The file header says in as many words: do not add numeric coercion here.
  - **Shared helpers were extracted rather than duplicated**, deliberately, with the XML adapter's 16 existing tests as the check that nothing moved. `http.ts` now holds the streamed 20 MB cap, the timeout and the fetch error mapping; `paths.ts` holds the slash-path `walk`. Messages are parameterised by a `subject` string so the XML adapter's wording is byte-identical to before. The argument against was that it edits a file which shipped one PR earlier; the argument for is `writtenOnUpdate` — this repository already lost a bug to two copies of one rule drifting apart, and a byte cap protecting the container's memory is not a rule worth keeping two of.
  - ⚠️ **One behaviour change came with the shared `walk`**: a numeric segment now indexes an array, so `variants/0/price` reads as "the first variant's price". It cannot affect an XML mapping — an XML element name may not begin with a digit, so a numeric segment matched nothing before and was already dead — but it is a change to newly shared code and is recorded rather than left for someone to find.
  - **Both adapters read the same slash-separated paths**, though JSON's native convention is dots. The mapping editor's help text, every path already stored in `feed_field_mappings` and the XML adapter all speak slashes; a per-provider separator would mean one editor teaching two syntaxes. What is *not* shared is the leaf: XML's `#text` unwrapping belongs to its parser, and a JSON leaf is already its own value.
  - **A test was inverted on purpose.** `credentials.test.ts` asserted that `adapterFor("shopify")` *throws* — the whole point of this slice is that it no longer does. Flagged because a suite that goes green after an assertion is inverted deserves one deliberate look: `adapterFor`'s refusal branch is still reached by any kind added to `PRODUCT_SOURCE_KINDS` without an adapter, which is what it was really guarding.
  - **What was verified.** Locally: four gates green — **820 unit tests, up 29** — `npm run build` clean with the three integrations routes still `ƒ` and **no public route changing rendering mode**, and **all 16 visual baselines passing**, the honest check that an admin-only slice stayed admin-only.

    **CI on PR #26's successor, PR #28, then closed the gaps this container could not: all three jobs green on the first run.** `Integration tests` re-applied all **17** migrations onto themselves (`Migrations are idempotent`) and passed. `E2E & visual regression` reports **`Running 49 tests` → `49 passed`, nothing skipped** — so the new Shopify spec genuinely executed rather than quietly opting out, which is the failure mode this file has now caught four times elsewhere. Visual held at 16 passed / 4 skipped (the admin baselines, never captured). **Merged to `main` as `6ed0447`.**

    ⚠️ **What CI did *not* prove, and cannot: nothing has ever run against a real Shopify store.** There is no shop domain or Admin API token available, and there deliberately will not be one in the suite — it would make a merchant's account part of CI. `fetchRecords` is exercised only over fixtures with an injected `fetch`. Pagination, the caps, the refusals and the header are tested; **the assumption that Shopify's payload actually looks like the fixture is not**. Two green suites are easy to read as "it works", so it is worth being exact: what is proven is that the pipeline, the UI and the specs behave correctly *given* that shape. The first real run is the test that matters and it has not happened.
