    The ingestion phase's equivalent paragraph predicted a lost CI round and was wrong. **No prediction was offered here**, and the outcome was green on the first run again.

    ⚠️ **And then the very next CI run went red with byte-identical application code** — the docs-only follow-up PR #29, which changes nothing but this file. So the sentence that stood here for about ten minutes ("starting to look like the locator discipline rather than luck") was too pleased with itself and is corrected rather than deleted. Two green runs were partly discipline and partly luck; the suite had a live race in it the whole time. See the `page.reload()` gotcha below.

- **Password recovery (this session).** `/sign-in` had no way out for anyone who did not know their password, and the account this project runs on had reached exactly that state. Now: a **"Forgot your password?"** link → `/forgot-password` → an emailed link → `/auth/callback` → **`/admin/password`**, already signed in, where a new one is set.

  - **`/auth/callback` needed no changes at all.** Phase 1c built it "ahead of the flows that need it, so adding *forgot password* later is configuration rather than new plumbing", including a `next` parameter restricted to same-origin paths. That prediction was exactly right two phases later — the recovery landing is `\`${origin}/auth/callback?next=/admin/password\`` and the route already exchanged the code and honoured the redirect.
  - ⚠️ **`redirectTo` is passed per request, and the project's Site URL is deliberately *not* used as the mechanism.** This is the trap that prompted the work: a recovery email built from Supabase's global **Site URL** setting sent the operator to `http://localhost:3000` — the project default, never changed, because nothing had used an email flow before. Setting Site URL to the Railway origin is not the fix either: the link would land on `/`, the public homepage, carrying a `?code=` that nothing there exchanges, so the visitor arrives *successfully* and is still signed out. Per-request `redirectTo` has neither problem. **It must still be added to Authentication → URL Configuration → Redirect URLs**, which is what keeps it from being an open redirect.
  - **The password page lives at `/admin/password`, and the location is forced.** `middleware.ts` redirects a signed-in visitor away from `/sign-in`, and a recovery callback leaves them signed in — so anything hosted beside the sign-in form would bounce to `/admin` before they could type. Under `/admin` it also inherits the layout's session gate and needs no gate of its own. It requires **no permission**: changing your own password is not an editorial capability.
  - ~~**No current-password check, deliberately**~~ — **there is one now, and it is conditional.** The tension this paragraph described is real and does not go away: `/admin/password` is where a recovery link lands, and someone arriving from one does not know their old password, so an unconditional check would not harden recovery — it would delete it. What resolves it is distinguishing *how the session was obtained*. `/auth/callback` is the only route that exchanges a one-time code, and a code only reaches a browser through a link Supabase emailed to the account's own address; a session minted there has proved something stronger than a password, and it is marked with a short-lived `httpOnly` cookie. Every other session gets asked. The marker is spent on the first successful change. **GoTrue's reauthentication nonce remains the stronger control and remains open** — see Known issues.
  - **The 72-byte ceiling in `lib/domain/passwords.ts` is the part most likely to be "tidied" away.** GoTrue hashes with bcrypt, **which truncates at 72 bytes and says nothing** — a longer passphrase is stored as its first 72 bytes and the tail protects nothing, in a way that never surfaces because the account still works. It is measured in **bytes, not characters**, so a 20-character emoji passphrase (80 bytes) crosses it while `length` says otherwise; that is a test rather than a comment. The minimum is 12 rather than Supabase's default 6, on the grounds that six is a default nobody revisits on a console with write access to a live storefront.
  - ⚠️ **The new E2E specs lost a CI round to two locator traps, and the fix worth keeping is not the locators.** Both failures were the spec's, not the feature's: `getByLabel("New password")` matched **two** fields, because Playwright's label matching is substring-by-default and "Confirm new password" contains it; and the "Password" link was scoped to `getByRole("navigation")` when it deliberately sits in the rail's *account footer*, outside the `<nav>`. A third was waiting behind them — `/at least 12 characters/i` matches the field's own help text as well as the error.
    - **The repeatable fix: the assumptions a spec makes about markup can be tested where the spec cannot run.** `/admin/password`'s E2E cannot execute in a fresh container (no service-role key, so no admin account), which is exactly how these shipped unverified. `app/(admin)/admin/password/PasswordForm.test.tsx` and `components/admin/AdminShell.test.tsx` now assert the same facts in jsdom on every save: the accessible names, that a loose label match really does hit two fields, that the error sentence is distinguishable from the help, and that exactly one "Password" link exists and is **not** inside the nav. **This is the answer to "the traps are in the half we cannot run"** — and it is cheaper than a CI round.
    - ⚠️ **A required field's label text is not its accessible name, and neither a plain string nor `exact` locates it.** `FieldShell` appends the asterisk in an `aria-hidden` span, so the accessible name is `New password` while the label's rendered text is `New password *`. `getByLabel("New password")` matches **two** fields (substring, and "Confirm new password" contains it); `getByLabel("New password", { exact: true })` matches **zero**, because the asterisk is part of what Playwright compares. **An anchored regex — `getByLabel(/^New password/)` — is the locator that works**, and it does not depend on the asterisk being there.
      - ⚠️ **This bullet previously said the opposite and was wrong: that Playwright matches the accessible name and therefore excludes the asterisk.** The jsdom guard written from that theory asserted `toHaveAccessibleName`, passed, and guarded nothing — CI then found `exact: true` matching zero elements, which is only possible if the asterisk counts. Left as a correction because **a guard that asserts the wrong property is worse than no guard: it reads as covered.** The guard now asserts the label *text*, including that the exact string finds nothing.
      - The general form, and the reason it took two rounds: **`exact` looks like the fix for an ambiguous label and is not.** Ambiguity and the asterisk are different problems, and the fix for the first makes the second fatal.
    - And `typecheck` caught `exact: true` being passed to Testing Library's `getByRole`, which has no such option — **while the tests were passing**. A silently-ignored option is exactly the kind of thing that makes a guard weaker than it looks.
  - **No E2E test completes a password change, on purpose.** Every signed-in spec authenticates with `E2E_ADMIN_PASSWORD`; a test that succeeded would pass and then break every spec after it, including itself on retry. The specs assert the refusals — which is where the logic is — and the last one signs out and back in to prove the credential still works, so a regression says so immediately instead of surfacing three files later.
  - ⚠️ **And building it uncovered a live production bug that had nothing to do with passwords: `/auth/callback` was redirecting to `https://localhost:8080`.** Probed with `curl` against the deployed site rather than reasoned about — `Location: https://localhost:8080/sign-in?error=missing_code`, straight from Railway. Behind a proxy a route handler's `request.nextUrl` is the **container's own address**, not the browser's. So the recovery link would have verified, exchanged the code, and then bounced the operator to an unreachable host — landing on the identical "localhost refused to connect" the whole slice existed to fix, for a completely different reason.
    - **The old comment had the right diagnosis and the wrong remedy.** Both auth routes carried a note saying `new URL(..., request.url)` "can carry the internal origin behind a proxy" and used `request.nextUrl.clone()` instead. On Railway **both** are the internal origin, so the fix never worked; it just moved the bug somewhere less suspicious. That note is now in the gotchas list below with the evidence attached.
    - **Middleware is fine, and that is what made it look impossible.** Its `nextUrl` reflects the original request, so the sign-in gate has always redirected to the correct public host — visible side by side in the same probe: `/admin/password` → the real origin, `/auth/callback` → `localhost:8080`.
    - `app/auth/_lib/publicUrl.ts` honours `x-forwarded-host` **only when it matches `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL`'s host**, because that header is attacker-supplied in principle and trusting it blindly turns both auth routes into open redirects. With no forwarded header it falls back to `nextUrl`, which is correct locally and in the E2E suite. Six tests reproduce the live failure, including the port trap: the URL `host` setter keeps an existing port when the new host omits one, which would have produced `…railway.app:8080`.
    - **No test in this repository could have caught it, and that is the honest lesson.** Unit tests, the build, the E2E suite and the visual baselines all run on a single host with no proxy in front. The bug only exists where the app is actually deployed. **A `curl` against the live URL found it in one request** — cheap, and worth doing after any change to a redirect.
    - ✅ **Confirmed fixed in production after PR #29 merged (`74fbd5d`)**, by the same probe that found it:

      ```
      /forgot-password            200
      /auth/callback              307 -> https://…up.railway.app/sign-in?error=missing_code
      /auth/callback?code=bogus   307 -> https://…up.railway.app/sign-in?error=invalid_code
      ```

      The public host in both, where it was `https://localhost:8080` before. The third probe is the one worth copying: a deliberately bogus `code` drives the **real exchange path** — `exchangeCodeForSession` fails and the route redirects — rather than only the missing-code guard, and it needs no valid token to run. `x-forwarded-host` is therefore confirmed to be what Railway sends, which was the assumption the fix rested on and the only part of it no test could check.
  - **Verified:** four gates green (**835 unit tests**, up 15), `npm run build` clean with `/forgot-password` static and `/admin/password` dynamic, **16/16 visual baselines unmoved** (the sign-in page gained a link and is not a baseline), and 55 E2E specs collected, up 6. The email round trip itself is **not** covered by the suite — proving a link arrives tests GoTrue, not this app — and deliberately never will be.

    ✅ **It has, however, been run once by hand, and it worked.** After `74fbd5d` deployed, the operator went through `/forgot-password` → Supabase email → `/auth/callback` → `/admin/password` and arrived signed in. That closes the last link in the chain nothing here could prove, and it is also what confirmed the two Supabase settings the repository structurally cannot check: **Site URL** at the bare origin, and `<origin>/**` in **Redirect URLs** — configuration `scripts/check-env.mjs` cannot see, because it is not an environment variable.

    ⚠️ **Do not read that as coverage.** One human run is evidence of a different kind from a test: it says the path worked once, on that day, with that configuration. **Nothing watches it.** A change to `redirectTo`, to the callback's `next` handling, or to either Supabase setting would break recovery silently and no job would go red. If that matters later, the thing to build is a check against Supabase's local mail server in the integration suite — not another E2E spec, since the real inbox is the part that cannot be automated honestly.

- **A deliberate departure from the design baseline, decided but not yet acted on.** Slice 3 above — the layout engine — was chosen as **arbitrary nesting with per-breakpoint control**, over a constrained grid built only from existing tokens. That choice **invents design vocabulary the handoff bundle does not contain**, and `design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/CLAUDE.md` forbids exactly that: *"Infer breakpoints only from the prototypes/screenshots — do not invent your own."* `05_SECTION_BUILDER.md` says the opposite of a layout primitive too — *"full-bleed vs. contained is a per-block concern — the renderer just stacks them."*

  - **The override is written into `design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/CLAUDE.md` itself, not only here**, and that placement is the point. A session doing visual work is instructed to read that file first; had the exception lived only in this document, a session following the rules correctly would reach the prohibition and stop — and be right to. **An override recorded away from the rule it overrides is not an override, it is a contradiction the next session has to adjudicate.**
  - **What is *not* overridden:** the tokens, the type scale, the 1320px column, the motion timings, and the sixteen pixel-verified baselines. The exception is scoped to a new layout block's own responsive behaviour. Everything that exists today still has to render byte-identically.
  - Recorded before the work starts rather than after, so nobody later reads the first invented breakpoint as drift.

- **Drag-from-library (builder slices 1 and 2).** The scoping session's three findings all survived contact: `store.insert(type, at?)` needed no change, `DndContext` had to come out of `Canvas.tsx` and out of the empty-tree ternary, and explicit `gap:N` droppables beat inferring an index from a hovered block's midpoint. What follows is what the scoping did **not** know.

  - ⚠️ **Click and drag coexist on one control only because of the 6px activation constraint, and that is now load-bearing.** The library entries are buttons that insert on click and drag sources for the same press. A stationary press never activates a drag, so the click lands; once a drag *has* activated, dnd-kit adds a **capture-phase `click` listener on the document** that stops propagation before React's root sees it, so a completed drop inserts one block rather than two. Remove or loosen the constraint and every click becomes a zero-distance drag that inserts nothing. **Found by a failing test, not by reading**: `InsertMenu.test.tsx` was first written against a bare `<DndContext>`, whose default sensors carry no constraint, and the click test failed exactly that way. The harness now configures the same constraint the builder does, and says why.
  - ⚠️ **The `KeyboardSensor`'s activator is deliberately not wired to the library entries.** `useDraggable`'s `listeners` bundle every registered sensor's activator, including an `onKeyDown` that claims Enter and Space and calls `preventDefault`. Spreading it would have converted the rail's keyboard path from *insert* to *drag* — removing the only accessible way to add a section, with nothing failing anywhere. Only `onPointerDown` is taken, and dnd-kit's `attributes` are skipped with it, so the entries are not announced as draggable. There is a test whose whole job is to press Enter and assert an insert.
  - **Droppables that mount mid-drag are measured.** The gaps are rendered only while a library item is in flight — that is what keeps block reordering unaffected, since a gap is then never a candidate `over` target for a canvas drag. This works because dnd-kit re-measures when its droppable-container list changes (`useDroppableMeasuring`, `containersRef.current !== containers`), which was read out of `node_modules` rather than assumed.
  - **The canvas takes the drag state as props rather than reading `useDndContext`.** It keeps the rendering rule a function of its inputs, so "one gap before each block and one after the last" is assertable in jsdom, where no drag can be simulated at all.

  - ⚠️ **A scripted drag can only drop onto a gap that is already in the viewport, and this is a test constraint rather than a bug.** A gap below the fold is attached and has a box, and dropping onto it silently changes nothing: Playwright jumps to the coordinates and releases, giving dnd-kit's autoscroll — which is what carries a *human* down a long page — no time to run. Found by driving the real thing: dropping onto the fourth gap of a three-section page left the count unchanged, while the same drop with the gap in view worked. The E2E spec therefore keeps its targets near the top of the canvas and says why.
  - **How that was found matters more than the finding.** The E2E suite **cannot run in a session container** — it needs admin credentials and a service-role key that no container has — so the alternative to shipping the spec unrun was a throwaway harness: an uncommitted route mounting `<Builder>` with fake actions (the builder needs no database for a drag; persistence arrives as injected callbacks), driven by a plain Playwright script at the E2E project's own 1280×720 viewport. Every ordering assertion in the spec was confirmed there before being written down — the empty-canvas drop, the trailing gap, the leading gap, and click-to-insert adding exactly one block. **Deleted afterwards.** This is worth reusing whenever a spec cannot be run: the harness proves the mechanism, CI proves the journey.
  - **Verified:** four gates green (**863 unit tests**, up 19), `npm run build` clean at 73/73 prerendered pages, and **16/16 visual baselines unmoved** — which is the assertion that matters most, since the builder is admin-only and any movement would mean something leaked into the public render path. Then **CI green on PR #32, all three jobs on the first run**, with **58/58 E2E and nothing skipped**.
  - **The count is the assertion, not the green tick.** These specs skip themselves when the admin credentials are absent, so "the E2E job passed" and "the drag ran" are different claims — a job with three silently skipped specs is indistinguishable from a job that exercised them. This is the same shape as the `format:check` trap recorded above: **a check that is real, green, and simply never looked at the thing.** Read the spec count on any PR that touches E2E.
  - The plan asked for **green twice** before trusting the suite's first real drag. The merge re-runs the same specs, so the second execution is the `push` run on `9d87568` — **read its outcome from Actions rather than from here**, because a run's result is exactly the kind of perishable fact this file should not carry. What makes the first green believable rather than lucky is the harness run that preceded it.

- **The layout engine (builder slice 3).** The riskiest work in the project so far, and the first to depart from the design baseline on purpose. Split into two commits — data and render, then the builder — so the pixel-neutral half could be proved before anything editable moved.

  - **The technique that made it safe is the one the theme editor established:** change nothing observable first, prove it, then build on top. `SectionRenderer` recurses **only for a manifest that declares a slot**, so every existing block is called with the same element and the same props as before — and that let the deep-compare be an assertion rather than a hope. **All 66 prerendered pages came out byte-identical**, twice: after the render change, and again after the builder work.
  - **`BlockSlot` sits beside `fields`, not in it.** A field holding blocks would have put children in `settings`, routing them around `walkBlocks` — and with it around publish validation, media extraction, diffing and binding, all four of which already recursed and would have silently stopped covering the nested half of a tree. It is singular by construction, because `children` is one array; **named areas remain a different shape and a different problem**, which is why `BLOCK_TREE_KEY.template` is still `null`.
  - **Declaring a slot is what makes children legal at all.** `validateBlock` refuses children on a manifest without one, so the other twenty-two blocks are *provably* leaves rather than incidentally childless — a tree that nests inside a `pullQuote` was built by something that ignored the manifests, and now says so.
  - ⚠️ **Three traps, and every one of them was silent.**
    - **The canvas's `[&_button]:pointer-events-none` is a descendant selector.** Left on a container it reaches every nested block's own toolbar and disables its drag, lock, duplicate and delete controls — and a `pointer-events-auto` utility on those buttons *cannot* win, because the arbitrary variant's specificity is higher. Applied to leaves only now.
    - **Clicking a nested block selected its container.** Every frame carries the same `onMouseDown`, so the click bubbled and the ancestor's handler ran last. The panel showed the wrong block's fields with nothing thrown. `stopPropagation` makes the innermost frame win.
    - **`findDraft` is deliberately a second recursion rather than a reuse of `findBlock`.** A draft's nodes are `Draft<BlockNode>`; writing to what `findBlock` returned inside a `produce` mutates a copy immer never finalises, so the edit vanishes with **no error at all**. Field edits stay inside `produce` (coalescing depends on it); structural edits go through the pure path in `tree.ts`.
  - **`tree.ts`'s two invariants are load-bearing, not stylistic.** Every function is non-mutating and returns the *original reference* when nothing changed. `commit` reads an unchanged tree as a no-op and records no history entry — which is what keeps a refused move out of the undo stack — and undo holds whole previous trees cheaply *because* untouched branches are shared. One function mutating in place would corrupt the undo stack silently, every entry pointing at the same arrays.
  - **`moveByKey` refuses to drop a container into its own subtree**, and that is a data-loss guard rather than a nicety: the move detaches before re-inserting, so the destination would cease to exist mid-operation and the whole branch would go with it.
  - **The store enforces no slot policy, deliberately.** `insert` puts a block into whatever container it is handed and `validateTree` reports the breach — the same division the store already keeps for field values, and it is what stops the manifests leaking into it. The UI only offers slots that exist.
  - **The design override was used as sparingly as it could be.** `columns` may invent breakpoints and does not: `stackAt` offers the site's own 680/820/1024. Licence to invent is not a reason to, and a fourth breakpoint would read as a layout changing where nothing else on the page does.
  - **Verified:** four gates (**923 unit tests**, up 61), `npm run build` clean at 73/73, **66/66 prerendered pages byte-identical**, **16/16 baselines unmoved**, and the whole nesting flow driven in a real browser against a throwaway harness — container onto an empty page, a section dragged into it, the nested toolbar clicked, the nested block selected and edited.
  - **Then CI ran it, and it took four rounds to settle.** Every round found something real, and **none of it was the nesting** — which is worth stating plainly, because "the E2E spec for the feature keeps failing" reads like the feature is broken. What it actually found, in order:
    1. **The spec depended on an unsaved block from the test before it.** The preceding test inserts by click and never saves; autosave is debounced, so whether that block was still there was a race the spec had bet on. Fixed by counting *relative* to whatever is present, and by waiting for the canvas before counting at all — the log showed the locator resolving to 0 elements mid-hydration, which would have poisoned every later assertion while looking like a nesting bug.
    2. **A container's toolbar could swallow its children's clicks.** Every frame pins its controls to its own top-right with a flat `z-10`, and `group-hover` fires for *every* ancestor group, so both are on screen and their corners can overlap. Toolbars now stack by depth.
    3. **The section preview was covering the canvas** — see its own entry below. It surfaced here as two more flaky specs and one hard failure in `media.spec.ts`, none of them in code that had changed.
    4. ⚠️ **The first click after a drop is swallowed, in the product as well as the tests.** dnd-kit adds a capture-phase `click` listener on the document when a drag activates — the mechanism that stops a completed drag from also firing the library entry's click and inserting twice — and `detach()` removes it on a `setTimeout(…, 50)`. So for 50ms after any drop, **every click anywhere is discarded**, including one on a block's own toolbar. It looks like a click that worked and did nothing: Playwright reports success, the handler never runs, and only the next assertion fails. The drag helper now waits the window out. **This is real for a user too** — drop a section, click its toolbar inside 50ms, and nothing happens. It is the cost of the double-insert guard, so it is recorded rather than worked around.

- **Section previews in the library rail.** Hovering or focusing an entry renders the section beside the rail, so a block can be recognised without inserting it and undoing.

  - **It renders the real component, not a picture of one.** `BlockCatalogEntry` has carried an unused `thumb?: string` since Phase 4 — the screenshot route — and this deliberately does not take it. A thumbnail is a second copy of a block's appearance that drifts from its component the moment anyone edits it, and it drifts **silently**: nothing can fail when a picture stops resembling its subject. This repo has been bitten by a second copy of the truth often enough that the choice was not close.
  - **The scale transform is what makes it both affordable and honest.** The section lays out at 1440px and is shrunk, so it shows the desktop layout rather than reflowing into a 230px rail and previewing its *mobile* breakpoint — a different kind of lie. One preview is rendered at a time, on demand; mounting twenty-three live sections would make opening the library the slowest thing in the builder.
  - **Hover *and* focus.** The keyboard is this rail's accessible path — the drag deliberately does not claim Enter — so a preview only a mouse could reach would be a feature built for half its users. The panel is `aria-hidden`: a whole section's markup in the accessibility tree would bury the label and description that actually describe the block.
  - **A container previews with the canvas's own empty-slot placeholder.** A freshly inserted `columns` has no children, so rendering it honestly shows an empty grid. The placeholder is not invented content — it is the state an editor lands in — and it is drawn heavier than the canvas's because at 0.29 scale a 1px hairline lands on a fraction of a pixel and vanishes.
  - ⚠️ **It shipped a bug that blocked the canvas, and the lesson is where it was found.** `pointer-events-none` was on the preview box but **not on the fixed wrapper around it**. The panel sits over the canvas and stays open while the pointer rests on an entry — which is exactly where click-to-insert leaves it — so with a preview open, every click on the section underneath hit the wrapper. One missing class; three failing specs, all in `media.spec.ts`, none in code that had changed.
    - **The browser harness could not have caught it.** It hovered five entries and screenshotted each, proving the preview renders, positions and clamps — and it never clicked the canvas *afterwards*. That is the boundary of the technique, now drawn twice in one session: **a single-surface harness verifies a mechanism; only a suite performing a real sequence finds what that mechanism breaks next to it.** Both times the harness found what CI structurally could not, and CI found what the harness structurally could not.

- **Templates' named areas — the last structurally missing builder feature, and the bug that was not in the feature.**

  The scoping in the next-phase list was right about the work: a `treeKey` that can name an area, and a switcher above the canvas. `BuilderDocument.treeKey` is now a payload **path** — `sections` for a page, `blocks` for a pattern, `areas.main` for a template — and `payload()` does a nested write when it names an area. `blockTreesOf` had walked areas since Phase 3, so **publish validation, revisions and version diffing needed no change at all**; the canvas, the library rail, the properties panel and the drag-and-drop needed none either. **No migration**, and that was checked rather than assumed: `document_table('template')` already resolved to `templates`, the four `template.*` permissions already existed, and `templates` held **zero rows** — so there was nothing to migrate into the new shape. Three slices in a row now with no migration.

  ⚠️ **The real work was dirtiness, not the switch.** `dirty` is a reference comparison of `tree` against `savedTree`, and both are swapped by an area switch — so the moment an edit moves into `doc.rest` it becomes invisible to the only thing that decides whether to save. Edit area A, switch to B, type and undo: B is back at its loaded tree, the comparison says clean, **and A's edit is never sent.** `dirtyElsewhere` closes it, and `markSaved` grew a second argument — the exact `rest` that was sent — so switching away and back *while a save is in flight* cannot clear the flag on a payload that predated the other area's edits. Both are asserted; the second one is the test worth keeping, because it is the case that survives the obvious fix.

  ⚠️ **And the autosave hook watched the wrong half of the state.** It subscribed on `state.tree !== previous.tree`, which was complete while one tree was all a document had. Renaming an area changes the payload and not the open tree, so a rename sat unsaved until the editor happened to type into a block — silent, and indistinguishable from working. It watches `doc.rest` as well now. **The general lesson: adding a second place state can live means auditing every place that decided the first one had changed**, and a subscription is the easiest of those to miss because nothing about it fails loudly.

  **Two refusals worth keeping, both of the "would look like it worked" kind.** A template arrives holding one area called `main` rather than `0003`'s `{"areas":{}}` default, because an empty map opens the builder with no tree and no obvious way forward. And **previews are switched off for templates**: `/preview/[token]` renders `BLOCK_TREE_KEY[type]`, which is `null` here, so a preview would show "nothing to show" for a template full of blocks — and concatenating the areas instead would render an arrangement no renderer will ever produce, which is the same "plausible page from a broken read" the homepage route deliberately refuses to fall back to.

  **Areas are ordered alphabetically, and cannot honestly be ordered any other way today** — measured, not assumed. Postgres `jsonb` sorts object keys by length then bytewise: written as `{header, main, footer, a}` the column reads back `{a, main, footer, header}`, confirmed against the live project before the switcher was written. Presenting them in payload order would show an arrangement that looks like the editor's intent and is not. A real order means storing one beside them, and nothing needs it yet.

  ⚠️ **The issue count is per area, because publish validates all of them and the tray only ever sees one.** Without `areaIssues` the tray could say "no issues" while publish refused the document over a block in an area nobody was looking at — and the publish bar's own "Validation: No issues" would have agreed with it, in the same dialog that failed. The bar totals every area now, and a publish refusal **opens the area holding the first offending block** before selecting it, since selecting a key the canvas is not showing puts the editor nowhere at all.

  ⚠️ **The E2E spec's first CI run found a defect — in the spec, not the feature.** `getByRole("button", { name: /^Masthead/i })` timed out waiting for a library entry that does not exist: **a library entry is named by its manifest's `label`, not by its type name**, and the `masthead` block's label is "The Masthead — team". The anchor could never match. Two things worth carrying forward: the type name and the label are different strings and only one of them is on screen, so **read `lib/blocks/manifests/*.ts` before writing a locator for a block**; and the run was still worth having — 68 passed, one failed, and **five specs never ran at all**, because a `serial` group abandons the rest once one fails. A green count is the only thing that proves the tail of a serial group executed. Replaced with `Newsletter`, whose label is that word verbatim.

  **While fixing it, one assumption was checked rather than carried:** every one of the twenty-four blocks validates straight out of the library with its default settings, confirmed by walking `blockManifests` through `validateTree`. That is why `builder.spec.ts` has to *empty* a field to produce an issue, and it means the choice of filler block cannot affect the publish assertion.

  ✅ **It is now verified end to end, and the count is what says so.** The second CI run reported **`74 passed`** with nothing skipped and nothing unrun — 67 → 74, the seven templates specs executing for the first time, against a stack CI seeded from scratch. That is the number that matters here rather than the green tick, because the signed-in specs `test.skip` themselves without `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL`/`E2E_ADMIN_PASSWORD` and a job that ran nothing would have looked identical. Integration green, 16/16 baselines unmoved, build green with all three routes present.

  **What had to be taken on trust before that, recorded because the order is the reusable part.** `tests/e2e/templates.spec.ts` is seven specs and is the only thing that exercises the area switch through a real browser and a real database — which is precisely where the payload assembly can go wrong. It **cannot run in a session container**: the signed-in specs need `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL`/`E2E_ADMIN_PASSWORD` and no container has them, so they `test.skip` themselves and a green run would look identical to a green run that executed nothing. **The number to check is the count — 67 → 74** — not the tick. What *was* run here: the four gates (**1062 unit tests**, up 47), `npm run build` with all three new routes present and no public route changing rendering mode, **16/16 visual baselines unmoved**, and a live probe against `qnfoztnyxhubnnulpfwt` confirming a two-area payload survives the jsonb column with both trees intact and their block settings unchanged. Every scratch row was deleted and the table counts confirmed back at zero.

  **One locator trap avoided rather than hit, for once.** The area chips carry their issue count *inside the button*, so an area with one issue has the accessible name "header 1" — a name match would have worked until a block went invalid and then failed as a missing element, and matching loosely is worse because Playwright matches names by substring and "header" also matches "header-two". The chips carry `data-area` and the spec locates by that, the same idiom `[data-block-key]` already gives the canvas. This is the third appearance of the substring trap in this file (`getByLabel("Quote")` matching six elements, `"Drag Column"` matching `"Drag Columns — layout"`); the first two cost a CI run each.

- **Synced patterns — the option that was a lie, and the block type nobody had noticed was missing.**

  `sync_mode` has been a column since `0003` and a hard-coded `'detachable'` since Phase 4, because `expandPatternRefs` had exactly one caller — `/preview/[token]`. **No public route expanded a `_ref`**, so a synced pattern rendered correctly in preview and vanished from the live site: the failure shape this file singles out as the worst available, because the person checking their work is shown it working. The next-phase entry framed it as either/or — expand on the public paths, or stop pretending the column is a choice — and expanding turned out to be the smaller half.

  **Most of it was already built and unused.** `lib/blocks/expand.ts` — cycle detection by ancestor set, a depth cap, key namespacing so one pattern used twice does not collide, and an unresolved ref *left in place* rather than dropped — was written, tested and dead since Phase 4. **No migration either**, and that was probed rather than assumed: `anon` already holds a column grant on `patterns.published_data` and the `patterns: public read published` policy already existed.

  ⚠️ **The thing the scoping missed: a `_ref` node needs a registered block type.** `validateBlock` looks up `manifestFor(node._type)` and refuses an unknown one, so a page holding a bare ref node would have composed, saved and previewed perfectly and then been refused at publish with *"Unknown block type"* — a failure at the last step, on the screen where an editor has least reason to expect one. Hence `patternRef`: a real component, manifest and registry entry, `hidden` from the library because a ref is never built from the block picker. **It has no fields**, deliberately — which pattern it points at is `_ref`, and a copy in `settings` would give one fact two homes.

  ⚠️ **The public read could not reuse the repository's, and the reason is a column grant.** `repo.getPatternsByIds` selects `draft_data`, which `0020` revoked from `anon`, so the shared read fails as `42501 permission denied for table patterns` — an error naming the **table** and not the column, during a static build. Measured against the live project, not reasoned about. `expandPublicPatterns` therefore has its own read, selecting `id, published_data` only, and the *absence* of the repository's `published_data ?? draft_data` fallback is the second half of the fix: falling back to a draft here would put one editor's half-finished pattern on every page using it.

  **A second probe settled which layer enforces that.** With the scratch pattern set to `draft`, the anon read returned `[]` **with and without** the `status = 'published'` filter — so RLS is the enforcement and the filter is belt-and-braces. Worth knowing before anyone "simplifies" the query.

  ⚠️ **Expansion runs before binding resolution on `/[category]`, and the order is not arbitrary.** A pattern's own blocks can carry `$bind` descriptors — a pattern holding an `articleGrid` is an obvious thing to build — and a block substituted in *after* `resolveBindings` had run would reach the renderer with its descriptor unresolved, rendering an empty grid on a page whose other grids were full.

  **Publishing a pattern revalidates the whole public site**, deliberately, following the `/admin/navigation` precedent. The precise version — find the documents whose `published_data` contains a ref and revalidate only those — needs a jsonb containment query matching at any depth of a nested tree, and it would be wrong in the direction that fails silently: a page it missed keeps serving the old blocks with nothing reporting a problem. Over-broad is merely wasteful. Publish, unpublish and rollback call it; **snapshot does not**, because a snapshot writes history and touches no published payload.

  **Two editor-facing refusals, both of the "would look like it worked" kind.** The canvas shows a reference as a **card**, not as the pattern's content: rendering the referenced blocks inline would look like the live page and behave like nothing else on the canvas, since they belong to another document and could not be selected, edited or deleted — every one of those failing silently under the cursor. Preview shows the result; the canvas edits this page. And the card **says when a synced pattern has never been published**, because the public expansion reads `published_data` only — so an unpublished one composes, previews (preview prefers drafts, by design) and then renders a gap.

  **And the delete dialog stopped lying.** It reassured the editor that "pages that already use this pattern keep their sections — inserting a pattern copies its blocks rather than linking to it", which was true for the three phases when `detachable` was the only mode and became the *opposite* of a reassurance the moment `synced` was real: deleting a synced pattern leaves every page referencing it rendering a gap. The dialog does not know which mode the row is, so the copy names both. Worth flagging as a category — **making an option real can falsify prose written when it was not**, and prose has no type checker.

  ⚠️ **The E2E spec's first CI run found a defect — in the spec, and in the same family as the last one.** `getByRole("button", { name: patternName, exact: true })` timed out on the library rail: **the rail's button holds two spans**, the pattern's name and a subtitle beneath it ("1 section" when it has no description), so its accessible name is `"<name> 1 section"` and an exact match could never succeed. Fixed with an anchored regex.

  **The second run then failed differently, and the difference is worth reading.** `getByText("Synced pattern", { exact: true })` hit a **strict-mode violation**: two elements matched, and both were correct. Inserting a reference selects it, so the properties panel shows the block's manifest label — which is *also* "Synced pattern" — while the canvas card carries the same words as an eyebrow. **The failure was evidence the feature worked**: element one in the error was the card, rendered exactly as designed. Fixed by scoping the assertion to the canvas's `<main>`, which Playwright's own error message suggested.

  Worth separating the two, because they look alike and are not: the first was a locator that could never match, the second a locator that matched **too much**. Only the first was a mistake about the markup; the second is what happens when one label legitimately appears on two surfaces.

  ⚠️ **The third run found the first real product bug of the slice, and it was invisible to every other kind of test.** `Detach a copy` could not be clicked — by Playwright or by a person. `PatternRefCard` renders inside the canvas frame's **leaf navigation killer**, `[&_a]:pointer-events-none [&_button]:pointer-events-none`, which exists so a *section's* own links cannot navigate away inside the builder and is applied to leaves only. `patternRef` declares no slot, so it is a leaf — but what it wraps is not a section at all. It is editor chrome whose only button is the one control the card exists to offer. The result was a button that was visible, enabled, focusable, hover-styled and **inert**: no error, no disabled state, nothing in the console. Playwright named it exactly — `<div class="mt-4">…</div> intercepts pointer events`, the card's own wrapper eating the click. `Canvas.tsx` now exempts a ref from the killer, with the reasoning beside the rule.

  **Two things about that are worth carrying forward.** The first is that the killer's own comment already explained why it is leaves-only and still did not prepare anyone for a leaf that renders chrome instead of a component — the rule's *stated* purpose ("a section's links must not navigate") was never true of this block, and nobody checked the purpose against the new case. The second is that **only an E2E test could have caught it.** `tsc`, ESLint, 1079 unit tests, the conformance suite and 16 visual baselines were all green; the defect lives entirely in the interaction between a Tailwind descendant selector and a component added three files away.

  **The same run also produced a phantom flake, which is worth naming because it will happen again.** Spec 2 passed on its first attempt and failed both retries on the old two-element strict-mode violation — this time with *two card eyebrows*, not a card and a panel. A `serial` group retries **from the top**, and this one composes shared fixture data: attempt 1 published its reference to `/grooming` before the detach spec failed three tests later, so attempt 2 opened a page that already had a card and inserted a second. **The spec Playwright reported as flaky was not the broken one** — it was downstream of the real failure, and following it would have led nowhere. The locators are now scoped to the frame carrying this attempt's stamped pattern name, which makes the assertions mean "*mine* is there" and "*mine* is gone" rather than "one exists" and "none exist".

  **What the run did prove, and it is the important half:** specs 3 and 4 passed on their first attempt. The pattern's blocks render on `/grooming` having been stored there as a pointer, and **editing the pattern changed a page nobody opened** — the property that makes it synced, and the one thing three phases of this feature never had.

  ✅ **The fourth run was green: `80 passed`, nothing failed, flaky, skipped or unrun** — the first time all six specs executed, cleanup included. Merged as `3d5511a`.

  That is the **third** time the name-matching family has cost a run — `getByLabel("Quote")` matched six elements, `"Drag Column"` matched `"Drag Columns — layout"`, and the templates spec's `/^Masthead/i` matched nothing because the label is "The Masthead — team". The lesson that keeps not transferring is not "use `exact`" or "don't use `exact`", both of which have now failed: it is **read the markup of the element you are locating, not just the text you can see on it**. A button is not its label.

  The runs were still worth having, and the shape is the same as the templates slice: **75 passed, 1 failed, 4 did not run**, then **77 passed, 1 failed, 1 flaky, 1 did not run** — a `serial` group abandons its tail, so each round buys back only the specs ahead of the break. Only a full count proves the rest.

  ⚠️ **Verification honesty: what had not run when this was written.** `tests/e2e/syncedPatterns.spec.ts` is six specs and the only thing that proves a *public route actually calls the expansion* — the unit suite proves `expandPatterns` substitutes blocks, the conformance suite proves `patternRef` is a real block, and neither can tell you the homepage is wired. It cannot run in a session container, for the usual reason. **The number to check on the first CI run is 74 → 80.** ✅ **It got there — on the fourth run, `80 passed` with nothing failed, flaky, skipped or unrun.** Three rounds of failures bought it: two spec locators and one real product bug (below). Keeping the prediction beside the outcome is the point — it took four CI runs to turn six written specs into six executed ones, and every intermediate run reported a plausible-looking pass count for a suite that had never reached its own last spec. What *was* run here: the four gates (**1079 unit tests**, up 17), `npm run build` with **`/` still `○ (Static)` and `/[category]` still `● (SSG)`** — the check that matters most, since `expandPublicPatterns` reads through the cookie-free client precisely so an `await` on a public route does not forfeit static rendering — **16/16 visual baselines unmoved**, and the two live probes above. Every scratch row was deleted and the table counts confirmed back at baseline.

  **The spec composes `/grooming` rather than the homepage, and that is a test-design decision worth copying.** The two public surfaces rendering a document's block tree are `/` and `/[category]`, and `/` and `/style` are both visual baselines — which run in the same CI job immediately after the E2E suite. A spec failing halfway while holding an extra block on either would turn one failure into five, four of them pointing at the wrong thing. `/grooming` has no baseline, so the blast radius of a mid-spec failure is the spec.

**Known issues**

- ⚠️ **`text-mg-fg/70` and every alpha utility on `fg`, `bd` and `bg` compile to no CSS at all — 417 usages across 129 files, and they never have.** Found while fixing `mg.accentSerif`, which was the same bug in a smaller place. Tailwind 3.4's `withAlphaValue` runs `parseColor` over the theme value and **drops the utility entirely** when it cannot parse one; `var(--x)` never parses. So `mg.fg: "var(--mg-fg)"` means `text-mg-fg/70` emits nothing, the class sits in the markup looking correct, and the text paints at full opacity.

  **Measured, not deduced**: running Tailwind over a probe file, `text-mg-accent/40` emits `rgb(var(--mg-accent-rgb) / 0.4)` and `border-mg-accentSerif/40` now does too, while `text-mg-fg/70`, `border-mg-bd/25` and `bg-mg-bg/50` emit nothing whatsoever. Nothing catches it on its own — `tsc`, ESLint, the unit suite and all sixteen baselines are green either way, because **a border that is absent looks like a design that never had one**, and text at 100% where 70% was asked for looks like a deliberately darker paragraph.

  **Deliberately not fixed, because it is the brand's call and not a session's.** Repairing it lightens muted text and hairline borders across the entire public site in one commit, and the sixteen baselines in `handoff/screenshots/` were captured with it broken — so the design as verified *is* the design as currently rendered. **It is asserted rather than described**, as a `KNOWN GAP` in `lib/domain/alphaUtilities.test.ts` (`KNOWN_BROKEN = ["bd", "bg", "fg"]`) carrying the inversion instructions — the fifth use of the technique this file records working. The mechanism is proven twice over: give each token an `R G B` twin in `app/globals.css` in **every** context that redeclares it, add it to `CHANNEL_TWIN` in `lib/domain/theme.ts` so the theme editor emits it too, point the palette entry at `rgb(var(--…-rgb) / <alpha-value>)`, and re-record the baselines.

- [x] ~~**The PDP's member price is computed in pounds, and disagrees with the bag by 25p.**~~ — **fixed.** `memberUnitPrice` in `lib/domain/pricing.ts`; the PDP now renders `Members £123.25`. ⚠️ **Two things this bullet asserted were wrong, and both are the reusable part.** It said the correct value "renders different pixels on the baseline-verified `product` screenshot, which makes it a decision about the sixteen shots" — **measured, all sixteen pass untouched**, so it was never a decision, and no baseline re-record was needed. And the one-line repair it recorded — `penceToPounds(applyRate(unitPence, 1 - cart.memberRate))` — is **the wrong shape**: multiplying by the complement rounds differently from the cart, which rounds the *discount* and subtracts. The two disagree by a penny in general (3p at 50% → 1 vs 2). A repair written down but never run is a hypothesis, not a fix.

- [x] ~~**`productJsonLd` advertises a single `Offer` at the product's own price, which a variant PDP now contradicts.**~~ — **fixed, and the predicted shape was right**: an `AggregateOffer` with `lowPrice`/`highPrice` plus a nested `Offer` per variant, and `getPublishedProductSeo` widened to read `product_variants`. What the entry did not predict is the judgement inside it — **the range spans only the *sellable* variants**, so a discontinued size cannot advertise a price nobody can buy, while still appearing as an offer with its own availability. ⚠️ Its stated reason for deferring ("it changes SEO output shape") was also the weaker half of the pair: structured data is a `<script>` in the `<head>` and **no screenshot has ever contained it**, so unlike the member price this one never had a baseline cost at all. The single-`Offer` branch is byte-identical for a variant-free product, which is every product the catalogue currently holds.

- ✅ ~~**`/shop`'s product cards show the product's own price, not a "from" price.**~~ — **done**, and taken as the design decision the bullet correctly said it was rather than slipped in. `cardPricePence` quotes the lowest *sellable* variant, reuses `productJsonLd`'s `AggregateOffer` rule and is asserted against it, and says "From" only when the buyable sizes actually differ. Original text follows.
  **`/shop`'s product cards show the product's own price, not a "from" price.** Also opened by variants: a card reading £145 for a product whose only in-stock size is £159.99 is misleading in the one direction that matters. Deliberately out of scope — the cards are pixel-verified and a "From £145" treatment is new design, not a prop change. ⚠️ **Its two siblings above are now struck through and this one is not, deliberately.** Both of those were arithmetic with one right answer; this one is a question about what a card should *say*, which is the brand's and not a session's. Do not close it by inferring a treatment from the two that closed beside it.

- ⚠️ **`patterns.preview_asset_id` is still written by nothing.** The description and category half of that table is closed as of this session; this column is the remainder. `0003` built it as a `media_assets` reference and no screen collects one, so the builder's rail labels every pattern with text alone. Smaller than the gap it is left over from, and genuinely optional — `BlockPreview` renders the real component on hover, which is better than a thumbnail and cannot drift from what gets inserted.

- ⚠️ **`product_variants.options` and `.stock` are collected by nothing and read by nothing on the public side, and that is now a decision.** `options` is a `jsonb` the admin's variant form never fills, so a picker built from an option matrix would be rendering a column no human writes — the picker uses `title`, which is what the form collects. `stock` is deliberately **not selected** by `lib/services/publicCatalog.ts`: an inventory count is not public data, so sellability comes from `availability` and the number never reaches a browser. Revisit if a merchant asks for size/colour axes rather than a flat list.

- ⚠️ **The palette does not meet AA contrast at small sizes, and the design baseline says it does.** ✅ **81% of this is now repaired and the rest is narrowed — read the decisions-log entry, not the paragraphs below, which describe the state before that.** What is left is **33 nodes**: the brand red at small sizes (24) and the light-mode grey ramp (9). The claim below that fixing it *"invalidates the sixteen baselines by construction"* was **measured and is false** — the repair moved not one of the sixteen. `color-contrast` is now *enforced* on every route except eight named pairs (`ACCEPTED_CONTRAST_GAPS`), where it used to be disabled outright.

  The original measurement, kept because the four-cause breakdown is still the map: **204 violations across 11 routes × 2 themes, and *nothing else* — every other WCAG A/AA rule passes.** They collapse into four token-level causes rather than scattered mistakes: `#696969` on `#0d0d0d` (156 of them — the dark-band mono labels at 9–10px, ratio **3.54** against 4.5); the racing red itself, `#c8102e` on dark and `#ff4d5e` on light at 10–12px (**2.94–3.3**); `--mg-muted` `#8a8a8a` and `--mg-faint` `#b0b0b0` on light (**1.97–3.45**); and pale pink on red in the CTA band, `#f4cfd5` on `#c8102e` (**4.12**, the near miss).

  **This is a decision, not a repair, which is why it is recorded rather than done.** `design_handoff_modern_gentlemen/CLAUDE.md` says two things that cannot both survive this measurement: *"Do not deviate, simplify, or improve — match the handoff bundle exactly"*, and *"Meet AA contrast — the tokens above already do"*. Fixing the contrast means changing tokens or the sizes they are used at, which moves pixels on a site that is pixel-verified against `handoff/screenshots/` — so it invalidates the sixteen baselines by construction. **Which of those two rules gives is the brand's call, not a session's.**

  **It is asserted rather than written down**, as a `KNOWN GAP` test carrying the instruction to invert it — the technique this file records working for `is_system` (`0018`) and `draft_data` (`0020`). The per-route audits exclude `color-contrast` and enforce every other rule from today, so the suite is informative now instead of failing everywhere for one undecided reason. **When the decision is made: drop the `disableRules`, delete the gap test.**

  Three routes to consider, in rough order of how much they cost the design: raise `--mg-muted`/`--mg-faint` and the dark-band grey to AA-passing values (touches every muted label, and is invisible on a chart but not on a page); keep the colours and raise the *sizes* of the 9–10px mono labels past the large-text threshold (changes the typographic rhythm the prototypes specify); or accept the gap formally as a documented deviation with the accent reserved for non-text use. The first is the only one that reaches AA without changing the type scale.
- [x] ~~**Every `*.delete` permission is additive rather than restrictive.**~~ — **fixed by `0022` for six of the seven tables.** A `for all` write policy covers DELETE and permissive policies are OR-ed, so the effective rule was `x.write OR x.delete`: the delete permission could grant and never refuse. `0022` adds one RESTRICTIVE `for delete` policy per table to `pages`, `templates`, `patterns`, `articles`, `products` and `media_assets`. Applied to the live project, replayed idempotently, and **probed behaviourally rather than inferred** — see the decisions log.
- ⚠️ **`categories` still has the additive shape, deliberately, and closing it is a product decision rather than a policy repair.** It is the one table `0022` left alone. A category has **two** delete routes and they check different permissions: `/admin/taxonomy` → `taxonomy.deleteCategory` → `taxonomy.write`, and `/admin/categories` → `documents.deleteDocument` → `category.delete`. `0021` widened the write policy to `taxonomy.write OR category.write` expressly so that "the `author` role behaves exactly as it did", and a restrictive `category.delete` gate breaks the taxonomy screen for precisely that role. **`rls.test.ts` carries this as a `KNOWN GAP` characterisation test** asserting today's behaviour with the instruction to invert it, which is the technique this file records working three times now. What has to be decided first: whether deleting a category from the taxonomy list still means the same thing now that a category is also a document with a published layout.
- [x] ~~**Only detachable patterns exist — a synced one would render in preview and vanish from the live site.**~~ — **fixed.** `expandPublicPatterns` is called by the homepage and `/[category]`, `patternRef` is a registered block so a page holding one can be published, publishing a pattern revalidates the public site, and `sync_mode` is offered at creation because it is now true rather than because the column existed. See the decisions log.
- [x] ~~**Nothing on the public path renders a template, so publishing one changes no page.**~~ — **fixed.** `/` resolves the template assigned to the `home` page and renders the area holding a `documentContent` marker, with the page's own sections spliced in at it. Both consequences this bullet predicted landed exactly as written: publishing a template **invalidates a set of paths** (`publicPathsForTemplate`, not `revalidatePublicPage`'s one-slug shape), and `/preview/[token]` **still cannot name an area** — that half is deliberately not in this slice, see below.
- [x] ~~**Templates still cannot be previewed, and the renderer landing makes that gap sharper rather than softer.**~~ — **fixed.** `/preview/[token]` reads a template's areas rather than `BLOCK_TREE_KEY[type]` (still `null`), takes the area to draw from `?area=`, and composes it exactly as `composePublishedPage` does. `createPreviewAction` mints a token instead of refusing, and the templates route offers the Preview button. **Both halves the bullet named landed**: a page *is* substituted when the answer is unambiguous, and the marker *does* draw itself when it is not. ⚠️ **What it predicted and got wrong: it called the area a thing the token should carry.** `preview_sessions.context` exists and looks like the home for it — `resolve_preview` does not return that column, and it is the only way an anonymous holder reads the row at all, so putting the area there means dropping and recreating a `security definer` function `anon` executes. It gates nothing anyway: the token already hands over **every area** of the draft. See the decisions log.
- ⚠️ **Only `page` templates render.** `article`, `product` and `archive` kinds resolve and publish and change nothing, because `/article/[slug]` and the PDP render fixed components (`ArticleHero`/`ArticleBody`/`RelatedGrid`) rather than block trees — the `articles.template` *column* is a design-variant name and has nothing to do with the `templates` table. Making those template-driven is a rewrite of pixel-verified routes, not an extension of this slice. **Areas other than the marker's are legal, editable, versioned and unrendered** — they are what the `header`/`footer` kinds will use.
- [x] ~~CI stopped running entirely~~ — **a GitHub Actions platform outage**, confirmed on githubstatus.com. Nothing in this repository, its settings or the account was ever at fault. Recorded because the *diagnosis* went badly wrong and the lesson is general.
  - **Check the provider's status page first.** It is one request, and it discriminates between "our config is broken" and "their platform is down" before any setting is touched. Skipping it cost a settings hunt, four retrigger attempts, and a recommendation to make the repository **public** for free Actions minutes — an irreversible disclosure that was never needed, since billing had already been ruled out arithmetically (~12 min per run × 35 runs ≈ 420 of 2,000 free minutes).
  - **The wrong conclusion was "Actions is disabled at the repository level."** A screenshot disproved it in one glance: *Allow all actions and reusable workflows* was selected, and the Actions tab was visible — which the "Disable actions" option explicitly says it would hide. The tell was available the whole time and was not asked for early enough.
  - **`list_workflows` reporting `state: active` is not reassurance.** It describes the workflow *file*, not whether Actions is enabled for the repo, and certainly not whether the platform is up.
  - The genuinely useful signal, for next time: `pull_request` events kept firing throughout (reopening a PR produced a fresh `Supabase Preview` check every time) while `list_workflow_runs` returned `total_count: 0`. **Webhooks working + zero runs created = the problem is upstream of the repository**, not in it.
- [x] **The repository is now public.** Git history was scanned before relying on that, not after: `.env.local` was never committed, there are no `sb_secret_` or `sb_publishable_` values in any commit, and the only JWT-shaped string in the whole history is `eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiIsInR5cCI6IkpXVCJ9` — the HS256 header, identical in every such token, carrying no payload and no signature (it is `REAL_PREFIX` in `lib/db/env.test.ts`). Every other match is a placeholder (`your_service_role_key`) or the deliberate CI literal `ci-local-stack-password`. **Nothing needed revoking.** What being public *does* change: the RLS policies are now readable, so a gap in them is publicly discoverable rather than merely present. RLS was verified empirically in Phase 1, but Phase 1 predates products, media and articles — an audit against the current schema is worth doing before the site carries real customer data.
- [x] ~~A credential pasted from a masked field fails with an unreadable error~~ — **fixed in `lib/db/env.ts`.** Worth reading, because the failure was a good example of a bad error message. A Railway deploy set `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` to a value copied from a field that renders it as `eyJhbGci••••••••` — present, non-empty, and unusable: supabase-js puts the key in the `apikey` and `Authorization` headers, and `fetch` refuses any header value holding a character above U+00FF. What surfaced was `TypeError: Cannot convert argument to a ByteString because the character at index 8 has a value of 8226`, thrown from inside undici and **attributed to the homepage render**, naming no variable and no fix. `required()` now rejects non-printable-ASCII at the boundary, names the variable and the index, and says that the value looks masked. It is deliberately stricter than `Headers`, which accepts anything ≤ U+00FF — an `é` in a JWT passes `fetch` and then fails as a 401 somewhere further away. It also trims surrounding whitespace (a paste artefact, safely repaired) but rejects whitespace *inside* a value (two values pasted together, or one truncated). The thrown message never contains the value: the same helper guards `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`, and error strings reach build logs and bug reports.
- [x] ~~`/admin` renders inside the public header/footer~~ — fixed by the Phase 4 route-group split.
- [x] ~~`0013_media_storage.sql` is unapplied~~ — **applied to the live project** after PR #8 merged, via the Supabase MCP (which regained access at that point after refusing across three earlier attempts). Verified: the `media` bucket exists, is public, carries the 50 MB limit, and `storage.objects` holds all four `media objects: …` policies. It had already been proven in CI, where `supabase start` applies it on every run.
- [x] ~~⚠️ **The Supabase GitHub integration cannot safely apply this repo's migrations**~~ — **fixed by making the migrations idempotent.** The mismatch itself is unchanged and unfixable without rewriting history: the live project records 14-digit timestamp versions (`20260731014806_identity_and_rbac` …) because `0001`–`0012` were applied through `apply_migration`, while this repo names its files `0001_`–`0014_`. The CLI reads the leading digits as the version, sees no overlap, and concludes that **none** of them have been applied. So a sync replays everything — and that is now safe.
  - **What changed:** every `create policy` (92) and `create trigger` (19) is preceded by a `drop policy/trigger if exists`. Postgres has no `if not exists` for either, which is why a replay previously died with `42710` on the first policy in `0001`. Tables, indexes and functions were already guarded (`if not exists`, `create or replace`), and both migration-level inserts were already conflict-guarded — the inserts inside `0010`/`0011` function bodies are runtime code, replaced wholesale, and never re-execute at apply time.
  - **A CI step proves it rather than asserting it.** `supabase start` applies each migration once; the `Migrations are idempotent` step then re-applies all fourteen on top of themselves with `ON_ERROR_STOP=1`. Without the guards that step fails immediately. **This is the difference between a migration that is believed idempotent and one that is known to be** — and the belief was wrong once already: this file previously claimed `0001`–`0012` were safe to re-run, which was flatly untrue.
  - **Still worth knowing:** the two fixes were alternatives, and renaming the files to the upstream timestamps was the other one. Guarding was chosen because it needs no access to the live project's migration history (unavailable this session) and because it makes the files correct in their own right rather than merely correct against one project's history.
- **The RLS suite, and the probe that was written first.** The Phase 1 leftover finally has a file — `tests/integration/rls.test.ts` — but the useful part of this slice is the order the work was done in, because the container could not run the suite and the deployed project was sitting right there.

  **A session container cannot start a stack.** Docker is installed and its daemon is unreachable, so `npx supabase start` is not available and neither is `npm run test:integration`. Writing a hundred assertions about policy behaviour blind and letting CI adjudicate is the loop that cost the nesting spec four runs. So the order was inverted: **probe the live project first, then encode what it actually does.**

  **What the probe was.** Two read-only sweeps and one deliberately non-destructive write sweep, all against `https://qnfoztnyxhubnnulpfwt.supabase.co` with the publishable key — the same shape as the `curl -w '%{redirect_url}'` check that found the proxy redirect bug, and available in any container with outbound HTTPS.
  - The **write** sweep is the part worth reusing. `INSERT {}` into a table answers the question without being able to write anything: `42501` means RLS refused it, while `23502` would mean RLS *allowed* it and only a NOT NULL constraint stopped the row. Likewise `UPDATE … set status='__rls_probe__'` against a CHECK-constrained column — an empty result means no row survived the `USING` clause, and `23514` would mean the policy let it through and only the CHECK saved it. **Neither branch can modify data**, which is what makes it safe to point at production. All 19 tables answered `42501`; the three document tables answered empty; a re-read confirmed nothing moved.
  - The **read** sweep compared what `anon` sees against what `service_role` sees, table by table, which turns "0 rows" into evidence rather than coincidence: `revisions` (3 rows), `job_runs` (43), `role_permissions` (113), `permissions` (40), `roles` (5), `profiles` (1), `user_roles` (1) and `product_sources` (1) all return **nothing** to anon, and `pages` returns 1 of 2 — the unpublished one correctly withheld.

  **The headline is that the write side is clean.** No anonymous write is possible anywhere, the gate functions (`has_permission`, `is_staff`, `is_admin`) are all `security definer` with a pinned `search_path` and phrased as `exists(...)` so they return false rather than NULL for a caller with no session, and row-level hiding of drafts works on every document table. Six phases of tables that had never been checked turned out to be right.

  **What it found is on the read side, and one of the two came from reading the policy catalogue rather than from any request.** `pages` has two PERMISSIVE policies covering DELETE, and permissive policies are OR-ed, so `not is_system` guards nothing against a `page.write` holder — the same shape makes every `*.delete` permission additive rather than restrictive. Both, plus the `draft_data` column exposure and the `using (true)` child tables, are written up under Known issues with their fixes. **None is fixed here**: each wants a migration, and the two that are policy changes would remove access that roles have today, which is a decision rather than a repair.

  **Two characterisation tests are the deliberate part.** `rls.test.ts` asserts the `draft_data` exposure and the `is_system` bypass *as they behave today*, each named `KNOWN GAP` and each carrying the instruction to invert it. A gap recorded only in prose is a gap nobody notices closing; asserted, the day someone fixes it the suite goes red and says so. The alternative — asserting the intended behaviour and shipping a red suite — would have made the file's first CI run uninformative about everything else in it.

  **It passed on its first CI run** — 25 tests, 2.0s, integration totals 69 → 94 across 8 → 9 files. (**Final state after `0018`: 26 tests, integration at 95**, green again, and the `Migrations are idempotent` step replayed all eighteen.) Worth stating why a first run went green here when the drag specs took four: the file could not be *executed* locally, but everything it depends on statically was checked against a source of truth rather than guessed. `tsc` validated every payload against the generated `database.types.ts` and caught the one real mistake (`permissions` takes `resource`/`action`, not `label`); the CHECK constraints behind `media_assets.kind`, `menu_items.link_type` and each `status` were read out of `pg_constraint`; and the assertions themselves were written to match what the live probe had already demonstrated. **The failure mode that remained was semantic, not structural**, and the probe had removed most of that too.

  ⚠️ **The green tick was not taken as the verification — the log was.** `npm run test:integration` runs with `--passWithNoTests`, and this project has found three separate checks that were green because they ran nothing. The number that settles it is the count: **the integration suite went from 69 tests to 94**, so the file collected and executed rather than being skipped.

  **The `is_system` bypass was then fixed in the same PR** — `0018`, one restrictive policy, applied to the live project and proven re-runnable by applying it twice. Worth noting what that did to the test: a characterisation test is not a permanent fixture, it is a **holding position**, and inverting it is the event it exists to force. It went from `KNOWN GAP: page.write alone can delete a system page` to `refuses to let a page.write holder delete one`, and gained a companion proving an ordinary page is still deletable — because a guard that over-refuses would pass the first assertion just as well.

  **And the run promoted one finding from inference to fact.** The `is_system` bypass was derived from reading `pg_policies` and applying the documented OR-semantics of permissive policies — no request was ever made against it, because proving it needs an actor holding `page.write` and nothing else, which needs the service-role key a container does not have. The characterisation test does exactly that on a throwaway system page, and it passed: **a `page.write`-only actor really does delete a page marked `is_system`.**
- **The accessibility pass — what a never-run audit actually turns up.**

  **The headline is that the checklist was mostly wrong in the reassuring direction.** `PROGRESS.md` carried "Accessibility: focus traps, `aria-expanded`, visible focus rings, reduced-motion, alt text" as `[ ] not started` for eight phases. Running it found the focus traps built (`lib/useFocusTrap`, `OverlayScrim` with Tab wrapping and focus restoration), the scroll lock built, reduced-motion gated, and every WCAG A/AA rule except one passing on all 11 public routes in both themes. **An unasserted requirement is indistinguishable from an unmet one**, and this file had been carrying the pessimistic reading of that ambiguity for most of a year.

  **Two real defects, both invisible without a keyboard.** The **search and bag triggers carried no `aria-expanded`** — the drawer's had it since Track A, so the omission looked like a pattern being followed. To a screen-reader user those are buttons that announce nothing and appear to do nothing. Neither named the dialog it opened either; `aria-controls` and matching ids fix that. **Adding ARIA attributes moves no pixels**, which is what made this safe on a pixel-verified site — the 16 baselines were re-run and unmoved.

  ⚠️ **And one finding that is a decision rather than a bug: 204 contrast violations, and nothing else.** Every one is a design token or the brand accent at a size the prototypes specify — see Known issues for the four causes and the numbers. **The design baseline asserts both that the tokens must not change and that they already meet AA; the second is measurably false.** Fixing it moves pixels by construction. Recorded as a `KNOWN GAP` test rather than prose, and the per-route audits exclude `color-contrast` so they can enforce the other twenty-odd rules starting today instead of failing everywhere for one undecided reason.

  **Three mistakes in the test file itself, and all three are the interesting part.**

  ⚠️ **The first assertion about alt text was stricter than WCAG and wrong.** It demanded a non-empty `alt` on every image and failed on the homepage's full-bleed feature — an editorial photograph with the headline overlaid on it. That `alt=""` is **correct**: adjacent text already names the thing, and repeating it makes a screen reader say it twice. The fix was to the *test*, not the component, and the replacement is narrower and mechanical — a link whose entire content is an undescribed image has no accessible name at all, which cannot produce a false positive on a captioned hero. **"Every image needs alt text" is a rule of thumb, not a success criterion.**

  ⚠️ **`networkidle` never settles on this site, and the visual suite had already written that down.** The first run timed out on all 27 tests. `tests/visual/public.spec.ts` says in a comment: "Not `networkidle`: the hero and film sections stream video, so the network never goes idle and the wait times out." **A trap recorded in one suite does not stop the next one walking into it** — what to wait for is `document.fonts.ready`, because an unloaded face reflows every text run and would move the contrast checks onto the wrong pixels.

  ⚠️ **The bag overlay test failed on a design rule it did not know about.** The bag trigger is gated on `showBag = isStoreRoute(pathname)` and does not exist on the homepage at all. The test read that as a missing dialog. Moving it to `/shop` was the fix; the failure was the spec's, not the chrome's — the same shape as the templates spec's locator and the `getByRole` substring trap this file already records twice.

  ⚠️ **One false failure came from neither: a stale `next start` still holding port 3000.** Playwright's `reuseExistingServer` picked up a server whose `.next` had been replaced underneath it, and 17 routes reported `html-has-lang` and `document-title` violations on `#__next_error__` — Next's error page, audited as if it were the site. **Two `document-title` failures in a row are a broken server, not a broken page**; the tell is the `#__next_error__` selector in the target. Second time this session a stale artefact produced a confident wrong answer, after the `.next` prerender in the template slice.

  **It is a Playwright project rather than more E2E specs, and that is load-bearing.** The `e2e` project `test.skip`s itself without `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL`, so a green run there can mean nothing ran. The `a11y` project needs **no credentials** — only a built, seeded site — which makes it the one Playwright suite a session container can actually execute. It runs in CI under `if: always()`, for the reason the visual step already documents: a broken spec elsewhere must not be able to hide a regression here.

- **Phase 8 — the template renderer, and the question three phases had deferred.**

  **The framing in this file understated it, and reading the routes was what showed that.** The Known-issues bullet said `resolveTemplateFor` "has no caller on the render path", which reads like a wiring job. What was actually missing was a *semantics*: nothing anywhere said what a template's named areas **mean** relative to a document's own section list. The templates phase had already bumped into this and refused to guess — it would not concatenate areas for preview, on the grounds that doing so "would render an arrangement no renderer will ever produce" — and that refusal was this decision, deferred.

  ⚠️ **Two facts about the routes have to be known before this is scoped, and neither is obvious from the admin.** Only **two** public routes are section-driven from the database: `/` and `/[category]`. `/about` renders a hard-coded `SECTIONS` constant. And `/article/[slug]` and the PDP render **fixed components** — `ArticleHero`, `ArticleBody`, `RelatedGrid` — chosen by the `articles.template` *column*, which is a design-variant name (one of twenty) and has **nothing to do with the `templates` table** despite sharing the word. So a template of kind `article` has nowhere to go without rewriting a pixel-verified route. The kind vocabulary promises seven things the render path can honour one of.
