  **The decision: a marker block, not bookend areas.** A `documentContent` node marks where the document's sections are spliced in. The alternative — conventional `before`/`after` areas — was less code and strictly less expressive: a template could bookend a page but never *wrap* it, so a full-bleed band around the content, the most obvious thing a layout wants, would have been impossible. The marker also composes with nesting for free: put it in a `column` and the page renders in that column, with no extra rule anywhere.

  **It is also the idiom this repo already had.** `patternRef` is a registered block that renders `null` and is substituted before the tree reaches `SectionRenderer`; `documentContent` is the same shape, and inherits the same hard-won lesson — it needs a **real manifest**, because `validateBlock` refuses an unknown `_type` and a template holding a bare marker would compose, save and preview and then be refused at publish, on the screen where an editor least expects it.

  **`onlyIn` is a third thing beside `hidden`, and the reason is recoverable state.** `column` and `patternRef` are hidden because nothing should ever pick them from the library. The marker is different: an editor **must** be able to place it, and must be able to put it back after deleting it. Hiding it everywhere would make a template unrecoverable from one keystroke; showing it everywhere would offer a block that means nothing in a page. So the manifest names the document types whose library offers it, and `blockCatalogFor` is where those bare strings meet the `DocumentType` union — `lib/blocks` stays a leaf that does not import `lib/domain`, the same split `lib/domain/templates.ts` makes for the `kind` vocabulary.

  **One area renders, and it is the one holding the marker.** Areas have no stored order — `areas.ts` measured that jsonb sorts keys by length then bytewise — so "render all the areas" can only mean alphabetically, which is an arrangement no editor asked for and none can control. Rendering exactly one is the only option that does not invent an ordering.

  ⚠️ **Which one was first decided by name, and that was wrong — the E2E found it in a single run.** The first version hard-coded `main`, with a publish rule refusing a marker anywhere else. The templates spec renames its only area to `body`, which is an entirely ordinary edit, and under a name-based rule that turned it into an unpublishable document that also silently unhooked itself from every page using it. **A magic name that must never be renamed is a trap, not a contract.** `findContentArea` keys the renderer on the marker instead: names became cosmetic, a rename is harmless, and an entire class of validation error stopped existing. The better design came out of a test failure, not out of the design step.

  **Publish validation carries what is left, because both failures are silent at authoring time and destructive at render time.** No marker → the template frames nothing and every assigned page loses its sections. Two markers → the page's sections render twice, and *which* area renders becomes arbitrary.

  ⚠️ **`applyTemplate` returns the document's sections when a template has no marker, which is the safe direction of the two.** Rendering the page without its frame loses styling; rendering the frame without the page loses the thing somebody came to read. Validation refuses to create that state; this is what happens if one exists anyway.

  ⚠️ **And the E2E was the only thing that could have caught it, which is the standing cost this repo keeps paying.** A session container cannot run the E2E suite — no Docker, no admin credentials — so the seeded marker changing what a newly created template looks like was invisible to every gate that runs locally: four green gates, a green build, 16 green baselines, and a spec asserting "Add your first section" that had been correct for exactly one slice. **Seeding a document with content is a change to every test that assumed it was empty**, and the serial group meant one stale assertion took five specs with it.

  **Two defects the tests caught, both mine, both real.** The structural-sharing assertion failed first: my recursion always allocated a new array, so `children === node.children` was never true and every branch was rebuilt. And the `changed` flag has to be tracked rather than inferred from length — a marker replaced by exactly one section leaves the count identical while the contents differ, and returning the original there would silently skip the substitution. Neither would have shown up in rendered output; both would have quietly cost the diffing contract.

  **The end-to-end proof was worth more than the unit tests, and it was cheap.** A throwaway published template of kind `page`, assigned `content_type: page`, holding `[documentContent, pullQuote]`. Rebuild, then grep the prerendered `index.html`: the quote is present, the page's own sections render **before** it at the marker's position, and no `documentContent` element survives in the HTML. Then delete both rows and re-run the baselines. **`templates` and `template_assignments` were both empty**, checked rather than assumed, so there was nothing to migrate and nothing to restore.

  ⚠️ **The first run of that probe reported a false negative, and the cause is worth remembering: `.next` was stale.** The grep found nothing, and the reflex was to suspect RLS — the anon reads were probed over PostgREST and were fine. `rm -rf .next` and rebuild, and the block was there. **A prerender is a cache; a build that changes no source may not rebuild the page you are looking at.**

  **Pixel-neutral by construction, and verified as such.** Nothing assigned means `composePublishedPage` returns the page's own sections untouched, so a project with no templates renders exactly as before — 16/16 baselines unmoved, before and after. And `/` is still `○ (Static)` in the build output: the resolution reads through `createPublicClient()`, because `resolveTemplateFor` goes through `lib/db/server.ts`, which awaits `cookies()`, and **any route that does is opted out of static rendering with nothing failing to say so**. That is the third time this file has had to record that hazard, and the second time it was avoided by declining to reuse an existing admin-side function.

- **`0022` — the delete permissions, and a probe that made the container's Docker gap irrelevant for once.**

  **The defect was one this file had already written down twice and fixed once.** `0018` closed the `is_system` half of it on `pages` and its closing comment named the rest as out of scope. The rest is six tables: a PERMISSIVE `for all` write policy and a PERMISSIVE `for delete` policy on the same table, OR-ed, so `x.write OR x.delete`. Every `*.delete` permission in the catalogue granted and could never refuse.

  ⚠️ **The next-phase entry named three tables and there were six.** It listed `articles`, `products` and `media_assets` — inherited verbatim from `0018`'s closing comment, which had been written from the point of view of the migration that had just touched `pages`. `templates`, `patterns` and `pages` itself have the identical pairing, and `page.delete` was *still* additive after `0018`. Fixing the three named would have left the item's own headline true. **A scope inherited from an earlier note is a scope worth re-deriving**, because the earlier note was answering a different question.

  **The decision the entry warned about was real and nearly free, and only measuring settled which.** "A migration plus the decision that some role loses access it has today" is accurate and reads far heavier than it is. The grant table:

  | resource | holds `.write` | holds `.delete` | loses |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | page, template, pattern | admin, editor | admin, editor | nobody |
  | product | admin, editor, merchandiser | admin, editor, merchandiser | nobody |
  | article | admin, author, editor | admin, editor | author |
  | media | admin, author, editor, merchandiser | admin, editor | author, merchandiser |

  Four of six tighten nothing. The two real losses are what the seeded role descriptions already promise — `author` is "Writes articles and uploads media; cannot publish", `merchandiser` holds `media.write` for product imagery. And `user_roles` holds **one row**, an `admin`, so the live blast radius was zero. **Price a permissions decision against the grant table, not against the abstraction**; the abstract version is much scarier than the concrete one.

  **The service layer already checked all six, which is the reason to do this rather than the reason not to.** `deleteDocument`, `lib/services/media.ts` and `lib/services/products.ts` each `requirePermission` the right `x.delete` first, so no button in the admin ever got past it and **this migration changes nothing an editor can see**. What it changes is the case where the service layer is not in the path: PostgREST is a public endpoint, the URL and publishable key ship in the client bundle by design, and a signed-in author holds a real JWT. `DELETE /rest/v1/articles?id=eq.…` carries none of the service checks with it. A defence-in-depth fix with no visible behaviour change is the easiest kind to skip and the cheapest kind to ship.

  ⚠️ **One of the seven tables had to be left out, and the policy catalogue could not have told me.** `categories` has the same shape, and it was in scope until the *delete routes* were read: there are two, on different permissions — `/admin/taxonomy` requires `taxonomy.write`, `/admin/categories` requires `category.delete` — and `0021` widened the write policy to `taxonomy.write OR category.write` deliberately so `author` would keep behaving as it did. A restrictive `category.delete` gate breaks the taxonomy screen for exactly that role. **The tell was in `lib/services/`, not in `pg_policies`.** Recorded as a `KNOWN GAP` test rather than as prose, so the day somebody reconciles the two routes the suite goes red and says so.

  **The probe is the part worth reusing, because it answered in production what the container cannot run.** This container has no Docker daemon, so `npm run test:integration` is unavailable and the RLS suite is written for CI — the loop that cost the nesting spec four rounds. The earlier RLS phase solved this with read and non-destructive write sweeps; this needed something stronger, because the question is *whether a specific actor may delete a specific row*, and that needs an actor.

  The answer was a single transaction, rolled back: insert a throwaway `auth.users` row, a throwaway role holding exactly the permissions under test, a `user_roles` row and a throwaway article; then `set local role authenticated` with `set local request.jwt.claims` to that user's `sub`; then attempt the delete; then `reset role` and count. **`set local` plus `rollback` makes production a safe place to ask a question about production**, and every row involved is one the probe created. Two runs settled it:

  - **write-only actor** (`article.read` + `article.write`): the `update` succeeded and the `delete` was refused, row surviving. The refusal is at DELETE specifically, not the row being hidden — which is the distinction a bare "it failed" cannot make.
  - **delete-holder** (`+ article.delete`): the article went, **and the same actor was still refused a `media_assets` row** it held no media permission on. That second half is what proves the policy is not a blanket `using (false)`, and it costs one extra statement.

  Verified afterwards that nothing leaked: zero probe rows, 53 articles, one `user_roles` row.

  **Also worth knowing: the third characterisation test has now completed the cycle.** `rls.test.ts`'s "still allows deleting an ordinary page" asserted, as the wrong-but-current behaviour, that a `page.write` holder deletes a non-system page — it was the empirical proof of the OR-semantics for `pages`, and it had been passing in CI since `0018`. `0022` inverts it: the same assertion now runs as a `page.delete` holder, because the narrowness of `0018`'s guard has to be shown by an actor that clears `0022`. A new test alongside it proves the two restrictive policies **stack** — a `page.delete` holder is still refused a system page. Three for three, and the technique is unchanged: **write the instruction to invert it inside the test.**

  ⚠️ **The integration half is unrun here and awaits CI**, for the Docker reason above. What was checked statically instead, which is what made the RLS suite green on its first run: `tsc` validated every fixture payload against `database.types.ts`; the CHECK constraint behind `templates.kind` and the NOT NULL columns of `templates`, `patterns` and `categories` were read out of the live catalogue rather than guessed; all fourteen permission keys the new fixtures request were confirmed to exist; and the only triggers on the seven tables are `touch_updated_at`, so the fixture `update` has no hidden dependency.

- **Reordering a column: two bugs wearing one coat.** The note this closes said column reordering was a *missing feature* — that insertion strips were library-drag-only, so there was no route. That was wrong on both counts, and finding out took a probe rather than a reading: **block-onto-block already reordered columns at the tree level**, and `moveByKey` handled it correctly. What was broken was elsewhere.

  ⚠️ **A forward drag was a silent no-op, for every block, since Phase 4.** `moveByKey` removes the active block and then inserts it at the target's index — but removing it shifts the target back into the index the active just vacated, so `[X, Y]` with X dropped onto Y comes back as `[X, Y]`. Dragging **up** always worked and dragging **down** never did; nothing threw, nothing failed, the block simply sprang back under the cursor. It survived because no test and no E2E ever dragged one block onto another — the suite's drags all come from the library — and because dragging up is what people try first. Fixed by landing *after* the target when the move is forward within one list.

  ⚠️ **And a column drag resolved to a block inside its neighbour.** `closestCenter` picks the nearest droppable centre, and a column's centre sits close to the centres of the blocks it holds. Passing that key to `moveByKey` nests the dragged column inside its sibling — legal, since a column's slot is unrestricted, and never what the gesture meant. `dropTargetFor` lifts the target back to the dragged block's own list, **scoped to horizontal slots only**: for a vertical list, "drop onto the block inside the container" is the behaviour, not the bug, and it is the only route into a non-empty one.

  **`BlockSlot` gained `direction`, so the row's refusal is declared rather than inferred.** Until now a row showed no strips because `allow: ["column"]` happened to exclude whatever was being dragged. That would have stopped being true the moment something a row *does* accept were dragged — a column — and the grid would have taken five cells again. Saying "these children sit side by side" is the honest rule.

  **The E2E swaps two columns by dragging**, which is the only place either bug was reachable: it needs a real browser to have a drag handle, a layout and a pointer at once.

  ⚠️ **And that spec failed first time on its own locator, not on the fix.** `getByRole`'s `name` matches a **substring** by default, so `"Drag Column"` also matched the row's own `"Drag Columns — layout"` and three handles resolved where the test asserted two. The assertion was right to refuse — without it the test would have dragged the *row* onto a column and proved something else entirely. `exact: true`. **The same trap this file already records for `getByLabel`**, which once matched six elements for "Quote"; the lesson did not transfer because the two APIs look unrelated until one of them bites.

- **Columns become real containers, and the bug that forced it.** Reported from the builder: *after dropping the first section into a column, dragging another into a different column does nothing.* It reproduced, and the cause was that **a column was not a thing**. `columns` declared one slot and held a flat list; the grid decided which cell a block landed in by its **index**. Three defects followed from that single fact.

  **There was no per-column drop target.** A drop could only ever say "at index N of the row", never "into this column", so the empty-looking cell was not a droppable at all. Nothing happened because there was nothing there.

  ⚠️ **The canvas's insertion strips were grid items.** They render as siblings of the children, so inside a container they are children of whatever it renders — and a row renders a CSS grid. Probed on the real canvas before changing anything: a two-column row holding two blocks produced `GAP(0), BLOCK, GAP(1), BLOCK, GAP(2)` — **five cells in a two-column grid**, so both blocks sat in the right-hand column on separate rows mid-drag and snapped back on drop. The published output was never affected; `SectionRenderer` passes children straight through.

  **And two blocks could not share a column**, nor could a row have an empty first cell and a filled second. Both are ordinary things to want and neither was expressible.

  **The fix is one shape: row → column → anything.** `column` is a container with its own slot; `columns` narrows to `allow: ["column"]` and seeds two via a new `insertChildren`. Nesting is unchanged — a column's slot is unrestricted, so a column may hold another row.

  **`allow` had been enforced since Phase 2 with no consumer.** `validate.ts` carried the branch and its own test said so outright: *"no shipped manifest can reach this branch"*. `columns` is the first, which is also what makes a pre-change document report itself — a section left directly in a row now validates as *"pullQuote is not allowed in Columns"*.

  **The canvas refuses strips a slot would reject, and that is what keeps the grid intact.** `BlockList` computes `accepts` from the slot's `allow` and the type in flight; a row offers no insertion points during a section drag, so its grid holds only columns. Asserted directly, and the assertion was checked against the old behaviour by disabling the gate — it fails with `['GAP', 'A', 'GAP', 'B', 'GAP']`, which is the reported symptom written as an array.

  **`column` is hidden from the insert menu** — a new manifest flag. It is a real, registered, validated block that means nothing on its own, and `columns` seeds its own; `blockTypes` still lists it, so the conformance suite walks it like everything else.

  ⚠️ **Side-by-side blocks broke the frame chrome, and CI found it on the first run.** A frame's toolbar is absolutely positioned, so it is not bounded by the column it belongs to. Stacked vertically that never mattered; side by side, the **Newsletter's label chip in the second column sat over the Timeline's Duplicate button in the first** and swallowed every click on it — Playwright reporting *"subtree intercepts pointer events"* and timing out on a button it could see perfectly well. The bar is now `pointer-events-none` with each control opting back in: the label and the issue badge are decorative and should never have been able to eat a click. **Not the same thing as the leaf-only navigation killer** on the content wrapper — that is a different element, and these buttons are its siblings rather than its descendants.

  **The two existing drafts were rewrapped rather than left to rot.** A page and a pattern held `columns` blocks with flat children; nothing published did. Each child became its own column, which reproduces exactly what the grid was already showing — child N was cell N — so the migration is meaning-preserving. Done through the Supabase MCP and read back to confirm.

- **"Should the builder have row support as well?" — asked in the same report, and the answer is that it already does, under another name.** Recorded here because it was answered in conversation and nowhere else, which is how a decision becomes a thing the next session re-litigates from scratch.

  **`columns` *is* the row.** Since the container fix above, the shape is row → column → anything: a `columns` block is a horizontal band, each `column` child is a cell, and a cell stacks whatever it holds. A separate `row` block would be a second name for the block that already exists, and the two would then disagree about which one a nested layout should use.

  **What a dedicated `row` would genuinely add is not rows — it is *bands*.** A full-bleed background colour or image behind a group of sections, its own vertical padding, and an inner content cap. None of that is about horizontal arrangement, and all of it is expressible as a container block with a slot and a handful of style fields — the `column` manifest is the template for it. **If that is ever wanted, name it `band` or `section`, not `row`**, so the vocabulary keeps meaning what it says.

  ⚠️ **Vertical grouping alone is not a reason to build it.** The canvas already moves a subtree with one drag, and the tree already nests arbitrarily; wrapping two blocks in a container purely to move them together buys nothing the block list does not already do, and costs a level of nesting in every traversal, diff and validation walk.

- **A document can be renamed, and the page-specific version stops being a special case.** `renamePage` was the only rename in the repository, so a pattern's name and key were fixed at creation: getting one wrong meant deleting it and starting again, **losing its revision history with it**. `renameDocument` takes the column names from `DOCUMENT_TABLES` — the title is `title` on a page and `name` on a pattern, the slug is `slug` and `key` — so it serves all six types. `renamePage` now delegates, and the page-specific repository function is deleted rather than left beside its replacement.

  **The error message names the type's own word.** A collision is `23505` on every document table, but being told "that **slug** is taken" about a field labelled **Key** is its own small confusion, so the service keeps a per-type noun. This is the same treatment the page version already gave the code, generalised.

  ⚠️ **`renamePageAction` has existed since Phase 4 with no caller at all.** A page could not be renamed from the admin, and nothing said so — found while generalising this. **It is still not wired, deliberately**: `app/(site)/page.tsx` reads the page with slug `home`, and there is no `/[slug]` route, so `pages` backs exactly one public URL. A rename dialog on the pages list would be a one-click way to make the homepage throw *"No published page with slug home"* — at build time on Railway and CI too, since the build prerenders it. Wiring it needs a guard on the system page first; the dialog was written, then reverted for exactly this reason.

  **A pattern's key is safe to move, and that asymmetry is the point.** Nothing links to it — it identifies the pattern to editors and to `listInsertablePatterns` — and insertion copies blocks rather than linking, so pages built from a pattern are untouched. A page's slug is an address, an article's is an address, and a product's is one **an import is explicitly forbidden from writing**. The repository writes what it is given; those policies live with the callers that know which case they are in.

  **Patterns had no E2E at all** — the suite reaches pages, articles, products, media, navigation, theme and integrations, and never this. Added with the rename, because the integration test beside it drives the repository with the **service-role client, which bypasses RLS**, so nothing there proves an editor's own session may perform the write. Four specs: create, rename both fields, the collision message, cleanup. ⚠️ The collision case needs a second pattern created by the test itself — **`scripts/seed.ts` writes no patterns**, so an assumed seeded key would have been a test that could only fail.

- **The binding filter editor, and the two defects its own tests found.** `BindingQuery.filter` has existed since Phase 2 and both sources have honoured it since 7c; what was missing was any way to *write* one, so an editor could retarget a category listing's sort but not its category. No migration and no service change — this is entirely a control.

  **It offers a typed vocabulary rather than a key/value box, and that is the whole design.** Both sources match with `===`, so `lead: "true"` and `issue: 40` match **nothing, silently** — an empty block, no error, nothing in a log, and no way for an editor to tell a mistyped filter from a category that genuinely has no stories. `lib/blocks/bindingFields.ts` declares each source's filterable facts *with the type its rows actually hold*, and the value control is built from that type: a Yes/No select for `lead`, a number box for `price`, text for the rest. The trap is unreachable rather than documented.

  ⚠️ **The conformance test found a real divergence between the two sources on its first run.** `FILTERABLE_FIELDS` is a hand-written description of row shapes that are built in code in two places, so the test walks the demo rows and checks every declared key and type against them. `author` failed: the Supabase source sets it on **every** article row, while the demo source sets it on each category's **lead** row only — its grid cards were transcribed without a byline. So `filter: { author: … }` would have returned different rows depending on which source map the site was handed, which is precisely the property `publicEditorial.test.ts` exists to compare. `author` is not offered, and the file records why. **The two source modules are documented as "identical field for field"; they are not, and now something checks.**

  ⚠️ **The component test found a second one, in the control itself.** Clearing a text filter to retype it made the row **vanish under the cursor** — the key left `filter` (correct: an empty filter must not be stored, since `{ category: "" }` matches nothing) and the row was rendered from `filter` alone, so the box disappeared mid-edit. The test could not type the new value because the input it had just cleared no longer existed. Cleared rows now fall back to a `pending` list and stay on screen; the ✕ is what removes one. **A controlled component needs a stateful test harness** — the first version of that test re-rendered a fixed prop and turned "type 040 into a box holding 039" into `"0390"`, which is a harness bug wearing a component bug's clothes.

  **Changing the source clears the filter, deliberately.** Filter keys belong to a source's row shape, so `category` carried over to `products` is a filter that can never match. Losing a filter on a deliberate source change is the smaller surprise, and the alternative — remembering a filter per source — is state this control does not need.

  **A stored key the vocabulary no longer knows is shown, not dropped.** It renders in the accent colour as "not a field of this source" with its own ✕. Silently discarding part of a saved query on open is how an editor loses work without being told.

- **KEEP READING gets its editor, and the interesting part is one word in an upsert.** `article_relations` has held 159 rows since Phase 7c and had no UI: the seeder wrote the demo's exact trios because module-insertion order is what the article baseline expects, and from that moment nobody could change one. **No migration** — `0004` built the table, `0019` scoped its reads, and everything this needed was already there.

  **An ordered list, not a set, and that distinction is the whole design.** The tags control three inches above it toggles chips, because a tag set has no order. These three have a sequence — left to right across the grid — which is exactly what `article_relations.position` is for. So: add, move up, move down, remove.

  ⚠️ **`setArticleTags` passes `ignoreDuplicates: true`; `setArticleRelations` must not, and the failure mode is silent.** A tag row carries nothing but its own existence, so a conflict really is a no-op. Here the conflicting row holds `position` — and **reordering an already-curated list writes the same pairs with different positions**, which is to say every reorder is *entirely* a conflict. With `ignoreDuplicates` the save succeeds, the toast says "Saved", the reload shows the old order, and nothing anywhere reports a problem. `tests/integration/articleRelations.test.ts` asserts precisely this case: same three articles, different order, read back.

  **The two constraints are handled in three places, on purpose.** `article_relation_not_self` (`23514`) and the `(article_id, related_id)` primary key (`23505`) are enforced by the table; `normalizeRelatedIds` in `lib/domain` drops the self-reference and collapses duplicates so a direct caller gets a stored list rather than a five-digit code in a toast; and the picker never *offers* the article itself or anything already chosen, so neither is reachable from the UI at all. The integration test asserts the database still refuses both — because a domain guard that silently covers for a constraint nobody checks is how the constraint quietly stops existing.

  **`KEEP_READING_COUNT` is shared with the public reader.** `publicEditorial.ts` had `.limit(3)` twice, the grid is three across, and the picker caps at three: a curated fourth would have been stored, never rendered, and impossible to explain to whoever chose it. One constant, three consumers.

  **A curated draft is hidden by RLS rather than by the query, and the picker says so.** The related-article select deliberately does not filter status — `0019` scoped `article_relations` so a row is readable only when *both* ends are published or the caller is staff. So an editor can curate an unpublished piece, see it marked "draft" in the admin, and watch it appear on the live page when it ships. Adding a status filter to the query would be redundant with the policy and would move the rule somewhere it is easier to forget. This is `0019` paying off in a feature written three slices later.

  **And the E2E asserts the property that surprises people: curating needs no publish.** `article_relations` is a live table, not part of `draft_data`, so the save alone changes the public page — the spec curates two articles on an already-published one, reorders them, reloads to prove persistence, then reads the titles back off `/article/<slug>` without touching Publish.

- **The public-site correctness trio — three defects this file had listed separately, fixed together because they are one shape.** Each was a place where the *served HTML* was wrong while the browser looked fine, which is why all three had survived a suite that drives a real browser.

  **The PDP's soft 404 was the client-component tax.** The whole page was `"use client"` — it holds the gallery index, the stepper and the add-to-bag state — so it could not call `notFound()` and rendered an in-page "we couldn't find that product" screen with a **200**. The fix is a server `page.tsx` in front of `ProductView.tsx` (the old page, moved, its signature changed and its miss branch removed). `layout.tsx` had already conceded the problem by marking that state `noindex`; the status code is now right, so the meta is belt and braces. ⚠️ **Two `<meta name="robots">` tags appear on that page now** — Next emits its own `noindex` for a `notFound()` render and the layout emits `noindex, follow`. Both say noindex, so this is untidy rather than wrong; it is recorded because it looks like a bug to the next person to `curl` it.

  **`ProductView` still calls `notFound()` itself, and not as belt and braces.** The server checks the database directly; the client resolves the slug against the catalogue the *site layout* fetched, and those two can disagree for up to an hour after a publish. That window used to render the soft-404 screen. `notFound()` works in a client component — it throws and the nearest boundary catches it — so the race now answers 404 rather than rendering blank.

  **`generateStaticParams` with `dynamicParams` left at its default.** Sixteen slugs prerender; an unlisted one still renders on demand. Setting it `false` would 404 every product published since the last build — a publish that silently does not work, which is precisely the failure mode this project keeps writing down.

  ⚠️ **`/shop`'s fallback was the whole bug, and the Suspense boundary was not the problem.** `useSearchParams` opts its subtree out of prerendering, so **whatever sits in the fallback is what the static HTML contains** — and the fallback was `<ShopHero />` alone. The store shipped a headline and a chip row to anything that does not run JavaScript; the products existed only in the flight payload. Measured before the change rather than assumed: `.next/server/app/shop.html` contained **zero** `href="/product/` occurrences. The fallback now renders the whole store unfiltered, which is what `/shop` with no query string means anyway and what its canonical already claimed. **The page stays `○` static** — reading `searchParams` on the server would render the right grid for every URL and turn the store into a per-request render, trading one defect for a worse one.

  **The new guard had to be proven able to fail, and the first attempt could not.** A test that fetches `/shop` with no browser and compares the product links against the hydrated page is the only kind that catches this — every other test waits for hydration, which paints the grid either way. Run against the *old* code it passed, which was wrong: the second `npm run start` had hit `EADDRINUSE` and a **stale server from the fixed build was still answering on 3000**. Killed properly, the same test failed with `Received: 0`. ⚠️ **A test verified against a server you did not restart has verified nothing** — and `reuseExistingServer: !CI` makes this easy to do by accident here.

  **The `product-missing` baseline was recaptured, not deleted.** `/product/the-driving-glove` used to screenshot the PDP's own miss screen; it now screenshots `app/not-found.tsx`, which carries the site chrome and had never had a baseline. Renamed to `not-found`, so the count stays at **16** and the coverage goes up. The evidence that nothing else moved is that the pre-change run failed **exactly two** shots — `/shop`, whose internals were restructured, matched its baseline untouched.

- **Category pages: the first slice built in the right order, and the migration that had been waiting for it.** Every recent feature has been an editor looking for a render path — synced patterns have none, templates have none. Categories were the mirror image: `/[category]` has rendered `categories.published_data` through `SectionRenderer` since Phase 7c, live, with `$bind` listings resolved against `articles` — and there was no way to edit it. So the work was the screen, and the screen was almost entirely reuse.

  **`0014` had already written the release condition.** Its header says `categories` stays off `document_table()` because they "carry the same columns but are not independently publishable, and adding it would imply a UI that does not exist". `0021` moves that line *because* the UI now exists — a deferral that named its own trigger, which is why this was one migration and not a debate.

  **Four permissions rather than reusing `taxonomy.write`, and the reason is a string.** `publish_document` asserts `has_permission(p_entity_type || '.publish')` — built from the type, so publishing a category needs a permission literally named `category.publish`. Nothing else can satisfy it short of special-casing a name inside a SECURITY INVOKER function every type depends on. The `categories: write` policy was **widened** to accept either permission rather than replaced, so `author` — which holds `taxonomy.write` — loses nothing.

  ⚠️ **`0001`'s role grants had to be replayed.** They read `select key from public.permissions … on conflict do nothing`, so a permission created later reaches no role until those statements run again. Insert four rows and stop, and `admin` would silently not hold them. That is a property of *any* future permission this project adds, not a quirk of this migration.

  **The test that had to be inverted said the right thing at the time.** `isDocumentType("category")` asserted `false` since Phase 1, citing 0010's allowlist — accurate until this slice. Inverted rather than deleted, the third characterisation test in this repo to complete that cycle after `is_system` (`0018`) and `draft_data` (`0020`).

  ⚠️ **`0021` shipped two defects, and both were caught by tests rather than by reading it. Worth recording individually, because each generalises.**

  **First: `create or replace` replaced the whole body, and the body was copied from the wrong migration.** `document_table()` was rewritten starting from `0014`'s version — which predates `0017` adding `when 'theme' then 'theme_settings'` — so applying `0021` silently *removed* theme from the allowlist and broke theme publishing everywhere it landed, the live project included. Nothing in the diff looked wrong; the migration reads as an addition. `publicTheme.test.ts` failed with "not a publishable entity type: theme" and named it exactly. **When replacing a function, copy the definition that is currently deployed, not the one in the migration you are imitating** — the header of `0021` now says so in place.

  **Second: the allowlist entry was a promise `publish_document` could not keep.** `categories` never had `created_by`/`updated_by`, unlike every other document table, and `publish_document` writes `updated_by = auth.uid()` in five separate statements → `42703`. Both columns added. **Everything static looked correct**: `document_table()` resolved, the four permissions existed, the RLS policy accepted the write. It took actually publishing a category to find it, which is the whole reason the positive integration test was written alongside the negative ones — *"the sixth document type is rejected"* would have passed against a completely broken implementation.

  ⚠️ **Two hard-coded counts rotted on the way through CI, in two different suites.** `publishing.test.ts` used `"category"` as its stand-in for "not a publishable type" — `0021` made it real, so the test asserted the opposite of the truth. And `auth.spec.ts` asserted `Permissions — 40 granted`; four new permissions made it 44, so an auth E2E went red on a change that touched no auth. The first was fixed by picking a type that is genuinely not publishable (`"tag"`); the second by **deriving the number** from `PERMISSIONS` in `lib/domain/permissions.ts`, since `0001` grants admin `select key from permissions` and the figure on screen therefore *is* the catalogue's size (verified against the live project: 44 rows, 44 granted, 44 in the union). The derived form is still a real assertion — it fails if a migration seeds a permission nobody added to the typed union — and it is no longer a number to remember to bump. **A literal that tracks seeded data is a scheduled failure in an unrelated suite.**

  **Verification honesty — Phase 7d.** Ran here, green: the four gates (**947 unit tests**), `npm run build` (both admin routes present, `/[category]` still `●` SSG with its 1h revalidate), and the live project probed directly to confirm `document_table()` resolves `page`, `theme` *and* `category` after the fix. **Not run here: the integration, visual and E2E suites** — CI is their first execution, and it is what found all four problems above across three runs. **Green on the fourth (run 137, `5edab40`), and read from the logs rather than the tick**, which is the habit this file keeps insisting on: integration **105 tests across 9 files** (up from 104 — the new category-publish test), `Migrations are idempotent` replaying all twenty-one, **E2E 59 passed in 2.4m**, visual 16 passed / 4 skipped. Both Playwright scripts run with `--pass-with-no-tests`, so the counts are the evidence, not the conclusion. Also unrun by anything: nobody has yet opened `/admin/categories/[id]` in a browser and dragged a block, so the builder is proven by reuse and by its actions' tests, not by use.

- **The rotation is done, and the last thing using the leaked key was the agent's own container.** Worth recording because it is the one failure mode nothing in the plan anticipated. The runbook's verification step was "check the dashboard's last-used indicator", and that indicator was showing the legacy anon key active *minutes ago* — correctly. What it could not show is that the caller was a **dev sandbox running `npm run build` against the live project**, not Railway. Following the plan literally, the honest conclusion would have been "something still depends on this key, do not deactivate", and the migration would have stalled on a signal that was true and useless.

  **The gateway logs are the tool that settles it**, because they carry `x_client_info` and a user agent: Railway's Node was 22.19.0 and the container's was 22.22.2, so two otherwise identical `supabase-js` callers were trivially separable. The query is in Known issues above. **A "last used" timestamp answers *whether*; you almost always need *by whom*.**

  ⚠️ **And the general point: an agent verifying a credential migration is itself a consumer of that credential.** Every `npm run build` in this session read the live project with whatever `.env.local` held. That is fine — it is how the builds proved anything — but it means the sandbox has to be migrated alongside production, or it keeps the old key looking alive. It was switched to the publishable key and rebuilt (73/73 static pages) before the toggle was flipped.

- **The key-format fix belongs to all four clients, and the runbook that said otherwise had an outage in it.** `adminFetchFor` shipped in `admin.ts` because the service-role key was the one being rotated. That was too narrow twice over.

  ⚠️ **Deactivating the legacy keys is one toggle for `anon` *and* `service_role`.** The dashboard tab is "Legacy anon, service\_role API keys"; the docs say "disable the `anon` and `service_role` keys". The runbook's step 4 read "deactivate the legacy key" as though it were per-key — following it would have killed `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY`, which feeds `public.ts`, `server.ts` and `client.ts`, and taken the whole site down rather than one cron route. **Retiring the leaked service-role key therefore requires migrating the anon key too**; they cannot be separated.

  **And the publishable key hits the same Bearer trap, with a wider blast radius.** A signed-*out* visitor has no session token, so the SDK falls back to sending the key as a Bearer on every public page read — the exact failure that produced the 500, but on the storefront instead of the jobs route. `@supabase/ssr` spreads `...options.global` into `createClient`, so `createServerClient` and `createBrowserClient` take the same `global.fetch` the plain client does.

  So the rule now lives in `lib/db/apiKey.ts` and all four clients use it. It still returns `undefined` for a legacy JWT, so it remains a no-op until each variable is actually swapped — which is what lets the code land well ahead of the ops step, in either order.

  **The lesson worth keeping is about the shape of the mistake, not the API.** The fix was scoped to the credential being changed rather than to the *rule* that changed, and the runbook inherited that scope. A migration step that says "deactivate the old thing" deserves a check of what else the old thing is holding up — here, one toggle covered two keys, and only one of them had been migrated.

  ⚠️ **And the same mistake had one more layer: there are FIVE clients, not four.** `middleware.ts` builds its own `createServerClient` — it has bespoke cookie plumbing, so it inherits nothing from `lib/db/server.ts` — and it runs on *every matched request*, calling `auth.getUser()`. It was missed twice: once when the rule shipped in `admin.ts`, and again when it was generalised to `lib/db/*`, because both passes enumerated the files under `lib/db/` rather than asking what constructs a client.

  **So the third pass asserted the property instead of the list.** `lib/db/apiKeyCoverage.test.ts` walks the source, finds every file importing a Supabase client constructor, and fails if one does not reference `fetchForKey`. Two details earn their keep: it matches the **import** rather than the call site, because `admin.ts` imports `createClient as createSupabaseClient` and no call-site regex finds that; and it asserts the scan finds **at least five** known files first, so a broken walk fails loudly instead of passing vacuously over zero results — the `--passWithNoTests` failure mode this repo has hit three times. The guard was verified by removing the wrapper from `middleware.ts` and watching it name that file.

  **A per-file unit test could not have caught any of this.** The gap was never a wrong assertion in a file someone thought about; it was a file nobody thought about. When the same omission happens twice, the fix is to stop maintaining the list.

- **A modern Supabase key is not a drop-in, and the SDK is why.** "It is read from the environment, so rotation needs no code change" was in Known issues for phases and was wrong in one specific way, found by doing it: a `sb_secret_…` key went into Railway and every scheduled-publish tick returned **HTTP 500**.

  **The mechanism.** The new keys are not JWTs, and the platform rejects them on `Authorization: Bearer` — it tries to parse one as a token and answers `Invalid JWT`. They belong on `apikey` alone. `supabase-js` sets `Authorization: Bearer <session token ?? the api key>`, and a service-role client never has a session token, so the fallback is always the key itself.

  ⚠️ **The SDK has the guard and does not apply it where we needed it.** `supabase-js` 2.111.0 defines `omitApiKeyAsBearer` and passes it **only** to the Edge Functions fetch — `this.fetch`, which every `.from()` query goes through, keeps the Bearer fallback whatever the key format. That asymmetry is exactly what made the wrong prediction reasonable: reading the SDK suggested REST was fine, and the docs' "Known limitations" section says plainly that it is not. **When the SDK and the docs disagree about a platform rule, the platform is the authority** — the SDK only reveals what its authors assumed.

  **The fix ships before the rotation, deliberately.** `adminFetchFor` returns a `global.fetch` that removes the Authorization header when it carries the key verbatim, and returns `undefined` for a legacy JWT key — a no-op with today's key, so it can merge on its own rather than being wedged into the ops window. It strips only the verbatim key, never a real user token, because removing that would silently escalate a request to the key's privileges.

  **Why it has a unit test rather than an integration one.** The failure appears only when someone edits Railway, and no local or CI run reproduces it: CI's service-role key comes from the throwaway local stack, which is a legacy JWT. The headers *are* the behaviour, so a fake `fetch` captures them and asserts the rule without a network.

- **`0020`, and why the draft-data leak was never a policy bug.** It sat in Known issues for phases described as "column privileges or views across six tables", which understated one thing and overstated another. Understated: **RLS could never have fixed it.** Row-level security decides which *rows* a role sees; `0012` granted SELECT at the **table** level, and a table-level grant carries every column — so a row RLS correctly handed over came with its unpublished draft attached. Overstated: it is seven tables, not six, and no views were needed.

  ⚠️ **The statement that looks like the fix and is not.** `revoke select (draft_data) on pages from anon` is valid SQL, runs without error, and changes nothing — it removes *column-level* grants, and the grant in force was table-level. The privilege has to be dropped at the table level and re-granted column by column. Anyone reaching for the one-liner will get a green migration and an unchanged leak.

  **The column lists are a maintenance cost, accepted deliberately because the failure mode is the right way round.** A column added later is invisible to `anon` until it is granted in `0020`, so forgetting produces a missing field on the public site — loud, and caught by the public read suites — rather than a silently re-exposed draft. That is the same fail-closed reasoning `0019` used.

  **The one code change was the one that would have failed silently.** `THEME_COLUMNS` listed `draft_data`, and `publicTheme.getPublishedThemeColors` used it *through the anonymous client*, inside a `try/catch` that falls back to the built-in palette. A refused column there does not throw a visible error — it turns into the whole site quietly reverting to default tokens with the editor's published theme still in the database. Hence `getPublishedThemeByKey` and a separate `PUBLIC_THEME_COLUMNS`, deliberately **not** a shared constant, so the public path cannot acquire a draft column by someone editing one list. Every other public read already selected explicit columns and needed nothing.

  ⚠️ **What `0020` means for synced patterns — still true, and it shaped the public read when they were built.** `expandPatternRefs` reads `patterns` with the caller's own session, and `/preview/[token]` is deliberately reachable by a signed-*out* viewer — whose role is `anon`. That viewer can no longer read `patterns.draft_data`, so a synced pattern's draft would not expand in an anonymous preview. Nothing breaks today (no pattern is synced; the create action hard-codes `detachable`). The fix when it matters is not to re-grant the column but to route the read the way `0010` already routes a page's draft to an anonymous previewer: through `resolve_preview`, a `security definer` function that checks the token inside the database.

- **The patterns slice, and the third time "the abstraction is the cheap half" has been true.** The Shopify adapter recorded it (the interface held; six of eleven changed files were UI) and this repeated it from the other direction. The builder accepted a pattern with **no changes at all** — `lib/db/repositories/documents.ts` aliases `name`→`title` and `key`→`slug`, so a pattern arrives in exactly a page's shape, and `/preview/[token]` was already type-agnostic through `BLOCK_TREE_KEY[type]`. Everything the slice actually cost was above that line: a list screen, a builder route, a history route, a store operation and a rail group.

  **The defect the abstraction hid.** `PublishBar` hard-coded `/admin/pages/${doc.id}/history`. Correct for as long as `page` was the only type with a builder route, and a 404 for the first type that was not — the shared component was type-agnostic everywhere *except* the one place it built a URL. Now `adminPathForDocument(doc.type, doc.id)` in `lib/domain/routes.ts`, declared `satisfies Record<DocumentType, string>` so a sixth document type cannot compile without a segment. **A component is not type-agnostic because it takes a type parameter; it is type-agnostic when nothing inside it names one type.**

  ⚠️ **Cloning a group is not cloning each of its members.** `store.insertMany` threads one taken-key set through the whole batch, because `cloneWithNewKeys` avoids only the keys it is handed — clone each node against `keysOf(tree)` alone and two blocks *within the same pattern* can draw the same key. The result is a tree that fails its own duplicate-key validation, produced by a single click, with nothing pointing at the cause. It has a test whose blocks are deliberately identical so that only the keys distinguish them.

  **Two deliberate omissions.** Pattern entries are click-only while section entries also drag, because a pattern drag needs a drag id, an overlay and an `active.type` branch in the drop handler — and click-to-insert is the rail's accessible path, so nothing became unreachable. And the pattern builder passes no `patterns` prop to itself, which is what stops a pattern nesting inside a pattern; that is a property of the wiring rather than a rule anyone has to remember.

  **Insertion prefers `published_data`, expansion prefers the draft**, and the asymmetry is the point: a preview asks "what would publishing this page produce", so it wants the draft; an insert asks "give me the approved version of this pattern", so it wants the published one. Written down because the two calls sit in the same file and look like an inconsistency.

- **`0019`, and the two questions a parent/child RLS fix actually turns on.** Five child tables shipped `using (true)` while their parents were scoped to published-or-staff, so the parent hid itself and handed over its children — a draft product's sku and `price_pence` being the sharp end, because `applyJob` creates imported products as drafts. The fix itself is `0015`'s `exists` join copied five times and took minutes. The two things worth keeping are the decisions around it.

  **Permissive or restrictive is not a style choice, and `0018` is the contrast.** `0018` needed RESTRICTIVE because it was narrowing a rule that several permissive policies could route around — the OR meant editing any one of them left the others open. Here each table has exactly one SELECT policy and it is being *replaced outright*, so a permissive policy says the thing directly. The general rule the two migrations now demonstrate between them: **restrictive when you are constraining every route to an operation at once, permissive when you are replacing the only route there is.**

  **One parent or both.** `article_relations` references two articles and `product_collection_items` a collection and a product. Checking only the owning side passes every obvious test and still discloses the id and the existence of the unpublished row on the far end. Both ends are required, which means a published article related to a draft one shows nothing publicly until the draft ships — the behaviour an editor would expect, and a constraint on consumers that do not exist yet rather than a change to any rendered page.

  ⚠️ **"Deny everything" passes every negative assertion in this slice.** Five of the six new tests assert that a child of a draft parent is invisible; a policy of `using (false)` would satisfy all five and empty the PDP gallery in production. That is why each test asserts the positive half in the same body, and why the live probe published the probe product and re-read it rather than stopping at the refusal. **A security fix verified only by what it blocks is half-verified.**

  **Verified on the live project because the suite could not run here** — same Docker constraint as the RLS suite itself. The probe was: create a draft product with a variant, read as `anon` (`0` where it would have been `1`), publish it, read again (`1`), delete the probe, then count the seeded rows as `anon` to prove nothing legitimate was lost — 48 `product_media`, 30 `article_tags`, 159 `article_relations`, all unmoved. Idempotency was deliberately **not** tested against production: replaying the DDL drops and recreates a live policy, and the gap between the two statements denies reads. `service_role` bypasses RLS so seeds and the jobs route are untouched either way, and `is_staff()` is "holds any role at all", so every admin screen still sees drafts and their children.

- **`SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` was never set in Railway, and eight phases of deploys never noticed** (set 2026-08-09; the lesson is what remains). Found by switching scheduled publishing on: the jobs route is the **first runtime code to call `createAdminClient()`**. Everything before it either used the editor's own session (`lib/db/server.ts`) or the anonymous public client, and the build needs only the two `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` values — so a missing service-role key was invisible. `PROGRESS.md` has said since Phase 1 to "mirror the first three in Railway"; nothing enforced it and nothing failed without it.

  **The general shape, and this session hit it three times: a variable nothing reads is a variable nobody notices is missing.** `JOBS_SECRET` absent from `.env.example` for four phases; `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` absent from Railway for eight; `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` absent from both `.env.example` and `ci.yml` until the SEO phase made something read it. Each was invisible until the first code path that needed it arrived, and each then failed somewhere far from its cause. **Worth a deployment checklist that asserts every variable the app can need, rather than the ones today's code paths happen to touch** — that is the change that would have caught all three at once.

  ✅ **Now written, and deliberately a script rather than a document** — `scripts/check-env.mjs`, run by CI's fast job as `Environment variables are declared`. A checklist in prose has precisely the weakness it exists to fix: nothing reads it. This scans `app/`, `lib/`, `components/`, `scripts/`, `tests/` and `middleware.ts` for `process.env.<NAME>`, compares against `.env.example`, and **fails the build** when something is read but undeclared. It needs no secrets, no stack and no network, which is why it sits beside ESLint rather than with the tests.

  **It found a fourth instance on its first run**, which is the argument for it: `SEED_ADMIN_EMAIL` and `SEED_ADMIN_PASSWORD` — read by `scripts/create-admin.ts`, the script this very file tells a new session to run — were undeclared, along with `E2E_ADMIN_EMAIL`/`_PASSWORD` and the three `SUPABASE_TEST_*`. Seven in total, all now declared. The seed pair is the worst of them: **both have defaults**, so unset they do not fail, they quietly provision an account nobody meant to create.

  Two deliberate non-failures. *Declared but never read* is reported and passes — Stripe's three variables are declared ahead of a payments phase that is out of scope by decision, and deleting a forward-looking declaration to satisfy a linter is the wrong trade. And a short, commented ignore list covers `NODE_ENV`, `CI`, `PORT`, `E2E_BASE_URL`; keep it short, because a long one hollows the check out until it passes by construction.

  **What it does not cover, so nobody mistakes it for completeness:** it is a regex over source text, not a resolved reference graph, so a dynamic `process.env[name]` is invisible to it. ~~Nothing does that today.~~ ⚠️ **Something does since the XML ingestion phase** — `resolveCredential` in `lib/integrations/commerce/index.ts`, which resolves a feed's `credentials_ref` by indexing `process.env` with an operator-supplied string. It is still the only one. It cannot be declared (the name belongs to a `product_sources` row, not to the codebase), so what guards it instead is a naming rule: the reference must match `^FEED_[A-Z0-9_]+$`, which keeps an operator from aiming a source at the service-role key. See the ingestion entry in the decisions log. It also says nothing about whether a *deployment* has the values — only that the catalogue is honest.
- **✅ Scheduled publishing is switched on and running in production** (closed 2026-08-09). `0016` applied and verified (`security_definer` true, executable by `service_role` alone); `JOBS_SECRET` in Railway *and* as a GitHub Actions secret; `SITE_URL` as a repository variable; `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` and — the last piece — **`SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` in Railway**. Confirmed by two green workflow runs, one scheduled (10:07) and one manual (10:44), after the 08:25 failure. Nothing about this feature is now unproven or unconfigured.

  **What made it diagnosable was the status codes, and they earned their keep.** 503 = a variable is missing (and since `f0e971d`, naming which), 401 = the two `JOBS_SECRET` values disagree, 500 = the run itself failed, 200 = it ran. Isolating a missing `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` took three HTTP probes and no log-reading, because those four answers are distinct — an unauthenticated 401 proved the secret was set and matching, while the workflow's own authenticated 500 proved it got past auth and then threw. **Worth preserving: an endpoint whose failure modes map to distinct status codes can be diagnosed from outside, by someone with no access to its logs.**

  **⚠️ And `*/5` is nothing like every five minutes in practice.** Between 06:21 and 08:50 the schedule fired **once** — GitHub throttles `schedule` triggers, aggressively on a repository with little activity, and the workflow's own note that "Actions treats cron as best-effort under load" understates it by an order of magnitude. Consequences worth knowing before anyone relies on this: a document scheduled for 09:00 may go live materially later than 09:05, and **the red runs from the missing variable accumulate far more slowly than "288 a day"** — which cuts both ways, because it also means a genuinely broken job stays quiet longer than expected. If publish timing ever needs to be tight, this is the component to replace, not the SQL.

  Confirmed still open at 08:25 on 2026-08-09, two ways: an unauthenticated POST to the live route answers **401**, so `JOBS_SECRET` is set and matching; and the scheduled workflow's own run — which sends the *real* secret — logs **`HTTP 500 {"error":"Run failed"}`**, so it authenticates and then throws. That is the service-role key and nothing else. **Every five-minute firing is a red run in the Actions history until the variable is set** — the workflow is deliberately loud about a non-200 rather than silent, because a 401 caused by drifting secrets would otherwise be indistinguishable from "nothing was due".
- [x] ~~`media_assets_search_idx` (0002) is a GIN index over an *expression* and is unreachable from PostgREST~~ — **fixed in `0026`**, and the prescription in this bullet was right as far as it went. It said "a stored generated `tsvector` column and a types regeneration", which is exactly what landed — and it missed the half that mattered: **swapping `ilike` for full-text is a downgrade on its own.** A filename is one token, so `hero:*` finds `hero-cover.jpg` and `cover:*` finds nothing. Full-text runs first and the substring query runs only when it matches zero. See the decisions log.
- [~] **Partly closed.** `width`/`height` **are** populated now — `lib/domain/imageDimensions.ts` parses the PNG/JPEG/GIF/WebP header on upload, so `AssetDetails`' Dimensions row finally renders. `duration_ms` and `placeholder` are still never written, and the original text is right about them: ~`media_assets.width` / `height` /~ `duration_ms` / `placeholder` are never populated — the service catalogues what the browser tells it and does not decode the file. The columns exist so the builder can reserve space without a fetch; nothing reads them yet.
- [x] ~~The service-role key passed through a chat transcript~~ — **rotated and the legacy keys are deactivated. The leaked credential is dead.** The project now runs on `sb_publishable_…` (all five clients) and `sb_secret_…` (the jobs route), with the legacy `anon`/`service_role` pair disabled in the dashboard. Verified from the gateway logs rather than the dashboard indicator — see the recipe below — with production at 945 × HTTP 200 on the publishable key and **zero 401/403** after the toggle. The runbook that follows is kept because the *next* rotation is now a routine operation rather than a project, and because two of its steps were wrong the first time.

  **⚠️ The verification that actually works, and why the dashboard indicator misleads.** Supabase's "last used" indicator tells you a legacy key was used recently but not *by what* — and on this project the last consumer was **the agent's own sandbox**, whose `.env.local` still held the legacy anon key and hit the live project on every `npm run build`. Read that indicator alone and you would conclude production still depended on the key and stall indefinitely. The gateway logs disambiguate it, because `x_client_info` and the Node runtime version identify the caller:

  ```sql
  -- Supabase MCP: query_logs. Railway runs one Node version, a dev container another.
  select
    log_attributes['request.sb.apikey.apikey.prefix'] as key_prefix,
    log_attributes['request.headers.x_client_info']   as client_info,
    log_attributes['request.headers.user_agent']      as ua,
    count(*) as n, max(timestamp) as last_seen
  from logs
  where source = 'edge_logs' and timestamp > now() - interval 4 hour
  group by key_prefix, client_info, ua order by last_seen desc
  ```

  A legacy key shows an **empty** `key_prefix`; the new ones show `sb_publishable_…` / `sb_secret_…`. `sb_temp_…` is the dashboard's own short-lived key and can be ignored. **Before deactivating anything, check every non-empty caller you cannot account for.**

- [ ] ~~The service-role key passed through a chat transcript~~ — **done, see above. The runbook is kept for the next rotation.** ~~It is read from the environment, so rotation needs no code change.~~ That was half right; see the decisions log. **This is also what blocks running the signed-in E2E locally**: `scripts/create-admin.ts` needs that key, so a fresh container cannot provision or reset an admin account.

  **Do not rotate the legacy key.** `anon` and `service_role` are JWTs signed by the project's JWT secret, so rotating one means rotating that secret — which invalidates the **anon** key too (inlined into the bundle at build time, so it needs a rebuild *and* redeploy) and signs out every existing admin session. Supabase's own migration guide calls the legacy keys "hard to rotate without downtime". The project already has the modern key system enabled.

  **The runbook**, in this order, because only the last step is hard to undo:
  1. Dashboard → Settings → API Keys → the `default` **secret** key (`sb_secret_…`). **Create it only if it is not already there** — a secret key stays revealable in the dashboard, so an existing one is copied rather than replaced. ⚠️ Never delete a key Railway still holds: when rotating one later, create the replacement *first*, swap, then delete.
  2. Railway → Variables → replace `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`. Server-only and read at call time, so a restart suffices — **no rebuild**, unlike the anon key.
  3. Verify with the `Scheduled publish` workflow's `workflow_dispatch` — the jobs route is the only production consumer of this key. Read the log, not the tick: the workflow `exit 0`s with a notice when `SITE_URL`/`JOBS_SECRET` are unset, which is green and proves nothing. `HTTP 200` is the pass; `503` means the variable never arrived; **`500` means the key was rejected**.
  4. ⚠️ **Deactivation is one toggle for the PAIR, not per key** — the dashboard tab is "Legacy anon, service_role API keys" and every doc phrasing is plural. **This step originally read "deactivate the legacy key" and would have taken the public site down**, because `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` still holds the legacy anon JWT and feeds `public.ts`, `server.ts` and `client.ts`. So before touching the toggle, swap anon → publishable as well:
     - Railway → `NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` → the `sb_publishable_…` value. ⚠️ **This one is `NEXT_PUBLIC_`, so it is inlined at build time — it needs a rebuild and redeploy, not just a restart**, unlike the service-role swap.
     - Verify the public site renders and an admin sign-in works. Use the dashboard's **"last used" indicators** to confirm neither legacy key is still being hit before flipping the toggle.
     - Then deactivate the pair. Reversible — Supabase lets you re-activate them.
     The Bearer fix covers **all five** Supabase clients (`lib/db/apiKey.ts`), so the publishable swap needs no further code change. ⚠️ **Five, not four** — `middleware.ts` builds its own `createServerClient` with its own cookie plumbing, so it inherits nothing from `lib/db/server.ts`, and it was missed on the first pass. `lib/db/apiKeyCoverage.test.ts` now scans the source and fails if any file importing a Supabase client constructor does not reference `fetchForKey`, which is what would catch a sixth.
  5. Update any local `.env.local`, or the seed, admin and E2E scripts break.

  **The old value is never lost**: it stays readable in the dashboard until step 4, so there is nothing to save beforehand.

  **Blast radius, checked rather than assumed.** Railway (the jobs route, via `scheduledPublishing.ts` → `createAdminClient`) and a local `.env.local`. **CI does not use it** — `ci.yml` takes `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` from `supabase status -o env`, the throwaway local stack, and says so in a comment. `scheduled-publish.yml` holds only `JOBS_SECRET` and `SITE_URL`; it POSTs over HTTP and never sees a database key.
- [x] ~~**`mg.accentSerif`'s eight alpha-modified utilities compile to no CSS at all**~~ — **fixed two sessions ago** (the channel twin), and the *literals* that bypassed the token entirely are fixed now too: 14 hard-coded `text-[#ff4d5e]` call sites across 13 components became `text-mg-accentSerif`. Kept for the mechanism. ~~**`mg.accentSerif`'s eight alpha-modified utilities compile to no CSS at all**~~, and have since Track A. `bg-mg-accentSerif/10`, `border-mg-accentSerif/40` and their siblings are dropped by Tailwind because the token's value is a bare `var()` and `parseColor` cannot parse one. Found while making the accent editable; deliberately not fixed there, because unlike the accent's channel-form flip this one **changes pixels** (`/sign-in` and several admin danger states are untinted today and would gain their tint). Needs its own slice and a conscious decision about the affected baselines. The fix is the same shape: a `--mg-accent-serif-rgb` channel twin.
- [x] ~~**Published rows are readable in full by anon, `draft_data` column included.**~~ — **closed for `anon` by `0020`, applied to the live project.** RLS is row-level, so this was never fixable with a policy: `0012` granted SELECT at the **table** level, and a table-level grant covers every column, including ones added later. A column-level `revoke select (draft_data)` is accepted by Postgres and does nothing here, because it only removes column-level grants. `0020` therefore revokes table SELECT from `anon` on all seven tables carrying the column and re-grants the columns individually, minus the draft. The `KNOWN GAP` characterisation test was inverted in the same commit — the second such test to complete that cycle after `0018`'s. ⚠️ **The lists must track the schema, and that is deliberate**: a new column is invisible to `anon` until granted here, so forgetting shows up as a missing field on the public site rather than a silent re-exposure of the draft.
- [ ] ⚠️ **The other half is still open, and column privileges cannot close it: a signed-in account holding no roles can still read `draft_data` on a published row.** `authenticated` keeps the column because the admin reads drafts through the editor's own session, which *is* that role — revoking there would break the builder outright. Grants cannot tell staff from non-staff; that distinction is `is_staff()`, and it exists only at the row level. Closing this means moving staff draft reads behind a `security definer` function (the shape `0010`'s `resolve_preview` already uses to hand an anonymous previewer a draft) or splitting drafts into their own table. Bigger than `0020` and a product decision as much as a technical one, since it changes how every admin screen reads. **The internet-facing half — no account at all — is closed.**
  - ✅ **Confirmed against the live deployment**, not merely inferred: `GET /rest/v1/pages?select=slug,status,draft_data` with the publishable key returns the home page's full `draft_data`. `tests/integration/rls.test.ts` now asserts this as a **characterisation test** — it encodes today's behaviour, so closing the gap turns that file red and forces the assertion to be inverted on purpose.
  - **Severity today is lower than it reads, and that will not last.** No published row currently carries a draft that differs from what is live (checked with `draft_data is distinct from published_data` across `pages`, `articles`, `products`, `categories` — zero rows), so the column leaks nothing unpublished *yet*. It starts leaking the moment an editor opens a live page and autosave fires, which is the normal way this CMS is used.
- [x] ~~**`is_system` does not protect a page from anyone holding `page.write`.**~~ — **fixed by `0018`, applied to the live project and proven re-runnable by applying it twice.** One RESTRICTIVE policy, `pages: system pages are undeletable`, `for delete using (not is_system)`. Restrictive policies are AND-ed with the whole permissive set, so it constrains **every** route to a DELETE at once, including any policy added later; folding `not is_system` into `pages: write` instead would have fixed today's two policies and silently reopened the hole the next time somebody added a third, which is exactly how this one arrived. `service_role` has BYPASSRLS (verified), so seeds, the scheduled job and the suite's own fixture teardown are untouched — a system page can still be removed deliberately with the service-role client, which is the right home for an operation that should never be one click away in a CMS. `lib/services/documents.ts:192` has said "`is_system` pages are additionally protected by their RLS delete policy" since Phase 4; **that comment is now true and was not before.** The characterisation test in `rls.test.ts` was inverted in the same commit, and gained a companion asserting an ordinary page is still deletable — the proof that the guard is narrow. The description of the original defect follows, because the mechanism is the reusable part:
- [ ] ~~**`is_system` does not protect a page from anyone holding `page.write`.**~~ (**fixed — see above.** Kept for the mechanism.) `pages` carries two PERMISSIVE policies covering DELETE — `pages: write` (`cmd=ALL`, `using has_permission('page.write')`) and `pages: delete` (`using has_permission('page.delete') and not is_system`) — and **permissive policies are OR-ed**, so the effective rule is `page.write OR (page.delete AND NOT is_system)`. The `not is_system` guard therefore binds only an actor who lacks `page.write`, and no page-deleting actor lacks it. Found by reading the policy catalogue rather than by a failing test — and then **proven**: `rls.test.ts` asserts it as a characterisation test, and on PR #37's first run a `page.write`-only actor deleted a throwaway page marked `is_system`. **The fix is one restrictive policy** — `create policy "pages: system pages are undeletable" on pages as restrictive for delete using (not is_system)` — because a RESTRICTIVE policy ANDs with the permissive set instead of joining the OR. Needs a migration, so it wants its own slice; `service_role` bypasses RLS entirely and so scripts and seeds are unaffected either way.
- [x] ~~**The `*.delete` permissions are additive, not restrictive — they gate nothing.**~~ — **closed by `0022` (six tables) and `0023` (`categories`, the seventh).** Kept for the mechanism, which is the reusable half: a `for all` write policy covers DELETE and permissive policies are OR-ed, so `x.write OR x.delete` can only ever grant. ⚠️ It named three tables; there were **six**, plus `categories`, which turned out to have no delete policy at all. Original text follows.
  **The `*.delete` permissions are additive, not restrictive — they gate nothing.** Same mechanism as the bullet above, and it generalises: `articles`, `products` and `media_assets` each pair an `ALL` policy on `x.write` with a `DELETE` policy on `x.delete`, so **`x.write` alone already permits deletion** and `x.delete` grants nothing its holder does not have. A role designed as "can edit but not delete" does not exist at the database level today, whatever the admin UI offers. Whether to make these real gates is a product decision, not a bug fix: doing it means restrictive policies, and it would **remove** delete access that write-holding roles currently have.
- [x] ~~**Child tables are readable regardless of whether their parent is published.**~~ — **fixed by `0019`, applied to the live project.** Five `using (true)` SELECT policies replaced with an `exists` join onto the parent, repeating the parent's own `status = 'published' or is_staff()` rather than leaning on the parent's policy to filter the subquery — self-contained, so it survives someone widening the parent's read scope later. It is `0015`'s shape (`menu_items`) applied to the five tables `0015` did not cover, and PERMISSIVE rather than RESTRICTIVE like `0018`, because each of these tables has exactly one SELECT policy being replaced outright instead of a rule several policies could route around. ⚠️ **`article_relations` and `product_collection_items` require BOTH ends readable**, which is a decision and not an implementation detail: checking only the owning side would still disclose the existence and the id of an unpublished row on the far end. **Proven on the live project, both halves**: a draft product's variant was readable by `anon` before and is not after, the same variant became readable the moment the product was published — the check that stops the fix from being "deny everything" — and the seeded rows are unmoved (48 `product_media`, 30 `article_tags`, 159 `article_relations` all still reaching `anon`). No rendered page changes: the public store and editorial reads only ever join children to published parents. Six assertions added to `rls.test.ts`, unrun here for the usual reason and left to CI. The description of the original defect follows, because the mechanism is the reusable part:
- [ ] ~~**Child tables are readable regardless of whether their parent is published.**~~ (**fixed — see above.** Kept for the mechanism.) `product_variants`, `product_media`, `product_collection_items`, `article_tags` and `article_relations` all carry `using (true)`, while their parents are scoped to `status = 'published' or is_staff()`. So a **draft** product's variants — sku, price, options — and its gallery are world-readable while the product itself is correctly hidden. Nothing leaks today (`product_variants` and `product_collections` are empty, and every seeded product is published), which is exactly why it is worth writing down before something does. ⚠️ **Ingestion points straight at this**: `applyJob` creates imported products as **drafts**, so an unreleased catalogue staged from a feed would be hidden at `products` and exposed one join away. `menu_items` shows the shape the fix should take — its policy joins `menus` and checks the parent's status.
- [x] ~~⚠️ **No public route expands a pattern `_ref`, so synced patterns cannot ship until one does.**~~ — **done**; the note below is kept because it named the hazard exactly and the fix is best read against it. `expandPatternRefs` has exactly one caller — `app/(site)/preview/[token]/page.tsx` — and the public render paths do not call it. A synced pattern would therefore render correctly **in preview** and silently render nothing **on the live site**: the worst failure shape available, because the person checking their work is the one who sees it working. Dormant today only because `createPatternAction` hard-codes `sync_mode: 'detachable'` and never accepts the client's value, which is the enforcement rather than a convention. **The fix is not a two-line wire-up**, and that is the part worth knowing before starting it: `expandPatternRefs` reads through `lib/db/server.ts`, which awaits `cookies()`, and any public route that awaits cookies is opted out of static rendering by Next — no error, no failing test, no visual diff, just a site that quietly stops being static. It needs a cookie-free lookup through `lib/db/public.ts` first. That is why synced patterns were left out of the pattern slice rather than finished in it.
